Conformally invariant fields out of Brownian loop soups
Antoine Jego, Titus Lupu, Wei Qian

Abstract
Consider a Brownian loop soup ℒ D θ \mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta} with subcritical intensity θ ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 2 ] \theta\in(0,1/2] in some 2D bounded simply connected domain D D . We define and study the properties of a conformally invariant field h θ h_{\theta} naturally associated to ℒ D θ \mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta} . Informally, this field is a signed version of the local time of ℒ D θ \mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta} to the power 1 − θ 1-\theta . When θ = 1 / 2 \theta=1/2 , h θ h_{\theta} is a Gaussian free field (GFF) in D D . Our construction of h θ h_{\theta} relies on the multiplicative chaos ℳ γ \mathcal{M}_{\gamma} associated with ℒ D θ \mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta} , as introduced in [ ABJL23 ] . Assigning independent symmetric signs to each cluster, we restrict ℳ γ \mathcal{M}_{\gamma} to positive clusters. We prove that, when θ = 1 / 2 \theta=1/2 , the resulting measure ℳ γ + \mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+} corresponds to the exponential of γ \gamma times a GFF. At this intensity, the GFF can be recovered by differentiating at γ = 0 \gamma=0 the measure ℳ γ + \mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+} . When θ < 1 / 2 \theta<1/2 , we show that ℳ γ + \mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+} has a nondegenerate fractional derivative at γ = 0 \gamma=0 defining a random generalised function h θ h_{\theta} . We establish a result which is analogous to the recent work [ ALS23 ] in the GFF case ( θ = 1 / 2 \theta=1/2 ), but for h θ h_{\theta} with θ ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 2 ] \theta\in(0,1/2] . Relying on the companion article [ JLQ23 ] , we prove that each cluster of ℒ D θ \mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta} possesses a nondegenerate Minkowski content in some non-explicit gauge function r ↦ r 2 ​ | log ⁡ r | 1 − θ + o ⁡ ( 1 ) r\mapsto r^{2}|\log r|^{1-\theta+o(1)} . We then prove that h θ h_{\theta} agrees a.s. with the sum of the Minkowski content of each cluster multiplied by its sign. We further extend the couplings between CLE 4 , SLE 4 and the GFF [ SS13 , MS ] to h θ h_{\theta} for θ ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 2 ] \theta\in(0,1/2] . We show that the (non-nested) CLE κ loops form level lines for h θ h_{\theta} and that there exists a constant height gap c ⁡ ( θ ) > 0 c(\theta)>0 between the values of the field on either side of the CLE loops. However, unless θ = 1 / 2 \theta=1/2 , h θ h_{\theta} is not equal to the CLE nesting field. We finally study the Wick powers of h θ h_{\theta} and the expansion of ℳ γ \mathcal{M}_{\gamma} and ℳ γ + \mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+} in power series of γ \gamma .



1
Introduction

The two-dimensional Brownian loop soup, introduced by Lawler and Werner
[
LW04
]
, is a fundamental object with numerous connections to other important conformally invariant/covariant models.
Brownian loops in the form of bubbles first appeared in
[
LSW03a
]
, where Lawler, Schramm and Werner constructed chordal conformal restriction measures by attaching Brownian loops to an SLE path.
A Brownian loop soup is a random collection of infinitely many Brownian-type loops in a given domain
D
of the plane, sampled according to a Poisson point process with intensity
\theta\mu^{\rm loop}_{D}
.
\theta
is a positive real parameter and
\mu^{\rm loop}_{D}
is a measure on loops defined by (
2.1
). The parameter
c=2\theta
corresponds to the central charge in conformal field theory.
In their seminal work
[
SW12
]
, Sheffield and Werner
showed the following phase transition:

•

Supercritical:
when
\theta>1/2
, there is a.s. only one cluster of loops;

•

Critical and subcritical:
when
\theta\leq 1/2
, there are a.s. infinitely many clusters. In this case, the outermost boundaries of the outermost clusters are distributed according to a (non-nested) conformal loop ensemble CLE
κ
where
\kappa=\kappa(\theta)\in(8/3,4]
is related to
\theta
by

c=2\theta=(6-\kappa)(3\kappa-8)/(2\kappa).

(1.1)

SLE (by Schramm
[
Sch00
]
) and its loop variant CLE (by Sheffield
[
She09
]
) were introduced to describe the scaling limits of discrete models from statistical mechanics, and have now become fundamental objects in random conformal geometry.

The intensity
\theta=1/2
is special for another (related) reason. Indeed, when
\theta=1/2
,
Le Jan
[
LJ10
,
LJ11
]
identified the occupation field of the loop soup with half of the square of the Gaussian free field (GFF), on discrete graphs and in dimensions
1,2,3
in the continuum. (In the 2D and 3D continuum, both the GFF and the occupation field are not defined pointwise and additive renormalisations are needed to define rigourously the square of the GFF and the occupation field of the loop soup.)
This result is part of the random walk/Brownian motion
representations of the GFF,
also known as
isomorphism theorems

[
Sym65
,
Sym66
,
Sym69
,
BFS82
,
Dyn84a
,
Dyn84b
,
Wol78
,
EKM+00
,
MR06
,
Szn12
]
.

The
critical
intensity
\theta=1/2
corresponds to
\kappa=4
, which is also the critical parameter for SLE in terms of self intersection
[
RS05
]
.
A remarkable relation between SLE
4
and the GFF was established by Schramm and Sheffield in
[
SS13
]
, where SLE
4
appeared as
level lines
of the GFF. There, SLE
4
was recovered as a
local set
of the GFF, so that the GFF satisfies a certain spatial Markov property when we condition on such sets. The value of the GFF is constant on either side of the level line, with a constant height gap
2\lambda>0
between the two sides. Such a coupling was also extended to CLE
4
by Miller and Sheffield
[
MS
]
(see also
[
MSW17
,
ASW19
]
), so that conditionally on the CLE
4
, the GFF restricted to the domain encircled by each CLE
4
loop is an independent GFF with boundary conditions

\pm 2\lambda
.

Figure 1.1:
Relation (1)
[
SW12
]
states that the outer boundaries of the outermost clusters in a loop soup are CLE. Relation (2)
[
LJ10
,
LJ11
]
states that the renormalised
h^{2}/2
is equal to the occupation time field of the loop soup. Relation (3)
[
MS
]
states that CLE
4
form the level loops of the GFF.
In this paper, we construct a field
h^{\theta}
out of a loop soup with intensity
\theta\in(0,1/2)
, and conjecture that the occupation time field of the loop soup is equal to a constant times the renormalised
|h_{\theta}|^{1/(1-\theta)}
. We prove that SLE
κ
/CLE
κ
appear as level lines of
h^{\theta}
.

The three relations between the critical loop soup, CLE
4
, and GFF in Figure
1.1
are proved using three different approaches, but are shown to commute in
[
QW19
]
, using the metric graph approximation by
[
Lup16
,
Lup18a
]
as a key input. The metric graph is locally one dimensional, so that the local times of a loop soup are defined pointwise. It was shown in
[
Lup16
]
that the GFF on the metric graph can be obtained by taking the square root of the occupation field of the critical loop soup, and then assigning i.i.d. symmetric signs in
\{\pm 1\}
to each cluster. In the 2D continuum, the local times are not defined pointwise,
so it is not immediately clear what the square root of the local time means.
Yet, the article
[
ALS23
]
gives the precise analogue of this procedure in the 2D continuum, and shows that the GFF is equal to the sum of the signed Minkowski contents of the clusters.

Relying on the connection with the GFF, one is able to deduce many properties about the loop soup at
\theta=1/2
. For example, the excursions induced by the Brownian loops that touch the boundary of a cluster were shown to
have a surprisingly simple distribution, forming a Poisson point process

[
QW19
,
QW18
]
.
In
[
ALS20a
]
, the Minkowski gauge function of the clusters was shown to be
r\mapsto|\log r|^{1/2}r^{2}
. For
\theta\in(0,1/2)
, without the powerful relation to the GFF, much less is known,
and the picture is expected to be overall more complicated.
This paper, together with the companion article
[
JLQ23
]
, aims to fill this gap.
The main question addressed in the current paper is:

What is the natural field associated to the Brownian loop soup when
\theta\in(0,1/2)
?

More precisely, we would like to have a field
h_{\theta}
that is related to the occupation field of a loop soup in a similar way that the GFF is related to the critical loop soup.
It was shown in
[
LJ11
,
LJMR15
]
that the occupation field of the loop soup is a permanental field, an object introduced by Vere-Jones
[
VJ97
]
long before the Brownian loop soup (also see
[
MR13
]
).
The field
h_{\theta}
that we are after would be a more fundamental object behind these permanental fields.

A natural idea is to proceed as in the critical case
[
Lup16
]
, i.e., to take the square root of the occupation field of a loop soup, and then assign i.i.d. signs to each cluster.
Although one can always do this on the metric graph, the resulting field is not guaranteed to have any nice property nor to converge to a nondegenerate limit in the continuum. As it turns out, surprisingly, the relevant field will not correspond to a signed version of the square root of the local time of the loop soup, as for
\theta=1/2
, but instead to a signed version of the local time to the power
1-\theta
.

Our construction of the field
h_{\theta}
is done directly in the continuum, relying on two key inputs. The first one is the computation of the crossing exponent and related estimates obtained in the companion paper
[
JLQ23
]
. The second key input is the construction in
[
ABJL23
]
of a multiplicative chaos associated to the Brownian loop soup with any intensity
\theta>0
.
Gaussian multiplicative chaos (GMC), initially introduced by Kahane
[
Kah85
]
in the eighties and extensively studied in the past decade
[
RV10
,
DS11
,
RV11
,
Sha16
,
Ber17
]
, is a theory that defines and studies properties of random measures informally defined as the exponential of a real parameter
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
times a Gaussian logarithmically-correlated field. The two-dimensional GFF is an archetypal example of such a field and its GMC measure is sometimes called Liouville measure.
GMC appears to be a universal feature of log-correlated fields going beyond the Gaussian setting:
[
Jun20
]
studies fields defined as random Fourier series with i.i.d. coefficients and
[
Jeg20
,
Jeg21
]
makes sense of the exponential of the square root of the local time of planar Brownian motion.
The article
[
ABJL23
]
provides a third example of a non-Gaussian multiplicative chaos and defines what can be thought of as the exponential of
\gamma
times the square root of twice the local time of the Brownian loop soup, for any intensity
\theta
. In view of Le Jan’s isomorphism, it is natural to expect, and has been proven in
[
ABJL23
]
, that when
\theta=1/2
the resulting measure agrees with an unsigned version of Liouville measure:
e^{\gamma|\text{GFF}|}\mathrm{d}x=e^{\gamma\text{GFF}}\mathrm{d}x+e^{-\gamma\text{GFF}}\mathrm{d}x
, i.e. a sum of two
correlated
Liouville measures.

Construction of
h_{\theta}

((2*) in Figure
1.1
)
Concretely, we assign i.i.d. symmetric signs to each cluster in the loop soup, and then consider the multiplicative chaos
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
of the loop soup restricted to positive clusters. We prove that, when
\theta=1/2
, the resulting measure
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}
agrees with
e^{\gamma\text{GFF}}\mathrm{d}x
. The GFF can therefore be recovered from the loop soup, together with the independent spins, by differentiating
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}
at

\gamma=0
.
This idea is used for instance in
[
ALS20a
]
.
When
\theta<1/2
, we show that the same procedure yields a nondegenerate conformally invariant field
h_{\theta}
, with the surprising and important difference that one has to take a
fractional derivative
of
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}
at
\gamma=0
. This fractional power is equal to
2(1-\theta)
which is twice the crossing exponent computed in
[
JLQ23
]
.

We provide a second and equivalent approach to
h_{\theta}
. We show that each cluster has a nondegenerate Minkowski content in some non-explicit gauge function
r\mapsto r^{2}|\log r|^{1-\theta+o(1)}
and we show that
h_{\theta}
is equal to the sum of the signed Minkowski contents of all the clusters for all
\theta\in(0,1/2)
, similarly to the case
\theta=1/2

[
ALS23
]
.

We emphasise that, when
\theta\not=1/2
,
h_{\theta}
is not log-correlated
(its correlations blow-up as a power of log, see Theorem
1.3
) nor is supposed to be Gaussian.
A conjectural expansion of
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}
for
\theta\in(0,1/2)
near
\gamma=0
in terms of
h_{\theta}
and the local times is given in (
9.24
). This conjecture is closely related to Conjecture
1.14
below.

Level lines and height gap

((3*) in Figure
1.1
)
In addition, we establish a coupling between
h_{\theta}
and CLE
κ
, where
\kappa
and
\theta
are related by (
1.1
).
For each
\theta\in(0,1/2]
, there is a height gap
c(\theta)>0
between the values of
h_{\theta}
on both sides of the level lines.
We show that our field (with
0
boundary conditions) can also be constructed by first sampling a CLE
κ
, and then sampling an independent
h_{\theta}
with wired boundary conditions
in each domain encircled by a CLE
κ
loop.
Although the field
h_{\theta}
with wired boundary condition has constant value
\pm c(\theta)
on the boundary, we emphasise that, when
\theta<1/2
and unlike the GFF, this field is
not
equal to
\pm c(\theta)
plus
h_{\theta}
with zero boundary conditions.
In particular, our field is
not
equal to the CLE nesting field constructed by
[
MWW15
]
.
By definition, the CLE nesting field should also admit CLE as its level lines, but it has no apparent relation to the loop soup occupation field to our knowledge (except when
\theta=1/2
, where the field is the GFF).

The fact that CLE
κ
or SLE
κ
are level lines of
h_{\theta}
is not at all clear form our definition. To prove the level line coupling, we need to prove additional properties about the Brownian excursions induced by the loops that touch the boundary of a cluster, and crucially rely on the conformal restriction property of such excursions deduced in
[
Qia19
]
.

This level line coupling can also be made to work in the chordal case, where an SLE
κ
curve can be coupled with a field
h_{\theta}
with mixed wired/zero boundary conditions.
This generalises the SLE
4
/GFF coupling.
Note that a coupling between SLE
κ
(for all
\kappa>0
) and GFF was established in
[
Dub09
]
and in the celebrated imaginary geometry series
[
MS16a
,
MS16b
,
MS16c
,
MS17
]
, which was another natural generalisation of the SLE
4
/GFF coupling. In the imaginary geometry coupling, when
\kappa\not=4
, the value of the GFF on both sides of the SLE
κ
curve are not constant, but is equal to a constant plus a winding term, which depends on the choice of the starting point and does not have pointwise value on the curve. In comparison, the value of
h_{\theta}
is constant along the curves, as in the SLE
4
/GFF coupling.

In a nutshell, we construct for any
\theta\in(0,1/2]
a coupling between a field
h_{\theta}
, a Brownian loop soup and a CLE
κ
such that:

•

(Figure
1.1
, (1))
The CLE loops are the outermost boundaries of the outermost clusters of the loop soup.

•

(Figure
1.1
, (2*))

h_{\theta}
is a signed version of the local time of the loop soup to the power
1-\theta
. The precise relation is more complicated as described above.

•

(Figure
1.1
, (3*))
The CLE loops form level lines of
h_{\theta}
with a constant height gap on either sides of the loops.

When
\theta=1/2
, this coupling possesses an additional feature: the centred occupation field of the loop soup is half the Wick square of the GFF and the CLE loops are some two-valued sets of the GFF. In particular both the occupation field and the CLE are measurable w.r.t. the GFF. To keep the paper of a reasonable size, we do not attempt to prove such a measurability feature when
\theta\in(0,1/2)
.

1.1
A conformally invariant field out of the Brownian loop soup

Let
D\subset\mathbb{C}
be a bounded simply connected domain and
\theta\in(0,1/2]
. Consider a Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
in
D
with intensity
\theta
.
Let us denote by
\mathfrak{C}
the collection of all clusters
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
. Conditionally on
\mathfrak{C}
, let
(\sigma_{\mathcal{C}})_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}}
be i.i.d. signs taking values in
\{\pm 1\}
with equal probability 1/2. If a point
x\in D
belongs to some cluster
\mathcal{C}
, we will write
\sigma_{x}
for the sign
\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}
of that cluster.

Let
\gamma\in(0,2)
and let
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
be the multiplicative chaos of the Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
constructed in
[
ABJL23
]
, normalised so that
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{d}x)\right]=2\mathrm{d}x
; see Section
2.2
for details. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sigma_{x}=+1\right\}}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{d}x)
be the multiplicative chaos of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
restricted to positive clusters. Note that this definition is meaningful since every
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
–typical point is visited by a loop (actually by infinitely many loops, see
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.8]
).
Let
h_{\gamma}
be the field obtained from
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}
by

(h_{\gamma},f):=\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{D}f(x)(\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)-\mathrm{d}x),

(1.2)

for any bounded measurable function
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}
. Here
Z_{\gamma}
is a normalising constant defined in (
1.3
) below in terms of some crossing probability.
With some abuse of notation, we will often write
(h_{\gamma},f)=\int h_{\gamma}(x)f(x)\mathrm{d}x
. Note that
h_{\gamma}
is defined as a random generalised function on the whole complex plane, vanishing outside of
D
.

To define the normalising constants we will use in this article, we first introduce the following notations:

Notation 1.1
.

For any family
\mathcal{L}
of loops and any sets
A,B\subset D
, we will denote by
\{A\overset{\mathcal{L}}{\leftrightarrow}B\}
the event that there is a cluster of loops in
\mathcal{L}
that intersects both
A
and
B
. If
x
and
y
are points, we will simply write
\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}}{\leftrightarrow}A\}
and
\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}}{\leftrightarrow}y\}
instead of
\{\{x\}\overset{\mathcal{L}}{\leftrightarrow}A\}
and
\{\{x\}\overset{\mathcal{L}}{\leftrightarrow}\{y\}\}
.

We will then use two normalising constants
Z_{r}
and
Z_{\gamma}
defined by

Z_{r}:=\mathbb{P}\Big(e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}r\mathbb{D}\Big),\quad r>0,\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad Z_{\gamma}:=\mathbb{P}\Big(e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}0\Big),\quad\gamma\in(0,2),

(1.3)

where
\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}
is an
a
-thick loop (
a=\gamma^{2}/2
) at the origin defined in Section
2.1
that is independent from
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
.

Theorem 1.2

(Construction of
h_{\theta}
)
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
.
For any bounded measurable function
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}
,
(h_{\gamma},f)
converges in
L^{2}
as
\gamma\to 0
.
Moreover, for any
\varepsilon>0
,
h_{\gamma}
converges in
L^{2}
as
\gamma\to 0
in the Sobolev space
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})
(
4.7
) to some random field
h_{\theta}\in H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})
.

Furthermore, the normalising constant
Z_{\gamma}
satisfies
Z_{\gamma}=\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
.

Theorem 1.3

(Properties of
h_{\theta}
)
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
.

1.

Covariance:
There exists a measurable function
C_{\theta}:D\times D\to[0,\infty]
such that for all test functions
f
,

\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta},f)^{2}\right]=\int_{D\times D}f(x)C_{\theta}(x,y)f(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

(1.4)

The blow-up of
C_{\theta}
on the diagonal is given by

C_{\theta}(x,y)=|\log|x-y||^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}

as
x-y\to 0
, while staying at a positive distance from
\partial D
.

2.

Conformal invariance: Let
\psi:D\to\widetilde{D}
be a conformal map between two bounded simply connected domains and let
h_{\theta,D}
and

h_{\theta,\widetilde{D}}
denote the fields from Theorem
1.2
in the domains
D
and
\widetilde{D}
.
Then
1
1

1

Recall that by definition
(h_{\theta,D}\circ\psi^{-1},\tilde{f})=(h_{\theta,D},\tilde{f}\circ\psi^{-1})
for any test function
\tilde{f}
.

h_{\theta,D}\circ\psi^{-1}
and
h_{\theta,\widetilde{D}}

have the same law.

3.

Symmetry: Let
h_{\theta}^{-}
denote the field obtained in a similar way as
h_{\theta}
with negative clusters instead of positive ones. Then
h_{\theta}^{-}=-h_{\theta}
a.s. In particular,
h_{\theta}\overset{\mathrm{(d)}}{=}-h_{\theta}
.

4.

Nondegeneracy: Let
f:D\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth test function, non identically zero. Then the law of
(h_{\theta},f)
is nonatomic. In particular,
(h_{\theta},f)\neq 0
a.s.

As already mentioned, when
\theta=1/2
,
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
agrees with the hyperbolic cosine
:e^{\gamma h}:\mathrm{d}x

+:e^{-\gamma h}:\mathrm{d}x
of the GFF
h
with Dirichlet boundary condition. Here
h
is normalised so that
\mathbb{E}\left[h(x)h(y)\right]\sim-\log|x-y|
as
x-y\to 0
and
:e^{\pm\gamma h}:\mathrm{d}x
are GMC measures normalised so that
\mathbb{E}\left[:e^{\pm\gamma h}:\mathrm{d}x\right]=\mathrm{d}x
. See
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.5]
. Because the isomorphism relating the local time to the GFF is much stronger in the discrete, the proof of this result was obtained by first proving a discrete approximation of
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
. In Theorem
8.3
, we show a stronger version of this convergence by showing that the discrete approximation holds cluster by cluster, for any
\theta\in(0,1/2]
. It implies that, when
\theta=1/2
, the measure
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sigma_{x}=+1\right\}}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{d}x)
agrees with
:e^{\gamma h}:\mathrm{d}x
. As a corollary,

Theorem 1.4
.

When
\theta=1/2
,
h_{\theta}
has the law of a multiple of the GFF in
D
.
More precisely, there is a deterministic constant
c_{1/2}\in(0,\infty)
and a coupling between a critical loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{1/2}
together with i.i.d. spins
(\sigma_{\mathcal{C}})_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}}
and a Gaussian free field
h
in
D
such that almost surely,

•

\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}=:e^{\gamma h}:\mathrm{d}x
and
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}=2:\cosh(\gamma h):\mathrm{d}x
for all
\gamma\in(0,2)
;

•

h_{1/2}=c_{1/2}h
and
:L:=\frac{1}{2}:h^{2}:
.

The appearance of the constant
c_{1/2}
comes from the fact that we do not renormalise
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)-\mathrm{d}x
by
\gamma
, but by a normalising constant
Z_{\gamma}
that behaves asymptotically like a multiple of
\gamma
(see
[
JLQ23
, Lemma 2.6]
for a similar result where the
a
-thick loop is replaced by a small disc).

Remark 1.5
.

As a consequence of Theorem
1.4
, the covariance
C_{\theta}
is a constant multiple of the Green function when
\theta=1/2
. When
\theta<1/2
,
we do not know any close formula for
C_{\theta}
.
We intend to investigate this question in the future.
However, note that the conformal invariance of
C_{\theta}
implies that there is some measurable function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that for all
x,y\in D
,
C_{\theta}(x,y)=F(G_{D}(x,y))
. This simply comes from the fact that
G_{D}(x,y)
characterises the conformal type of a disc with two punctures.

1.2
Level lines and the height gap of
h_{\theta}

The outermost boundaries of the outermost clusters form natural interfaces coupled with
h_{\theta}
.
In Theorem
1.7
below, we show that, for general intensities
\theta\in(0,1/2]
, the (non-nested) CLE
κ
loops are level lines of
h_{\theta}
, where
\kappa
and
\theta
are related by (
1.1
),
and that there is a constant height gap
c(\theta)>0
.

The field
h_{\theta}
given by Theorem
1.2
is a field with
0
boundary conditions.
In order to state Theorem
1.7
, we need to define a field
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}}
out of a loop soup with wired boundary conditions, which will turn out to have constant value
\pm c(\theta)
on the boundary.
Let
x_{0}\in D
and let
\mathcal{C}
be the outermost cluster surrounding
x_{0}
. Let
O
be the simply connected domain delimited by the outermost boundary of
\mathcal{C}
and let
\sigma_{\partial O}
be the sign associated to
\mathcal{C}
.
We recall the following fact from
[
QW19
, Theorem 1]
. The law of the loop soup inside
O
, conditionally given
O
, is that of the union of two conditionally independent collections of loops. The loops that do not touch the boundary of
O
are simply distributed according to an unconditioned loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{O}^{\theta}
in
O
. The loops that touch the boundary form a random collection
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}_{\partial O}
of excursions. The law of
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}_{\partial O}
is explicit only when
\theta=1/2
. However, for general intensities
\theta\leq 1/2
,
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}_{\partial O}
is conformally invariant in the following strong sense: if
\mathcal{E}^{\theta}_{\partial O}
is conformally mapped to the unit disc, then the resulting collection of excursions
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
is independent of
O
and invariant in law under Möbius transformations.

Let
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}}
be the random field in
\mathbb{D}
obtained by restricting
h_{\theta}
to test functions compactly supported in
O
and then by conformally mapping
O
onto
\mathbb{D}
. More precisely, let
\psi:O\to\mathbb{D}
be a conformal map. For any smooth test function
f:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
with compact support in
\mathbb{D}
, we define
(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)=(h_{\theta},f\circ\psi)
. By
[
QW19
, Theorem 1]
, the law of
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}}
does not depend on the choice of the conformal map
\psi
and
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}}
is independent of
O
.

In Theorem
1.7
below, we will consider smooth test functions
f_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{D}\to[0,\infty)
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, whose supports concentrate on the circle
\partial\mathbb{D}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We will make the following assumptions:

Assumption 1.6
.

For all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
f_{\varepsilon}
is compactly supported in
\{x\in\mathbb{D}:\mathrm{d}(x,\partial\mathbb{D})<\varepsilon\}
and
\int_{\mathbb{D}}f_{\varepsilon}=1
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\left(C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}}(x,y)+\max(1,-\log|x-y|)\right)f_{\varepsilon}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y<\infty,

(1.5)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{|x-y|<\delta\right\}}\left(C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}}(x,y)+\max(1,-\log|x-y|)\right)f_{\varepsilon}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=0

(1.6)

where
C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}}
denotes the covariance of the field
h_{\theta}
in the domain
\mathbb{D}
with zero boundary condition; see (
1.4
).

If
u:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a smooth function with
u(0)=0
,
u(r)=0
for
r\geq 1
and
\int_{(0,\infty)}u=1
, then
f_{\varepsilon}:x\in\mathbb{D}\mapsto\frac{1}{2\pi\varepsilon|x|}u(\frac{1-|x|}{\varepsilon}),\varepsilon\in(0,1),
satisfies the desired properties.

Theorem 1.7
.

For
\theta\in(0,1/2]
, there exists a constant
c(\theta)>0
depending only on
\theta
such that:

•

(Constant expectation)
For all test function
f
compactly supported in
\mathbb{D}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\right]=\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}c(\theta)\int_{\mathbb{D}}f,\quad\quad\text{a.s.}

(1.7)

•

(Constant boundary conditions) Let
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be a sequence of test functions satisfying Assumption
1.6
. Then

(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f_{\varepsilon})-\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}c(\theta)\xrightarrow[\varepsilon\to 0]{L^{2}}0.

(1.8)

•

(Spatial Markov property) We can construct
h_{\theta}
in the domain
D
as follows. We first sample a CLE
{}_{\kappa}=\{\ell,\ell\in
CLE
\}
and i.i.d. signs
\{\sigma_{\ell},\ell\in
CLE
\}
.
Let
h_{\theta,\ell}^{\mathrm{wired}}
,
\ell\in
CLE, be independent fields in
\mathbb{D}
with wired boundary conditions and sign
\sigma_{\ell}
on
\partial\mathbb{D}
. For
\ell\in
CLE, let
\psi_{\ell}
be a conformal map from
\mathbb{D}
onto the domain encircled by
\ell
.
Then

\sum_{\ell}h_{\theta,\ell}^{\mathrm{wired}}\circ\psi_{\ell}^{-1}

has the law of the field
h_{\theta}
in
D
with zero boundary condition.

The height gap
c(\theta)
is expressed as the limit of the ratio of the probabilities of some crossing event; see (
7.2
).

We will prove in Section
9.4
that a stronger version of the “constant expectation” property holds in dimension one, i.e., the 1D analogue of this field is a martingale.
In Section
9.4
, we define
h_{\theta}
in 1D to be a square Bessel process taken to the power
\nu=1-\theta
and multiplied by an i.i.d. sign for each of its excursion away from
0
. In Lemma
9.9
, we show that this one-dimensional process is a martingale if and only if
\nu=1-\theta
, which again singles out the special role of the exponent
1-\theta
.

We believe that, if
\theta<1/2
, the “spatial Markov” decomposition for
h_{\theta}
only holds along some special interfaces,
including the boundaries of the clusters. This makes our field more complicated to analyse than the GFF. For example, we believe that we cannot couple a
nested
CLE
κ
as the level lines of
h_{\theta}
, unless
\theta=1/2
. As already mentioned, for
\theta<1/2
, our fields
h_{\theta}
are different from the
nesting fields
built in
[
MWW15
]
.
Indeed, for the nesting fields, the correlation blows up logarithmically on the diagonal

[
MWW15
, Theorem 1.3]
,
and in our case it blows up as
\log
to the power
2(1-\theta)+o(1)
.

By
[
SW12
]
, one can
define an exploration process of a CLE which stops in the middle of a loop. One can conformally map
\varphi:H\to\mathbb{H}
the unexplored region
H
onto the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}
in such a way that the explored portion of this CLE loop is sent to
\mathbb{R}^{-}
and the rest of the boundary is sent to
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. The portion of the CLE loop that remains unexplored is a chordal SLE
κ
curve
\eta
from 0 to infinity. Suppose that this CLE is the outer boundaries of the clusters in a loop soup
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\mathbb{H}}
.
For any smooth test function
f:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
with compact support in
\mathbb{H}
, we define
(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}},f)=(h_{\theta},f\circ\varphi^{-1})
. By
[
Qia19
]
, the law of
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}
does not depend on the choice of the conformal map
\varphi
and
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}
is independent of
H
. Moreover, the loop soup satisfies spatial Markov property as one continues to explore along
\eta
.
This implies a coupling between
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}
and
\eta
, stated in the following.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}
be a field in
\mathbb{H}
with wired boundary conditions on
\mathbb{R}^{-}
and zero on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. There is a curve
\eta
coupled with
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}
, such that
\eta
is a chordal SLE
κ
from
0
to
\infty
. For each
t>0
, let
g_{t}
be a conformal map from
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
onto
\mathbb{H}
that sends
\eta(t)
to
0
and
\infty
to
\infty
.
Then conditionally on
\eta([0,t])
,
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}\circ g_{t}^{-1}
is distributed as
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}
and is independent from
\eta([0,t])
.

This is a generalisation of the SLE
4
/GFF coupling.
We can also immediately deduce from Theorem
1.7
that
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}
has boundary value
c(\theta)
(resp.
0
) on the left (resp. right) side of
\eta
.

Combining (
1.7
) from Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.8
, we see that for all
x\in\mathbb{H}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{mixed}}(x)\right]
is equal to
c(\theta)
times the probability that a chordal SLE
κ
from 0 to
\infty
goes to the right of
x
. It turns out that this probability is explicit, given by Schramm’s formula
[
Sch01
]
.

1.3
h_{\theta}
as a sum of Minkowski contents

We now give another description of
h_{\theta}
, somewhat more geometric. We will show that the field
h_{\theta}
is a limit of random signed Radon measures supported on the topological closures of clusters of

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D}
,
and give an expression for these measures.
This establishes the analogue of the result in the critical case
[
ALS20a
,
ALS23
]
, where
h_{1/2}
is the GFF.

We start by defining a nondegenerate measure supported on the closure of a given cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
.
Given a closed (fractal) set
A\subset\mathbb{C}
, it is a classical problem (that can be highly non-trivial) to define a nondegenerate measure supported on
A
. One possibility is to try to renormalise the Lebesgue measure of the
r
-neighbourhood of
A
and take the limit as
r\to 0^{+}
. More precisely, one seeks an appropriate gauge function
\phi:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
such that the measure

r^{-2}\phi(r)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mathrm{dist}(x,A)<r\right\}}\mathrm{d}x

converges weakly as
r\to 0^{+}
to a nondegenerate limit. If such a function can be found, the resulting measure is then called the Minkowski content of
A
in the gauge
\phi
.

It is a well known fact that the Minkowski content of a single planar Brownian trajectory is well defined and nondegenerate in the gauge
r\mapsto r^{2}|\log r|
. In this case, it coincides with the occupation measure of the path.
Because the non-renormalised local time of a given cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
(i.e. the sum of the local times of all the trajectories of a given cluster) is infinite, the Brownian gauge function
r\mapsto r^{2}|\log r|
is not appropriate for the clusters of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
. And indeed,
[
ALS20a
]
showed that, when
\theta=1/2
, the Minkowski content of a given cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
is well-defined and nondegenerate in the gauge
r\mapsto r^{2}|\log r|^{1/2}
.
In Theorem
1.9
below, we resolve the analogous problem when
\theta\in(0,1/2)
by showing that the Minkowski content of a given cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
is well-defined and nondegenerate in some non-explicit gauge function
r\mapsto r^{2}/Z_{r}
that behaves like
r^{2}|\log r|^{1-\theta+o(1)}
as
r\to 0
.

We order the clusters of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D}
as follows.
Let
(x_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a dense sequence in
D
.
We will denote by
\mathcal{C}_{k}
the outermost cluster of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D}

surrounding
x_{k}
, among the clusters different from

\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k-1}
.
By construction, the clusters
(\mathcal{C}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
are two by two distinct.
It is also easy to see that a.s. this is an enumeration of the clusters of

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D}
.
For
k\geq 1
, let
\mu_{k,r}
and
\mu_{k,\gamma}
be the random Radon measures defined by

\mu_{k,r}(B)=\dfrac{1}{Z_{r}}\int_{B}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})<r\right\}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad\mu_{k,\gamma}(B)=\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{B}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})<1\right\}}(1-\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2})\,\mathrm{d}x,

for all Borel set
B\subset D
.
Recall that the normalising constants
Z_{r}
and
Z_{\gamma}
are defined in (
1.3
).

Theorem 1.9

(Minkowksi contents of clusters)
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
. For all
k\geq 1
,
\mu_{k,r}
and
\mu_{k,\gamma}
converge in probability w.r.t. the topology of weak convergence as
r\to 0
and
\gamma\to 0
to the same random Radon measure
\mu_{k}
. Moreover,
\mu_{k}
is a.s. a non-negative finite measure supported on
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
and
\mu_{k}(\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k})>0
a.s.

Recall that we denote by
\sigma_{\mathcal{C}_{k}}
the spin associated to the
k
-th cluster
\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
The field
h_{\theta}
is then obtained by:

Theorem 1.10

(Construction of
h_{\theta}
from the Minkowski contents)
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
. For all
\varepsilon>0
and all bounded measurable function
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}
, the following convergences hold a.s. and in
L^{2}
:

\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{\mathcal{C}_{j}}\mu_{j}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}h_{\theta}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{\mathcal{C}_{j}}(\mu_{j},f)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}(h_{\theta},f)

in the spaces
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathbb{R}
respectively.

In other words, the “restriction of
|h_{\theta}|
” to
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is a well-defined Radon measure that agrees with
\mu_{k}
.
Note however that this does not at all mean that the field
h_{\theta}
is a signed measure.
Most likely, the associated total variation measure

\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{j}

diverges, as
k\to+\infty
, in every open subset of
D
;
so the compensations induced by the signs are necessary for the convergence.
This phenomenon is known to occur when
\theta=1/2
.

1.4
Connecting two points with a cluster

We now want to elaborate on the intimate relation between the field
h_{\theta}
and the connectivity properties of the loop soup.
We are first going to define the law
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y}
of the loop soup conditioned on the event that two given points
x
and
y
belong to the closure of the same cluster. Because this event has a vanishing probability, we will define this law via a limiting procedure; see Theorem
1.11
.
Theorem
1.12
then provides a Doob-transform approach to the definition of
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y}
:
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y}
can be informally obtained by reweighting the law of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
by
h_{\theta}(x)h_{\theta}(y)/C_{\theta}(x,y)
.

Let
x,y\in D
be two distinct points. For all
r>0
, let
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y,r}
be the law of a loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
conditioned on the event
\{D(x,r)\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(y,r)\}
that a cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
connects the two discs
D(x,r)
and
D(y,r)
.

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
x,y\in D
be two distinct points. Then
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y,r}
converges weakly as
r\to 0
to some probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y}
, with respect to the topology induced by
d_{\mathfrak{L}}
(
2.4
).

We can now state the aforementioned relation between the field
h_{\theta}
and the connectivity properties of the loop soup:

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
F:D\times D\times\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable function such that for all
\mathcal{L}\in\mathfrak{L}
,
F(\cdot,\cdot,\mathcal{L})
is smooth. Then

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{D}F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})h_{\theta}(x)h_{\theta}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\right]=\int_{D\times D}C_{\theta}(x,y)\mathbb{E}_{x\leftrightarrow y}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L})\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

There are many instances in statistical mechanics where such a relationship between the two-point function of a field and connection probabilities holds.
The Edwards–Sokal coupling between the Potts model and the random cluster model is a famous example
[
FK72
,
ES88
]
.
These relations can be very powerful tools to study one model via the knowledge of the other.
As already alluded to, the clusters of the loop soup are well understood at criticality (
\theta=1/2
) thanks to the GFF. On the other hand, the current paper, together with
[
JLQ23
]
, studies directly the clusters (
\theta\leq 1/2
) and then infers properties on
h_{\theta}
.

Remark 1.13
.

When
\theta=1/2
, the law
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y}
can be described precisely as follows. Consider the metric graph GFF
\tilde{\varphi}
on some graph and let
x
and
y
be two distinct points. The BFS-Dynkin isomorphism
[
BFS82
,
Dyn84a
,
Dyn84b
]
tells us that for any test function
F
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{\varphi}(x)\tilde{\varphi}(y)F(\tilde{\varphi}^{2}/2)\right]=G(x,y)\mathbb{E}\left[F(\tilde{\varphi}^{2}/2+\ell_{\wp})\right]

(1.9)

where
\wp
is an independent Brownian excursion from
x
to
y
and
\ell_{\wp}
is its local time. Note that by independence of the signs on different clusters, the left hand side can be rewritten as
\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{\varphi}(x)\tilde{\varphi}(y)F(\tilde{\varphi}^{2}/2)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\leftrightarrow y\right\}}\right]
.
In the scaling limit, the identity (
1.9
) suggests that the law of the cluster joining
x
to
y
under
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y}
can be obtained as follows. Sample a critical Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{1/2}
and an independent Brownian excursion
\wp
from
x
to
y
, then consider the cluster joining
x
to
y
in the collection of paths
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{1/2}\cup\{\wp\}
.

Viewing the path
\wp
as an SLE
2
curve decorated by Brownian loops coming from a loop soup with intensity
\theta=1

[
LSW03a
]
, we can see the above description as a notion of duality between the loop soup with intensity
1/2
and the loop soup with intensity
1/2+1=3/2
. See also Lemma
9.10
.
As we will see in Section
9
, some notion of duality between the intensities
\theta
and
2-\theta
might hold more generally for all
\theta\in(0,1/2]
. We will see that this is reminiscent to the duality between Bessel processes of dimensions
d
and
4-d
.

1.5
Conjectural construction of
h_{\theta}
from a discrete loop soup

Let
D_{N}\subset\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cap D
be a subset of the square lattice with mesh size
\frac{1}{N}
which approximates the continuous domain
D
. See (
2.6
) for a precise definition.
Fix
\theta\in(0,1/2]
and let
\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
be a random walk loop soup in the metric graph
\widetilde{D}_{N}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{N}=\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N}}
be the collection of clusters
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
. Conditionally on
\mathfrak{C}_{N}
, let
\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N}
, be i.i.d. spins taking values in
\{\pm 1\}
with equal probability.
For all
x\in\widetilde{D}_{N}
, let
\ell_{x}
be the total local time accumulated by all loops at
x
, see (
2.7
).
For orientation, we recall that for all
x
,
\ell_{x}
is distributed according to Gamma
(\theta,G_{D_{N}}(x,x))
where
G_{D_{N}}(x,x)
is the discrete Green function which behaves, with our normalisation, like
G_{D_{N}}(x,x)\sim\frac{1}{2\pi}\log N
as
N\to\infty
.
We can now define what we conjecture being the analogue of
h_{\theta}
in the discrete:

h_{\theta,N}(x):=\frac{\Gamma(\theta)}{2^{1-\theta}\Gamma(2-\theta)}\sigma_{\mathcal{C}_{x}}\{2\pi\ell_{x}\}^{1-\theta},

(1.10)

where
\mathcal{C}_{x}
denotes the cluster containing
x
. Importantly, there is no renormalisation in the definition of
h_{\theta,N}
.

Conjecture 1.14
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
. There exists
c_{\mathrm{conj}}=c_{\mathrm{conj}}(\theta)>0
such that for all bounded smooth test function
f
,

\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{x\in D_{N}}f(x)h_{\theta,N}(x)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{\mathrm{(d)}}c_{\mathrm{conj}}(h_{\theta},f).

(1.11)

In Section
9
, we considerably elaborate on this conjecture and address the question of defining the renormalised powers of
h_{\theta}
. The powers of
h_{\theta}
that reduce to integer powers
:L_{x}^{n}:
of the local time
:L_{x}:
have already been defined in
[
LJ11
,
LJMR17
]
. In order to define the remaining powers (the “odd” powers of
h_{\theta}
), we give a specific approximation scheme from the discrete and we conjecture that it possesses a nondegenerate scaling limit (Conjecture
9.11
). This is based on a connection with the one-dimensional Brownian loop soup (first revealed in
[
JLQ23
]
) and reveals a surprising notion of duality between the intensities
\theta
and
\theta^{*}=2-\theta
. If this conjecture held true, we would obtain the following expansion for
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}
around
\gamma=0
:

\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)=\Big(\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\gamma^{2k}}{2^{k}}\frac{\Gamma(\theta)}{k!\Gamma(k+\theta)}(2\pi)^{k}:L_{x}^{k}:+c_{\mathrm{conj}}\gamma^{2(1-\theta)}\frac{\gamma^{2k}}{2^{k}}\frac{\Gamma(\theta^{*})}{k!\Gamma(k+\theta^{*})}(2\pi)^{k}:h_{\theta}(x)L_{x}^{k}:\Big)\mathrm{d}x.

See Section
9.5
for the definition of the different terms appearing in the above display.
We plan to prove these conjectures in a future work.

1.6
Relation to other conformally invariant and covariant fields

Let us review some other conformally invariant and covariant fields that appeared in the mathematical literature.

For
\theta=1/2
, our field
h_{\theta}
is the continuum GFF (up to a multiplicative constant).
We believe that for
\theta<1/2
, the fields
h_{\theta}

are neither Gaussian nor Markovian (physicists would say non-local fields).

The GFF is in particular a CLE
4
nesting field (Miller-Sheffield coupling).
In
[
MWW15
]
, the authors constructed other conformally invariant
fields using the nested CLE
κ
for

\kappa\in(8/3,4]
.
However, as already mentioned, for
\theta<1/2
,
our fields
h_{\theta}
are different from the
nesting fields
built in
[
MWW15
]
.
In the case
\theta=1/2
, the clusters of the corresponding Brownian loop soup can be described through the nested labelled CLE
4
; see
[
ALS23
, Remark 17]
.
However, for
\theta<1/2
, we believe that the clusters of the Brownian loop soup cannot be described through the nested CLE
κ
,
since there is too much independence in the latter.

For
\theta=1/4
, the outer boundaries of the Brownian loop soup clusters
are the CLE
3
loops,
which are also the scaling limits of interfaces in the critical 2D Ising model

[
KS16
]
.
But this coincidence is just for the outer boundaries.
The full scaling limit of Ising spin clusters is different from the Brownian loop soup clusters.
The dimension of the former is
187/96

(dimension of the CLE
3
gasket
[
MSW14
]
),
and the dimension of the latter is
2
.
We further believe that our field is none of the Ising local fields studied in the Conformal Field Theory (CFT),
and in particular not the continuum
Ising spin field
constructed
in
[
CGN15
,
CGN16
]
,
since the two-point correlations of these fields from the Ising CFT
blow up on the diagonal as a power (and not a power of the
\log
),
and these fields are conformally covariant but not invariant
(under conformal mappings, the modulus of the derivative to some power appears).
Still, it is an open question whether
h_{\theta}
for
\theta=1/4
is related to the Ising model in some other way, perhaps through a non-local transformation.

We would like to mention that other conformally covariant (but not invariant)
fields have been constructed using the Brownian loop soup.
First, there are the so-called
winding fields
, obtained by renormalising

e^{i\beta W(z)}
, where
W(z)
is the sum of the indices of the Brownian loops around the point
z\in D
, and
\beta
is a parameter in
[0,\pi]

[
CGK16
,
vdBCL18
,
LJ19
]
.
The correlations of the winding fields blow up as a power on the diagonal.
Informally speaking, the winding fields have constant values on the dual clusters of the Brownian loop soup, that is to say on the connected components of the domain minus the Brownian loops, all these connected components being
CLE
κ
gaskets in the scaling limit.
So this is different from our setting, since the fields
h_{\theta}

are in a sense constant on the clusters of the Brownian loop soup,
not on their planar duals.
It has been observed that for
\theta=1/2
and

\beta=\pi
(so that
e^{i\beta W(z)}\in\{-1,1\}
),
the corresponding winding field is exactly the Kramers-Wannier
dual (a planar duality inherited from the duality in FK random clusters)
of the 2D GFF
[
vdBCL18
, Theorem 2.1]
.
Another family of fields constructed from the Brownian loop soups are the
so called
layering fields
,
which are a variant of winding fields, where one considers only the outer boundaries of Brownian loops, which are SLE
8/3
loops.
The latter construction does not capture the structure of the clusters of Brownian loops, since one keeps only little information on those loops.
However, these layering fields are known to satisfy a lot of exact solvability
and have been related to the CFT
[
CGK16
,
CGPRR21
,
CFGK20
,
CFGK22a
,
CFGK22b
]
.

1.7
Organisation of the paper

The paper is organised as follows:

•

Section
2
: we recall some definitions and results that we will need from the literature. We will also state and prove preliminary lemmas that we will be useful in the rest of the paper.

•

Section
3
: we prove the convergence of the ratio of two hitting probabilities by clusters; see Theorem
3.1
. To do so, we will develop in Theorem
3.3
a general coupling result between two trajectories of the same Markov chain with distinct starting points.

•

Section
4
: we prove Theorems
1.2
,
1.11
and
1.12
about the construction of
h_{\theta}
, the conditioned probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{x\leftrightarrow y}
and the link between the two.

•

Section
5
: we prove Theorems
1.9
and
1.10
concerning the definition of the Minkowski contents of the clusters and their relations to the field
h_{\theta}
.

•

Section
6
: we obtain estimates on the loops that touch the outer boundary of a given cluster; see in particular Propositions
6.4
,
6.5
and
6.16
.

•

Section
7
: we prove the properties of
h_{\theta}
stated in Theorem
1.3
. We also prove that CLE/SLE are level lines of
h_{\theta}
as stated in Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
.

•

Section
8
: Relying on a discrete approximation, we prove the identification of
h_{\theta}
with the GFF when
\theta=1/2
(Theorem
1.4
). See in particular Theorem
8.3
.

•

Section
9
: we prove an expansion of the unsigned measure
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
in powers of
\gamma
; see Theorem
9.2
. We then investigate the Wick powers of
h_{\theta}
and state a precise conjecture about a renormalisation procedure that would define them (Conjecture
9.11
). We further show that these Wick powers would allow one to expand the signed measure
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}
; see Theorem
9.12
and (
9.24
). Finally, we study the analogue of the field
h_{\theta}
in dimension 1; see Section
9.4
.

Most sections of the current paper contain an introduction that describes more thoroughly its content.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Measures on paths and definition of the Brownian loop soup

•

Brownian motion: in this paper, we will always consider Brownian motions with infinitesimal generator
\Delta
rather than the standard Brownian motion,
which has generator
\frac{1}{2}\Delta
(this is to have more tractable constants in isomorphism relations).

•

p_{D}(t,z,w),t>0,z,w\in D
: transition probability of Brownian motion killed upon leaving the domain
D
.
If

p_{\mathbb{C}}(t,z,w)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}\exp\Big(-\frac{|w-z|^{2}}{4\pi t}\Big),\quad\quad t>0,\quad z,w\in D,

denotes the transition probabilities of Brownian motion in the full plane, and if
\pi_{D}(t,z,w)
denotes the probability that a Brownian bridge of duration
t
remains in the domain
D
throughout, then

p_{D}(t,z,w)=p_{\mathbb{C}}(t,z,w)\pi_{D}(t,z,w).

•

Green function
G_{D}(z,w)=\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{D}(t,z,w)\mathrm{d}t
,
z,w\in D
. In our normalisation,

G_{D}(z,w)\sim-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(|w-z|)

as
|w-z|\to 0
.

•

Gaussian free field
\varphi
: random generalised function such that
((\varphi,f))_{f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C})}
is a centred Gaussian process with covariance given by
\mathbb{E}\left[(\varphi,f)(\varphi,g)\right]=\int_{D\times D}f(z)G_{D}(z,w)f(w)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}w
. See
[
WP21
,
BP21
]
for introductions to the GFF. We will denote by
h=\sqrt{2\pi}\varphi
the GFF in
D
normalised so that
\mathbb{E}\left[h_{z}h_{w}\right]\sim-\log|z-w|
as
z-w\to 0
.

•

\mathbb{P}_{D,t}^{z,w},t>0,z,w\in D
: probability measure on
Brownian bridges from
z
to
w
of duration
t
, conditioned on staying in
D
.

•

\mu^{\rm loop}_{D}
: loop measure in
D
:

\mu^{\rm loop}_{D}(d\wp)=\int_{D}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{D,t}^{z,z}(d\wp)p_{D}(t,z,z)\dfrac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\mathrm{d}z.

(2.1)

•

\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
,
\theta>0
: Brownian loop soup in
D
with intensity
\theta
. Random collection of loops distributed according to a Poisson point process with intensity
\theta\mu^{\rm loop}_{D}
.

•

\mu_{D}^{z,w},z,w\in D
: measure on continuous paths
from
z
to
w
in
D
:

\mu_{D}^{z,w}(d\wp)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}_{D,t}^{z,w}(d\wp)p_{D}(t,z,w)\mathrm{d}t.

(2.2)

•

\Xi_{a}^{x,D}
,
x\in D
,
a>0
: random loop which is the concatenation at
x
of all the excursions in a Poisson point process with intensity
a\mu_{D}^{x,x}
. If there is no ambiguity, we will simply write
\Xi_{a}^{x}
.

Depending on our needs, we will view clusters of loops as random collections of loops or as random compact subsets of
\overline{D}
.
We now define the metrics we will use in these two situations. In the latter case, we will denote by
(\mathcal{K},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{K}})
the collection of all compact subsets of
\overline{D}
, endowed with the Hausdorff metric:

\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{K}}(A,B)=\max\left(\sup_{x\in A}d(x,B),\sup_{x\in B}d(x,A)\right),\quad\quad A,B\in\mathcal{K}.

(2.3)

We now specify the distance we will use between collections of loops.

Distance between collections of loops

It is easier to consider the loops in the loop soup as rooted loops (we can sample a root uniformly at random w.r.t. the time parametrization of each loop).
Let
\mathcal{P}
be the set of all parametrized continuous planar curves
\gamma
defined on a finite time-interval
[0,t_{\gamma}]
. We endow
\mathcal{P}
with the metric

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{P}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=|t_{\gamma}-t_{\gamma^{\prime}}|+\sup|\gamma(\cdot/t_{\gamma})-\gamma^{\prime}(\cdot/t_{\gamma^{\prime}})|.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
be the collection of locally finite families
\mathcal{L}
of loops in
\mathcal{P}
. We define the following metric on
\mathfrak{L}
:

\displaystyle d_{\mathfrak{L}}(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\iff\,

\displaystyle\exists f:\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\text{ injective such that }\forall\gamma\in\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon},d_{\mathcal{P}}(\gamma,f(\gamma))\leq\varepsilon

(2.4)

\displaystyle\text{ and similarly when we exchange }\mathcal{L}\text{ and }\mathcal{L}^{\prime},

where
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
is the collection of loops in
\mathcal{L}
with a diameter larger than
\varepsilon
.

Random walk loop soup

For a portion
D_{N}
of the square lattice
\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we will also need to consider the random walk loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
in
D_{N}
with intensity
\theta
, introduced by
[
LTF07
]
. Its definition is analogous to the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
with discrete versions of the heat kernel and Brownian bridges. See
[
ABJL23
, Section 2.2]
for details.

2.2
Multiplicative chaos of the Brownian loop soup

In this section, we will recall the definition and some properties of the multiplicative chaos measure
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
of the Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
in a given bounded simply connected domain
D\subset\mathbb{C}
. We will recall the construction from the discrete setting since it is the easiest one to explain and since it will be the approximation we will use in Section
8
. Let us mention however that there is a construction directly in the continuum which is based on Brownian multiplicative chaos measures
[
BBK94
,
AHS20
,
Jeg20
,
Jeg22
,
Jeg21
]
. We follow
[
ABJL23
]
. Let us stress already that our normalisation of
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
is given by
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{d}z)\right]=2\mathrm{d}z
which is different from the one in
[
ABJL23
]
: if we denote by
\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\gamma}
the measure constructed in
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.1]
, we will have

\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{d}z)=a^{1-\theta}2^{1+\theta}\Gamma(\theta)\CR(z,D)^{-\gamma^{2}/2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\gamma}(\mathrm{d}z).

(2.5)

Let
N\geq 1
be a large integer and let
D_{N}\subset\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be a discrete approximation of
D
. Specifically, assuming without loss of generality that
D
contains the origin, let

D_{N}:=\Big\{z\in D\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{2}:\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{there~exists~a~path~in~}\tfrac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathrm{~from~}z\mathrm{~to~the~origin}\\
\mathrm{whose~distance~to~the~boundary~of~}D\mathrm{~is~at~least~}\tfrac{1}{N}\end{array}\Big\}.

(2.6)

Let
\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
be a random walk loop soup with intensity

\theta
. For any vertex
x\in D_{N}
and any loop
\wp=(\wp(t))_{0\leq t\leq T(\wp)}\in\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
, we denote by
\ell_{x}(\wp)
the local time of
\wp
at
x
and
\ell_{x}
the local time of the loop soup at
x
:

\ell_{x}(\wp):=N^{2}\int_{0}^{T(\wp)}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\wp(t)=z\right\}}\mathrm{d}t\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\ell_{x}:=\sum_{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}}\ell_{x}(\wp).

(2.7)

In our normalisation,

\mathbb{E}\left[\ell_{x}\right]\sim\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\log N

as
N\to\infty
,
for any
x
in the bulk of
D_{N}
. We then define the set of
a
-thick points by

\mathcal{T}_{N}(a):=\left\{x\in D_{N}:\ell_{x}\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}a(\log N)^{2}\right\}.

(2.8)

The parameter
a
can be thought of as a thickness parameter. It is related to the GMC parameter
\gamma
by

a=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2},\quad\quad\gamma=\sqrt{2a}.

(2.9)

We finally define the discrete approximation
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}
of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
as the uniform measure on the set of thick points: for all Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{C}
,

\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(A):=\frac{1}{c_{*}(a)}\frac{(\log N)^{1-\theta}}{N^{2-a}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{T}_{N}(a)}\CR(x,D)^{-a}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in A\right\}}.

(2.10)

where the constant
c_{*}(a)
is given by
c_{*}(a)=\frac{(2\sqrt{2})^{a}e^{a\gamma_{\text{EM}}}}{2a^{1-\theta}\Gamma(\theta)}
with
\gamma_{\text{EM}}
being the Euler–Mascheroni constant.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.12]
)
.

Let
\theta>0
and
a\in(0,2)
. The following convergence holds in distribution

(\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N})\to(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta},\mathcal{M}_{a})\quad\quad\text{as}\quad N\to\infty,

relatively to the topology induced by
d_{\mathfrak{L}}
for
\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
and the weak topology on
\mathbb{C}
for
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}
.

With some abuse of notation, we will write
\mathcal{M}_{a}
or
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
depending on whether we view the measure as a function of
a
or
\gamma
. In this paper
a
and
\gamma
will always be related by (
2.9
).

Remark 2.2
.

Let us mention that the previous results can easily be generalised to finitely connected domains. In that case, one would need to change in (
2.10
)
\log\CR(z,D)
by the harmonic extension of
\log|z-\cdot|
from
\partial D
to
D
. Indeed, this is the function that appears in the asymptotic behaviour of the Green function on the diagonal (see e.g. (1.4) in
[
BP21
]
).

Some properties

In the following lemma we recall some first and second moments computations related to
\mathcal{M}_{a}
. Let
a,a^{\prime}\in(0,1)
be two thickness parameters lying in the
L^{2}
-regime. Let
\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots\}
and
\{a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime},\dots\}
be two independent random partitions of
[0,a]
and
[0,a^{\prime}]
, respectively, distributed according to a Poisson–Dirichlet distribution with parameter
\theta
. Conditionally on these partitions, let
\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x},\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}
,
i\geq 1
, be independent loops whose distributions are described in Section
2.1
.
We also recall the definition of the modified Bessel function of the first kind

I_{\theta-1}(u)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!\Gamma(\theta+n)}\left(\frac{u}{2}\right)^{2n+\theta-1},\quad\quad\theta>0,\quad u>0.

(2.11)

Lemma 2.3
.

For
a\in(0,2)
and
F:D\times\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
a bounded measurable admissible function, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{D}F(x,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathrm{d}x)\right]=2\int_{D}\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1})\right]\mathrm{d}x

(2.12)

where the two collections of loops
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
and
\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}
are independent.
Let
a,a^{\prime}\in(0,1)
.
We have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}y)\right]=4\left(2\pi\sqrt{aa^{\prime}}G_{D}(x,y)\right)^{1-\theta}\Gamma(\theta)I_{\theta-1}\left(4\pi\sqrt{aa^{\prime}}G_{D}(x,y)\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

(2.13)

Moreover, if
F:D\times D\times\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded measurable admissible function, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\nexists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\text{~visiting~both~}x\text{~and~}y\right\}}\right]

(2.14)

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

where the three collections of loops
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
,
\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}
are independent.

Proof.

Recall that our normalisation of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is different than in
[
ABJL23
]
(see (
2.5
)).
(
2.12
) is the content of
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.8]
and
(
2.13
) can be found in
[
ABJL23
, Remark 1.3]
. It remains to prove (
2.14
). We are going to see that it is a quick consequence of (
2.12
).
Indeed, the left hand side of (
2.14
) is equal to

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1})\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\nexists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\text{~visiting~both~}x\text{~and~}y\right\}}\right]\mathrm{d}x

where
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
and
\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}
are independent. In the above display, we denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}}
the multiplicative chaos generated by the loops in
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}
. For more details see
[
ABJL23
, Section 1.3]
. Here, we notice that on the event that none of the loops in
\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}
visits
y
, none of these loops can contribute to the thickness at
y
:

\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\nexists\wp\in\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\text{~visiting~}y\right\}}=\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\nexists\wp\in\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\text{~visiting~}y\right\}}\quad\quad\text{a.s.}

Hence, the left hand side of (
2.14
) is equal to

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1})\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\nexists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\text{~visiting~both~}x\text{~and~}y\right\}}\right]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}\Big[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})\mathbf{1}_{\{\nexists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}\text{~visiting~both~}x\text{~and~}y\}}\Big]\mathrm{d}x~\mathrm{d}y

where in the last equality we applied once again
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.8]
. Finally, we notice that for Lebesgue-typical points
x
and
y
, almost surely none of the loops in
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}
visits both
x
and
y
. This concludes the proof.
∎

2.3
Crossing exponent in the Brownian loop soup

We now recall the main result of
[
JLQ23
]
concerning the decay rate of the probability of some crossing event in the loop soup. This decay rate is particularly important to us since it drives the blow-up of the correlations of
h_{\theta}
(Theorem
1.3
). The decay of the normalising constant
Z_{\gamma}
in Theorem
1.2
is also obtained from the asymptotic behaviour of these crossing probabilities.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
JLQ23
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
.
The probability that a cluster in a Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
intersects both the microscopic circle
r\partial\mathbb{D}
and the macroscopic circle
e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}
decays like

\mathbb{P}\left(r\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}\right)=|\log r|^{-1+\theta+o(1)}\quad\quad\text{as~}\quad r\to 0.

We will also need the following estimates from
[
JLQ23
]
. As opposed to Theorem
2.4
, the next results concern some crossing events by
loops
rather than
clusters
(they are in particular much easier to establish).
In the first lemma below and in the rest of the paper, we will say that a loop
\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
surrounds the disc
r\mathbb{D}
if
\wp
does not intersect
r\mathbb{D}
but disconnects it from
\partial\mathbb{D}
.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
JLQ23
, Lemma 2.5]
)
.

There exists
c=c(\theta)>0
such that for all
r\in(0,1/10)
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\mathrm{~surrounding~}r\mathbb{D}\right)\geq 1-r^{c}.

(2.15)

Lemma 2.6

(
[
JLQ23
, (2.3) in Lemma 2.2]
)
.

Let
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<1
. We have

\mu^{\rm loop}_{\mathbb{D}}(\{\wp\text{~crossing~}r_{2}\mathbb{D}\setminus r_{1}\mathbb{D}\})=\left(1+O(1)\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\right)\log\frac{\log(1/r_{1})}{\log(r_{2}/r_{1})}.

In particular, for all
r_{0}\in(0,e^{-1})
and
s\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}:r_{0}\overset{\wp}{\longleftrightarrow}r_{0}^{s}\right)=(1+O(s^{-1}))\theta s^{-1}.

We finally recall the FKG inequality which is an import tool that we will use frequently in the paper.
A function
f:\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be increasing if for all
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}
with
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
,
f(\mathcal{L})\leq f(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.7

(FKG inequality
[
Jan84
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

For all increasing bounded measurable functions
f,g:\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[f(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})g(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[f(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[g(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\right].

In most places we will apply this result to indicator functions of increasing events.

2.4
Some properties of
\Xi_{a}^{z}

We start by recalling a restriction property for the
a
-thick loop
\Xi_{a}^{z}
defined in Section
2.1
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
z\in D
and let
D^{\prime}\subset D
be a simply connected domain containing
z
. Then

\mathbb{E}\left[F(\Xi_{a}^{z,D})\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\Xi_{a}^{z,D}\subset D^{\prime}\right\}}\right]=\frac{\CR(z,D^{\prime})^{a}}{\CR(z,D)^{a}}\mathbb{E}\left[F(\Xi_{a}^{z,D^{\prime}})\right].

(2.16)

Proof.

This follows quickly from the restriction property of the measures
\mu_{D}^{z,w}
. A proof of this result can be found in
[
ABJL23
]
; see equation (5.2) therein.
∎

As a direct consequence,

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
x\in D
and
a>0
. For any
r<\mathrm{d}(x,\partial D)
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\Xi_{a}^{x}-x\right\|_{\infty}\in\mathrm{d}r\right)=\frac{ar^{a-1}}{\CR(x,D)^{a}}\mathrm{d}r.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.8
, the probability that
\left\|\Xi_{a}^{x}-x\right\|_{\infty}<r
is equal to
\frac{\CR(x,D\cap D(x,r))^{a}}{\CR(x,D)^{a}}
. When
r<\mathrm{d}(x,\partial D)
, this is simply equal to
\frac{r^{a}}{\CR(x,D)^{a}}
. The lemma then follows after differentiating with respect to
r
.
∎

We will also need the following two-point estimate:

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
x,y\in D
with
|x-y|>e^{-1/a}
and let
k\geq 0
.

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\Xi_{a}^{x}-y\right\|_{\infty}\frac{2}{|x-y|}\in[e^{-k-1},e^{-k}]\right)\leq Ca\frac{\left(\log|x-y|\right)^{2}}{k\left(k+|\log|x-y||\right)}.

(2.17)

Proof.

We will only explain where this bound comes from without providing all the details.
By Lemma
2.9
, the probability that
\Xi_{a}^{x}
reaches the circle
\partial D(x,|x-y|/2)
is given by

1-\left(\frac{|x-y|}{2\CR(x,D)}\right)^{a}.

Using the assumption that
|x-y|>e^{-1/a}
, we can bound this probability by
Ca|\log|x-y||
. Conditioned on the event that
\Xi_{a}^{x}
has reached
\partial D(x,|x-y|/2)
, the probability that
\frac{2}{|x-y|}\left\|\Xi_{a}^{x}-y\right\|_{\infty}\in[e^{-k-1},e^{-k}]
can be compared with the probability for a simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
starting at
0
to reach
k
before reaching
-\log|x-y|
(hit the small ball before exiting the domain) and then to come back to
0
without hitting
k+1
(do not hit
\partial D(y,|x-y|e^{-k+1}/2)
before going back to
\partial D(y,|x-y|/2)
).
The probabilities of these events are respectively equal to

\frac{|\log|x-y||}{k+|\log|x-y||}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{k}.

Putting things together, we obtain that (
2.17
).
∎

We finish this section by combining Theorem
2.4
and Lemma
2.9
to show that, as stated in Theorem
1.2
, the normalising constant
Z_{\gamma}
(
1.3
) behaves like
\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
.
Importantly, a slight modification of the proof below shows that for any
z\in D
and compact set
K\subset D\setminus\{z\}
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z}}{\longleftrightarrow}z\Big)=(1+o(1))\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(z,\left\|\Xi_{a}^{z}-z\right\|_{\infty})\Big),

(2.18)

i.e. we can replace the thick loop at
z
by a disc centred at
z
with random radius
\left\|\Xi_{a}^{z}-z\right\|_{\infty}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
– asymptotic behaviour of
Z_{\gamma}
.

We are going to show that
Z_{\gamma}=\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
as
\gamma\to 0
.
We start with the upper bound.
If the origin is connected to the circle
e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}
by a cluster of
\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}\cup\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
, there must be a cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
which intersects both
r\partial\mathbb{D}
and
e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}
where
r=\left\|\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}\right\|_{\infty}
.
By independence of
\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
this gives

\displaystyle Z_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}\right\|_{\infty}\geq e^{-1}\right)+\int_{0}^{e^{-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}\right\|_{\infty}\in\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathbb{P}\left(r\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}\right).

Let
\varepsilon>0
. By Theorem
2.4
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that the last probability in the above display is at most
C_{\varepsilon}|\log r|^{-1+\theta+\varepsilon}
for all
r\in(0,e^{-1})
. Together with Lemma
2.9
, we obtain that

Z_{\gamma}\leq 1-e^{-a}+\int_{0}^{e^{-1}}ar^{a-1}C_{\varepsilon}|\log r|^{-1+\theta+\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}r\sim C_{\varepsilon}\Gamma(\theta+\varepsilon)a^{1-\theta-\varepsilon}

as
a\to 0
. Since
a=\gamma^{2}/2
, this proves the desired upper bound:
Z_{\gamma}\leq\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
.

We now turn to the lower bound. The event
\{0\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}\}
occurs as soon as
1)
\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}
reaches
e^{-1/a}\partial\mathbb{D}
,
2) a cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
intersects the circles
e^{-2/a}\partial\mathbb{D}
and
e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}
and
3) there is a loop in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
surrounding
e^{-2/a}\mathbb{D}
while staying in
e^{-1/a}\mathbb{D}
.
By independence of
\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
and by FKG, the probability of the intersection of these three events is at least the product of each probability. By Lemma
2.9
, the probability of the first event is
1-e^{-1}
. By Theorem
2.4
, the probability of the second event is
a^{1-\theta+o(1)}
. By Lemma
2.5
, the probability of the last event is
1+o(1)
. Overall, we conclude that
Z_{\gamma}\geq a^{1-\theta+o(1)}=\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
.
∎

2.5
Preliminary estimate

We finish this preliminary section with a lemma that we state here for ease of future reference.

Lemma 2.11
.

For
\gamma>0
, let

R_{\gamma}:=\sup\{r>0:r\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}0\}.

(2.19)

Then for all
\eta>0
, there exists
C=C(\eta)>0
such that for all
\gamma>0
and
r\in(0,1)
,

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)\leq C|1+\log r|^{1-\theta+\eta}.

(2.20)

Moreover,

\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)\to 0\quad\quad\text{as}\quad r\to 1.

(2.21)

Proof.

By definition,
Z_{\gamma}=\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\right)
. See (
1.3
). Hence,
\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)/Z_{\gamma}\leq 1
if
r\geq e^{-1}
which concludes the proof of (
2.20
) in this case. We now consider the case
r\in(0,e^{-1}).
This time,

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}|R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)^{-1}.

Let
E_{1}:=\{e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}r/2\partial\mathbb{D}\}
and
E_{2}
be the event that there is a loop in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
surrounding
r/2\mathbb{D}
while staying in
r\mathbb{D}
.
Conditionally on
\{R_{\gamma}\geq r\}
,
\{R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\}\supset E_{1}\cap E_{2}
. By FKG inequality, we deduce that

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(E_{1}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(E_{2}\right)^{-1}.

By Lemma
2.5
,
\mathbb{P}\left(E_{2}\right)\geq c
and, by Theorem
2.4
, for all
\eta>0
, there exists
c>0
such that
\mathbb{P}\left(E_{1}\right)\geq c|\log r|^{-1+\theta-\eta}
. This concludes the proof of (
2.20
).

We now prove (
2.21
). Let
r\in(e^{-1},1)
.
FKG inequality cannot be used directly to bound
\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)/Z_{\gamma}=\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r|R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\right)
(it goes in the wrong direction). Let
E
be the event that
\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}\subset e^{-1}\mathbb{D}
. By Lemma
2.9
,
\mathbb{P}\left(E^{c}\right)\leq C\gamma^{2}
. Hence

\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(E^{c},R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)=0.

We now work on the event
E
. Let
r^{\prime}\in(e^{-1},r)
be an intermediate radius.
We decompose the loops of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
into two independent sets
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
of loops: the ones that are contained in
\mathbb{D}\setminus e^{-1}\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and the ones that touch
e^{-1}\overline{\mathbb{D}}
respectively.
Notice that the event
\{R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\}
is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}_{2}
. If none of the loops in
\mathcal{L}_{2}
reaches
r^{\prime}\partial\mathbb{D}
, then there must be a cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
that intersects both
r^{\prime}\partial\mathbb{D}
and
r\partial\mathbb{D}
in order to have
R_{\gamma}\geq r
. That is,

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(E,R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(r\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\longleftrightarrow}r^{\prime}\partial\mathbb{D}|R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(E,\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{2}:r^{\prime}\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\wp}{\longleftrightarrow}e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}|R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\right).

Since
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is independent of
\{R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\}
, the first probability on the right hand side is equal to
\mathbb{P}\left(r\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\longleftrightarrow}r^{\prime}\partial\mathbb{D}\right)
.
r^{\prime}
being fixed, this event can occur only if a macroscopic CLE loop (the outermost boundary of a cluster) in the domain
\mathbb{D}\setminus e^{-1}\overline{\mathbb{D}}
reaches the circle
r\partial\mathbb{D}
. This probability vanishes as
r\to 1
(this follows from
[
SW12
, Corollaries 4.3 and 6.6]
where the exponent of the probability that a macroscopic CLE loop gets close to a fixed boundary point is computed).
We have shown that for all
r^{\prime}\in(e^{-1},1)
,

\limsup_{r\to 1}\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq r\right)\leq\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{2}:r^{\prime}\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\wp}{\longleftrightarrow}e^{-1}\partial\mathbb{D}|R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\right).

(2.22)

Since the left hand side is independent of
r^{\prime}
, it is enough to show that the right hand side vanishes as
r^{\prime}\to 1
to conclude the proof of (
2.21
).
Let
R:=\inf\{x>0:\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{2}:r^{\prime}\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\wp}{\longleftrightarrow}x\partial\mathbb{D}\}
. The probability on the right hand side of (
2.22
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{e^{-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(R\in\mathrm{d}x,R_{\gamma}\geq e^{-1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{e^{-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(R\in\mathrm{d}x\right)\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq x\right)

using the independence of the loops that touch
x\mathbb{D}
and the ones that do not.
By (
2.20
) (applied to
\eta=\theta/2
),
\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq x\right)/Z_{\gamma}\leq C|\log x|^{1-\theta/2}
. Injecting this estimate in the above integral and then integrating by parts shows that the right hand side of (
2.22
) is at most

\mathbb{P}\left(R\leq e^{-1}\right)+(1-\theta/2)\int_{0}^{e^{-1}}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(R\leq x\right)}{x|\log x|^{\theta/2}}\mathrm{d}x.

By Lemma
2.6
,
\mathbb{P}\left(R\leq x\right)\leq C|\log r^{\prime}|/|\log x|,x\in(0,e^{-1}]
. The integral
\int_{0}^{e^{-1}}x^{-1}|\log x|^{-1-\theta/2}\mathrm{d}x
being finite, the right hand side of (
2.22
) is thus at most
C|\log r^{\prime}|
which vanishes as
r^{\prime}\to 1
. This concludes the proof of (
2.21
).
∎

3
Convergence of renormalised crossing probabilities and coupling of clusters

The main goal of this section is to prove the following result. This will be a key result for the rest of the article.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
and
D
be a bounded simply connected domain. Let
x\in D
and
K
and
\hat{K}
be two connected compact subsets of
\overline{D}\setminus\{x\}
. The following limits exist and are identical

\lim_{r\to 0}\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(x,r)\Big)\Big/\mathbb{P}\Big(\hat{K}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(x,r)\Big)=\lim_{a\to 0}\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x,D}}{\longleftrightarrow}x\Big)\Big/\mathbb{P}\Big(\hat{K}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x,D}}{\longleftrightarrow}x\Big).

(3.1)

The proof of this result is based on the construction of a coupling between two processes of conditioned clusters. Let us describe this result now.

For
t\geq 0
, let
\mathtt{C}_{t}
be the open cylinder
\mathtt{C}_{t}=(t,+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{t}
the corresponding closed cylinder,
and
\partial\mathtt{C}_{t}
the circle

\partial\mathtt{C}_{t}=\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{t}
.
The cylinder
\mathtt{C}_{0}
is conformally equivalent to

\mathbb{D}\setminus\{0\}
through the map

(t,\omega)\mapsto e^{-t}\omega
.
Since the 2D Brownian loop soups are conformally invariant in law (up to time change), and
\{0\}
is polar for Brownian motion,
working on
\mathtt{C}_{0}
and working on
\mathbb{D}
(and therefore on any simply connected domain
D
) will be equivalent.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
and consider a Brownian loop soup with intensity
\theta
in
\mathtt{C}_{0}
that we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}
. For
t>0
, let
\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t]}^{\theta}
be the subset of
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}
consisting of all the loops that intersect
(0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Let
K_{0}\subset\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}
be a connected compact subset of
\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}
. For all integer
t\geq 1
, let
Q_{0}(t)
be the union of all clusters of
\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t]}^{\theta}
that intersect
K_{0}
. Let
K_{0}(t):=((\overline{Q_{0}(t)}\cap\mathtt{C}_{t})-t)\cup\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
(for
A\subset\mathtt{C}_{0}
,
A-t
stands for the longitudinal translation of
A
by
t
, to the left)
and
T^{\dagger}:=\inf\{t\in\mathbb{N}:K_{0}(t)=\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}\}
. The event
\{T^{\dagger}>t\}
corresponds to the event that there is a cluster intersecting both
K_{0}
and
\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Let
\hat{K}_{0}
be another initial connected compact subset of
\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}
and define analogously
(\hat{K}_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\hat{T}^{\dagger}
.
We would like to stress that
(K_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
is
not
a Markov process; see Figure
3.1
. For this reason, we will need to consider a larger filtration than the natural filtration associated to
(K_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
; this filtration is introduced in (
3.10
) and will be denoted by
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Figure 3.1:
Schematic representation of the sequence
(K_{0}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
. The filled grey region is the initial set
K_{0}
. The clusters of
