\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t_{1}]}^{\theta}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t_{2}]}^{\theta}\setminus\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t_{1}]}^{\theta}
) are depicted in red (resp. blue).
Q_{0}(t_{1})\cap\mathtt{C}_{t_{1}}
and
Q_{0}(t_{2})\cap\mathtt{C}_{t_{2}}
correspond to the dotted red and blue regions respectively.
Q_{0}(t_{2})
is
not
measurable w.r.t.
Q_{0}(t_{1})
and the loops in
\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t_{2}]}^{\theta}\setminus\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t_{1}]}^{\theta}
since it also depends on the other clusters of
\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t_{1}]}^{\theta}
(bottom red cluster in this picture).

Our coupling result reads as follows:

Theorem 3.2
.

For every initial conditions
K_{0}
and
\hat{K}_{0}
not identical to
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
, and every
T\geq 1
large enough, we can couple on the same probability space the two stochastic processes
(K_{0}(t))_{t=0,\dots,T}
under
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|T^{\dagger}>T)
and
(\hat{K}_{0}(t))_{t=0,\dots,T}
under
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)
such that there exists a stopping time
\mathcal{T}^{T}
w.r.t. the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
such that the following holds:

1.

Coalescence:
On the event
\{\mathcal{T}^{T}<T\}
,
K_{0}(t)=\hat{K}_{0}(t)
for all
t\in\{\mathcal{T}^{T},\dots,T\}
;

2.

In finite time:
Moreover
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{T\geq n}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{T}^{T}\geq n\right)=0
.

This result will be crucial in the proof of the convergence of the ratio
\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T)/\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)
as
T\to\infty
(see Theorem
3.1
). Indeed, thanks to the above coupling, as soon as the two processes
(K_{0}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
and
(\hat{K}_{0}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
have coalesced, surviving up to time
T
will have the same cost. The cost will be different only before the coalescence time that we know being finite almost surely. Making this argument rigorous requires some extra work (especially on the event that the coalescence time is large), but most of the difficulty lies in Theorem
3.2
above. See Section
3.5
for details.

To prove Theorem
3.2
, we will develop a general abstract result that produces a coupling between two trajectories of the same Markov chain with distinct starting points. In this coupling the two trajectories coalesce in finite time. See Section
3.2
and Theorem
3.3
.

As already mentioned,
(K_{0}(t))_{t\geq 1}
is not Markovian. In Section
3.3
, we introduce an enlarged process of clusters (actually a collection of clusters) that is Markovian. In Section
3.4
, we show that we can apply Theorem
3.3
to the framework described in Section
3.3
, concluding the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

We will start in the next section by recalling some facts about the coupling of two random variables.

3.1
Total variation and optimal coupling

Let
(\mathcal{E},d)
be a Polish space, that is to say a separable and complete metric space, endowed with its Borel sigma-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
generated by the open subsets.
Given two probability measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
on

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E}))
,
the total variation distance
d_{\rm TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

is given by

d_{\rm TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=2\sup\{|\mu_{2}(A)-\mu_{1}(A)|~:A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})\}\leq 2.

This is also the total mass of total variation measure corresponding to the signed measure
\mu_{2}-\mu_{1}
.
According to Lebesgue’s decomposition theorem,
there are measures
\nu
,
\nu_{1}
,
\nu_{2}
,
two by two mutually singular, and non-negative measurable functions
f_{1}
,

f_{2}
, such that

\mu_{1}=f_{1}\nu+\nu_{1}

and

\mu_{2}=f_{2}\nu+\nu_{2}.

One can also impose that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are
0

\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}
almost everywhere,
and this is the convention we will use.
Then

d_{\rm TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int_{E}|f_{2}-f_{1}|d\nu+\nu_{1}(\mathcal{E})+\nu_{2}(\mathcal{E})\quad\text{and}\quad 1-\dfrac{1}{2}d_{\rm TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\int_{E}f_{1}\wedge f_{2}\,d\nu=:q.

(3.2)

We are interested in the couplings
(X_{1},X_{2})
,
where the marginal distribution of
X_{i}
is
\mu_{i}
,

i=1,2
,
so as to maximize
\mathbb{P}(X_{1}=X_{2})
.
This maximal probability is actually given by
q
defined in (
3.2
) above.
An optimal coupling can be constructed as follows.
Let
Y
,
Y_{i},i=1,2,
be three independent r.v.s taking values in
\mathcal{E}
distributed respectively according to

\dfrac{1}{q}f_{1}\wedge f_{2}\,d\nu\quad\text{and}\quad\dfrac{1}{1-q}\big((f_{i}-f_{1}\wedge f_{2})d\nu+d\nu_{i}\big),\quad i=1,2.

Moreover, let
Z
be a Bernouilli r.v. independent from

(Y,Y_{1},Y_{2})
, with
\mathbb{P}(Z=1)=q
.
On the event
\{Z=1\}
, we set

X_{1}=X_{2}=Y
.
On the event
\{Z=0\}
, we set

X_{1}=Y_{1}
and
X_{2}=Y_{2}
.
It is easy to check that each
X_{i}
,
i=1,2
,
has for distribution
\mu_{i}
.
Note that in this construction,

\mathbb{P}(X_{2}=X_{1}|X_{1})=(f_{2}(X_{1})/f_{1}(X_{1}))\wedge 1.

Moreover, conditionally on the value of
X_{1}
and the event

\{X_{2}\neq X_{1}\}
,
the conditional distribution of
X_{2}
is that of
Y_{2}
.

3.2
Coupling Markov processes conditioned to survive

Let
(\mathcal{E},d)
be a Polish space, endowed with its Borel sigma-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
generated by the open subsets.
Let
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a time-homogeneous Markov semi-group on
\mathcal{E}
.
That is to say, for every
n\geq 1
and
x\in\mathcal{E}
,

P_{n}(x,dy)
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{E}
and the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation holds: for every
x\in\mathcal{E}
and
n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1
,

\int_{y\in\mathcal{E}}P_{n_{1}}(x,dy)P_{n_{2}}(y,dz)=P_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(x,dz).

For
x\in\mathcal{E}
, let
(\xi^{x}(n))_{n\geq 0}
denote a Markov chain
starting from
x
, with transition semi-group
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.

Let
\dagger
be a measurable subset of
\mathcal{E}
,
with
\dagger\neq\mathcal{E}
.
We will denote
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}=\mathcal{E}\setminus\dagger
.
For
x\in\mathcal{E}
, let

T_{\dagger}^{x}=\inf\{n\geq 0:\xi^{x}(n)\in\dagger\}

be the first hitting time of
\dagger
.
We assume that for every
x\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
and

T\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,

\mathbb{P}(T_{\dagger}^{x}>T)>0
.
We will denote by
(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{n\geq 0}
the trajectory

(\xi^{x}(n))_{n\geq 0}
conditioned on the event

\{T_{\dagger}^{x}>T\}
.
Let
P^{T}_{n}(x,dy)
denote the law of

\xi^{x,T}(n)
.
We have a time-inhomogeneous Markov property:
for every
x\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
,

n_{1},n_{2}\geq 1
and
T\geq n_{1}+n_{2}
,

P^{T}_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(x,dz)=\int_{y\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}}P^{T}_{n_{1}}(x,dy)P^{T-n_{1}}_{n_{2}}(y,dz).

Given
x,y\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
, and
T\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
we will denote by
(\xi^{x,y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

the process
(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

conditioned on
\xi^{x,T}(T)=y

(Markovian bridge conditioned on not hitting
\dagger
).
This conditional distribution is defined for

P^{T}_{T}(x,dy)
almost every
y
.
For this, we refer to the existence of regular conditional distributions on Polish spaces
[
KS10
, Section 5.3.C, Theorem 3.19]
.
Further, for every
T^{\prime}\geq T
,
conditionally on
\xi^{x,T^{\prime}}(T)=y
,
the two processes
(\xi^{x,T^{\prime}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

and
(\xi^{x,T^{\prime}}(T+n))_{n\geq 0}

are independent, and distributed respectively as

(\xi^{x,y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
and

(\xi^{y,T^{\prime}-T}(n))_{n\geq 0}
.

We are interested in coupling the conditioned processes

(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
and

(\xi^{y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
,
for two distinct starting points
x\neq y\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
,
on the same probability space,
such that the two processes coalesce with high probability for

T
large enough, and with a uniform control in
T
over the coalescence time.
In our approach, we will need the following data:

•

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
, a non-empty measurable subset

\mathtt{F}_{n}
of
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
.
This will be the subset of favourable configurations (favourable to attempt a
coalescence).

•

A measurable function

\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{E}^{\ast}\mapsto\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
The value
\mathbf{T}(x)
is a sort of characteristic time
that is roughly proportional to the time that the process
\xi^{x,T}
needs in order to reach a favourable configuration.
We will also use the following notation:

\mathbf{T}(x,y):=\mathbf{T}(x)\vee\mathbf{T}(y)
.

Theorem 3.3
.

With the notations above, we assume that
there are integer constants
c_{2}>c_{1}\geq 2
, such that the following holds.

1.

Control of coalescence starting from favourable configurations.
We have that

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}\\
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathtt{F}_{n}\end{subarray}}\inf_{T\geq c_{2}n}\Big(1-\frac{1}{2}d_{\rm TV}(P^{T-n}_{(c_{1}-1)n}(x^{\prime},dz),P^{T-n}_{(c_{1}-1)n}(y^{\prime},dz))\Big)>0.

(3.3)

2.

Reaching the favourable set
\mathtt{F}_{n}
.
We have that

\inf_{x\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}}\inf_{n\geq\mathbf{T}(x)}\inf_{T\geq c_{2}n}\mathbb{P}\big(\xi^{x,T}(n)\in\mathtt{F}_{n}\big)>0.

(3.4)

3.

Control on the characteristic time.
We have that

\lim_{u\to+\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}}\sup_{n\geq\mathbf{T}(x)}\sup_{T\geq c_{2}n}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{T}(\xi^{x,T}(c_{1}n))\geq un)=0.

(3.5)

Then, for every
x,y\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
and
every
T\geq c_{2}\mathbf{T}(x,y)
,
one can couple on the same probability space the stochastic processes

(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
and

(\xi^{y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
, such
that there exists a stopping time

\mathcal{T}^{T}\in\{0,1,\dots,T\}

with respect to the joint process

(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

such that the following holds.

1.

On the event
\{\mathcal{T}^{T}<T\}
,
for every
n\in\{\mathcal{T}^{T},\dots,T\}
,

\xi^{x,T}(n)=\xi^{y,T}(n)
a.s.

2.

On the event
\{\mathcal{T}^{T}<T\}
,
conditionally on

(\mathcal{T}^{T},(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq\mathcal{T}^{T}})
,
the processes

(\xi^{x,T}(\mathcal{T}^{T}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T-\mathcal{T}^{T}}
and
(\xi^{y,T}(\mathcal{T}^{T}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T-\mathcal{T}^{T}}
coincide a.s and are distributed as

(\xi^{z,T^{\prime}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T^{\prime}}
,
with
z=\xi^{x,T}(\mathcal{T}^{T})

and
T^{\prime}=T-\mathcal{T}^{T}
.

3.

We have that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{T>n}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}^{T}>n)=0.

(3.6)

In particular,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{T\geq n}d_{\rm TV}(P^{T}_{n}(x,dz),P^{T}_{n}(y,dz))=0.

(3.7)

Proof.

Denote by
q
the infimum (
3.3
), and
p
the infimum (
3.4
).
Fix
x,y\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
and an integer

T\geq c_{2}\mathbf{T}(x,y)
.
Let
(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
be a conditioned Markov trajectory
starting from
x
.
We will construct by induction a sequence of processes

(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T,i\geq 1}
,
two random sequences of points
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
(y_{i})_{i\geq 1}

in
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
, and a non-increasing sequence of random non-negative integer times
(T_{i})_{i\geq 1}
. We will also denote by
\tau_{i}=\mathbf{T}(x_{i},y_{i}),i\geq 1
.
We initiate these sequences by setting
x_{1}=x
,
y_{1}=y
and
T_{1}=T
.
If
y=x
,
we set
x_{i}=x
,
y_{i}=y
,
T_{i}=T
and

(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}=(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

for every
i\geq 1
.
If
y\neq x
, we take
(\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

distributed as
(\xi^{y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

and independent from
(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.
We will explain in the next few paragraphs how our inductive definition works.

If
x_{i}\neq y_{i}
and
c_{2}\tau_{i}\leq T_{i}
,
we will set
T_{i+1}=T_{i}-c_{1}\tau_{i}
.
Whenever
x_{i}=y_{i}
or
c_{2}\tau_{i}>T_{i}
,
we will set
x_{j}=x_{i}
,
y_{j}=y_{i}
,
T_{j}=T_{i}

and
(\xi^{y,T}_{j}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}=(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

for every
j\geq i
.
We will set

i^{\ast}=\inf\{i\geq 1:x_{i}=y_{i}\text{ or }c_{2}\tau_{i}>T_{i}\}.

We will see that
i^{\ast}<+\infty
a.s.

We now consider the case where

y\neq x
and we explain our definition of
x_{2}
,
y_{2}
,
T_{2}
and
(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.
We set
T_{2}=T-c_{1}\tau_{1}
.
Denote by
F_{1}
the event

\{\xi^{x,T}(\tau_{1})\in\mathtt{F}_{\tau_{1}}\text{ and }\xi^{y,T}_{1}(\tau_{1})\in\mathtt{F}_{\tau_{1}}\}
,
and by
F_{1}^{\rm c}
its complementary.
We have that

\mathbb{P}(F_{1})\geq p^{2}>0.

On the event
F_{1}^{\rm c}
,
then we set
\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n)=\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n)
for every
n\in\{0,1,\dots,T\}
.
We set
x_{2}=\xi^{x,T}(c_{1}\tau_{1})

and
y_{2}=\xi^{y,T}_{1}(c_{1}\tau_{1})
.
On the event
F_{1}
,
we set
x_{2}=\xi^{x,T}(c_{1}\tau_{1})
.
Let
x_{1}^{\prime}=\xi^{x,T}(\tau_{1})

and
y_{1}^{\prime}=\xi^{y,T}_{1}(\tau_{1})
.
We consider the Lebesgue decomposition of the probability measure

P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(y_{1}^{\prime},dz)
with respect to

P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},dz)
:

P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(y_{1}^{\prime},dz)=f_{1}(z)P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},dz)+\nu_{1}(dz),

where the measure
\nu_{1}
is singular with respect to

P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},dz)
.
Let
Z_{1}
be a Bernoulli random variable,
conditionally independent from

(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y_{1},T}(t))_{0\leq n\leq T}

given
(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})
, with

\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}=1|x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})=f_{1}(x_{2})\wedge 1.

Let
V_{1}
be a random variable on
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
,
conditionally independent from

(Z_{1},(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y_{1},T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T})

given
(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})
, with
the conditional distribution given
(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})

equal to

\dfrac{1}{1-q_{1}}\big((f_{1}(z)-f_{1}(z)\wedge 1)P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},dz)+\nu_{1}(dz)\big),

where
1-q_{1}
is the normalization constant.
On the event
F_{1}\cap\{Z_{1}=1\}
,
we further set
y_{2}=x_{2}
.
On the event
F_{1}\cap\{Z_{1}=0\}
, we set
y_{2}=V_{1}
.
In this way,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(y_{2}=x_{2}\big|(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq\tau_{1}},F_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=1-\dfrac{1}{2}d_{\rm TV}(P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime},dz),P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(y_{1}^{\prime},dz))=q_{1}\geq q>0,

and the conditional distribution of
y_{2}
given

(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq\tau_{1}}

on the event
F_{1}
is

P^{T-\tau_{1}}_{(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(y_{1}^{\prime},dz)
;
see Section
3.1
.
In this way,
the distribution of

((\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq\tau_{1}},y_{2})

conditionally on the event
F_{1}

coincides with the conditional distribution of

((\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq\tau_{1}},\xi^{y,T}_{1}(c_{1}\tau_{1}))

given the same event.
Consider a conditioned Markov process

(\xi^{y_{2},T-c_{1}\tau_{1}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T-c_{1}\tau_{1}}

conditionally independent from

((\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T},Z_{1},V_{1})

given
y_{2}
.
Consider also a Markovian bridge conditioned on not hitting
\dagger
,

(\xi^{y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2},(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}
,
with the bridge being conditionally independent from

((\xi^{x,T}(t),\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T},Z_{1},V_{1},(\xi^{y_{2},T-c_{1}\tau_{1}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T-c_{1}\tau_{1}})

given
(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2})
.
On the event
F_{1}\cap\{Z_{1}=1\}
, we define

(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
as follows:

\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n)&\text{for }n\in\{0,\dots,\tau_{1}\},\\
\xi^{y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2},(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(n-\tau_{1})&\text{for }n\in\{\tau_{1}+1,\dots,c_{1}\tau_{1}-1\},\\
\xi^{x,T}(n)&\text{for }n\in\{c_{1}\tau_{1},\dots,T\}.\end{array}\right.

On the event
F_{1}\cap\{Z_{1}=0\}
, we define

(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
as follows:

\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n)&\text{for }n\in\{0,\dots,\tau_{1}\},\\
\xi^{y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2},(c_{1}-1)\tau_{1}}(n-\tau_{1})&\text{for }n\in\{\tau_{1}+1,\dots,c_{1}\tau_{1}-1\},\\
\xi^{y_{2},T-c_{1}\tau_{1}}(n-c_{1}\tau_{1})&\text{for }n\in\{c_{1}\tau_{1},\dots,T\}.\end{array}\right.

The process
(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
has been constructed in such a way that it has the same distribution as
(\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.
But now
(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
is correlated to

(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.
What we have gained is that the two process
(\xi^{x,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
and

(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
have a positive probability to coalesce.
More precisely,

\mathbb{P}(\forall n\in\{c_{1}\tau_{1},\dots,T\},\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n)=\xi^{x,T}(n))=\mathbb{P}(F_{1}\cap\{Z_{1}=1\})\geq p^{2}q>0.

Consider now the r.v.
\tau_{2}=\mathbf{T}(x_{2},y_{2})
.
The condition (
3.5
) provides a control on the tail of

\tau_{2}/\tau_{1}
: for all
u>0
,
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{2}\geq u\tau_{1})
is at most

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{T}(x_{2})\geq u\tau_{1})+\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{T}(y_{2})\geq u\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{T}(\xi^{x,T}(c_{1}\tau_{1}))\geq u\tau_{1})+\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{T}(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(c_{1}\tau_{1}))\geq u\tau_{1})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{z\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}}\sup_{n\geq\mathbf{T}(z)}\sup_{T^{\prime}\geq c_{2}n}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{T}(\xi^{z,T^{\prime}}(c_{1}n))\geq un).

Let

\psi:u\in[0,\infty)\mapsto 1\wedge 2\sup_{z\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}}\sup_{n\geq\mathbf{T}(z)}\sup_{T^{\prime}\geq c_{2}n}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{T}(\xi^{z,T^{\prime}}(c_{1}n))\geq un).

The function
\psi
is non-increasing, but not necessarily left-continuous.
We will denote by
\psi(u^{-})
the limit to the left of
\psi(u)
.
Let
U_{2}
be a r.v. in
[0+\infty)
with distribution
characterised by

\mathbb{P}(U_{2}\geq u)=\psi(u^{-}).

(3.8)

The condition (
3.5
) ensures that
U_{2}<+\infty
.
The r.v.
\tau_{2}
is stochastically dominated by

U_{2}\tau_{1}
.
Note however that conditionally on
y_{2}\neq x_{2}
,
the r.v.
\tau_{2}
is not necessarily stochastically dominated by

U_{2}\tau_{1}
.

On the event
\{x_{2}=y_{2}\}
, we have
i^{\ast}=2
,
and for every
i\geq 3
,

x_{i}=x_{2}=y_{i}=y_{2}
,
T_{i}=T_{2}
,
and

(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}=(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.
On the event
\{x_{2}\neq y_{2}\}
,
conditionally on

((\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{2}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T-T_{2}},(\xi^{y,T}_{1}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}),

the processes

(\xi^{x,T}(T-T_{2}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{2}}

and
(\xi^{y,T}_{2}(T-T_{2}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{2}}

are independent,
distributed as

(\xi^{x_{2},T_{2}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{2}}
and

(\xi^{y_{2},T_{2}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{2}}
respectively.
Thus, we can iterate by applying the previous construction
to
(\xi^{x,T}(T-T_{2}+n),\xi^{y,T}_{2}(T-T_{2}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{2}}
.

By iterating the construction further, we get the sequences

(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
,
(y_{i})_{i\geq 1}
,

(T_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T,i\geq 1}

satisfying the following properties:

•

For every
i\geq 1
,
the process
(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}

is distributed as the conditioned Markov process

(\xi^{y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.

•

For every
i\geq 1
,

x_{i}=\xi^{x,T}(T-T_{i})
and

y_{i}=\xi^{y,T}_{i}(T-T_{i})
.

•

For every
i\geq 2
,
a.s. for every
n\in\{0,\dots,T-T_{i-1}\}
,

\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n)=\xi^{y,T}_{i-1}(n)
.

•

For every
i\geq 1
,
on the event
\{x_{i}=y_{i}\}
,
we have that a.s. for every
n\in\{T-T_{i},\dots,T\}
,

\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n)=\xi^{x,T}(n)
,
and for every
j\geq i
,
T_{j}=T_{i}
,

x_{j}=y_{j}=x_{i}=y_{i}
,
and
(\xi^{y,T}_{j}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}=(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.

•

For every
i\geq 1
,
on the event
\{c_{2}\tau_{i}>T_{i}\}

(where
\tau_{i}=\mathbf{T}(x_{i},y_{i})
),
we have for every
j\geq i
,
T_{j}=T_{i}
,

x_{j}=x_{i}
,
y_{j}=y_{i}
,
and
(\xi^{y,T}_{j}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}=(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.

•

For every
i\geq 1
,
on the event
\{x_{i}=y_{i}\text{ and }c_{2}\tau_{i-1}\leq T_{i-1}\}
,
conditionally on

((\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T-T_{i}},(\xi^{y,T}_{j}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T,1\leq j\leq i-1})
,
the process

(\xi^{x,T}(T-T_{i}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{i}}

is distributed as the conditioned Markov processes

(\xi^{x_{i},T_{i}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{i}}
.

•

For every
i\geq 2
,
on the event
\{x_{i}\neq y_{i}\text{ and }c_{2}\tau_{i-1}\leq T_{i-1}\}
,
conditionally on

((\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T-T_{i}},(\xi^{y,T}_{j}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T,1\leq j\leq i-1})
,
the processes

(\xi^{x,T}(T-T_{i}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{i}}

and
(\xi^{y,T}_{i}(T-T_{i}+n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{i}}

are independent,
distributed as the conditioned Markov processes

(\xi^{x_{i},T_{i}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{i}}
and

(\xi^{y_{i},T_{i}}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T_{i}}
respectively.

•

For every
i\geq 1
,
on the event
\{x_{i}\neq y_{i}\text{ and }c_{2}\tau_{i}\leq T_{i}\}

(i.e.
\{i^{\ast}>i\}
),

T_{i+1}=T_{i}-c_{1}\tau_{i}
.

•

For every
i\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}\big(x_{i+1}=y_{i+1}\big|i^{\ast}>i,(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{i}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T-T_{i}},(\xi^{y,T}_{j}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T,1\leq j\leq i-1}\big)\geq p^{2}q.

From the above properties one can derive the following.
First of all, for every
i\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}(i^{\ast}>i)\leq(1-p^{2}q)^{i-1},

and in particular,
i^{\ast}<+\infty
a.s.
Further, for every
i\geq 2
,
the r.v.
T-T_{i}
is stochastically dominated by

c_{1}(1+U_{2}+U_{2}U_{3}+\dots+U_{2}U_{3}\dots U_{i-1})\tau_{1},

where the r.v.s
U_{j}
,
j\geq 3
, are i.i.d. copies of the r.v.
U_{2}
(
3.8
).
We will denote

\widehat{U}_{i}=1+U_{2}+U_{2}U_{3}+\dots+U_{2}U_{3}\dots U_{i},

with
\widehat{U}_{1}=1
,
so that
T-T_{i}
is stochastically dominated by

c_{1}\widehat{U}_{i-1}\tau_{1}
.
Note that this stochastic domination is uniform in

T\geq c_{2}\tau_{1}
.

Then, for every
i\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}(i^{\ast}>i,y_{i}\neq x_{i})\leq\mathbb{P}(i^{\ast}>i)\leq(1-p^{2}q)^{i-1}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(i^{\ast}\leq i,y_{i}\neq x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(c_{2}\tau_{i}>T_{i})=\mathbb{P}(T-T_{i}+c_{2}\tau_{i}>T)\leq\mathbb{P}((c_{1}\widehat{U}_{i-1}+c_{2}(\widehat{U}_{i}-\widehat{U}_{i-1}))\tau_{1}>T).

In this way,

\mathbb{P}(y_{i}\neq x_{i})\leq(1-p^{2}q)^{i-1}+\mathbb{P}((c_{1}\widehat{U}_{i-1}+c_{2}(\widehat{U}_{i}-\widehat{U}_{i-1}))\tau_{1}>T).

Let
I(T)
be a
deterministic
integer-valued function such that

\lim_{T\to+\infty}I(T)=+\infty
and

\lim_{T\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}((c_{1}\widehat{U}_{I(T)-1}+c_{2}(\widehat{U}_{I(T)}-\widehat{U}_{I(T)-1}))\tau_{1}>T)=0,

so that

\lim_{T\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}(y_{I(T)}=x_{I(T)})=1.

On the event
\{y_{I(T)}=x_{I(T)}\}

we necessarily have
i^{\ast}\leq I(T)
, and we will set

\mathcal{T}^{T}=T-T_{i^{\ast}}
.
On the event
\{y_{I(T)}\neq x_{I(T)}\}
, we will set
\mathcal{T}^{T}=T
.
Then we take the coupling

(\xi^{x,T}(n),\xi^{y,T}_{I(T)}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.
Since
I(T)
is deterministic, the process

(\xi^{y,T}_{I(T)}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
is distributed as the conditioned
Markov process
(\xi^{y,T}(n))_{0\leq n\leq T}
.
Moreover, on the event
\{\mathcal{T}^{T}<T\}
,
the processes
\xi^{x,T}(n)
and
\xi^{y,T}_{I(T)}(n)

coincide on
\{\mathcal{T}^{T},\dots,T\}
, as desired.
Finally, to bound the tail of the distribution
\mathcal{T}^{T}
,
we use the following.

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}^{T}>n)\leq\mathbf{1}_{\left\{T>n\right\}}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}^{T}=T)+\mathbb{P}(i^{\ast}>i)+\mathbb{P}(T-T_{i}>n))\\
\leq\mathbf{1}_{\left\{T>n\right\}}(\mathbb{P}(y_{I(T)}\neq x_{I(T)})+(1-p^{2}q)^{i-1}+\mathbb{P}(c_{1}\widehat{U}_{i-1}\tau_{1}>n)),

where
i
is arbitrary.
By taking
i=I(n)
, we get (
3.6
).

For (
3.7
), we use that

d_{\rm TV}(P^{T}_{n}(x,dz),P^{T}_{n}(y,dz))\leq 2(1-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}^{T}<T,\mathcal{T}^{T}\leq n)).

This concludes the proof.
∎

3.3
Markov chain of clusters in a Brownian loop soup

We now describe precisely the Markov chain we will consider in our Brownian loop soup setting. We start by introducing the relevant spaces. Recall that we denote by
\mathtt{C}_{0}
the open cylinder
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

The space
(\mathcal{Q},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}})
.

Denote

\mathcal{Q}=\{K\text{ compact subset of }\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}:\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}\subset K,K\text{ connected}\}.

We endow
\mathcal{Q}
with the Hausdorff distance
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}}
on compact subsets of

\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}
.
The metric space
(\mathcal{Q},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}})
is Polish, i.e. separable and complete.
Indeed, the space of compact subsets of
\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}
endowed with

\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is Polish
(see e.g.
[
Mol05
]
, Theorems C.2 and C.8),
and
\mathcal{Q}
is a closed subset of this space, thus also Polish.
Note that in the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
it is important to take

K
connected and not connected by arcs,
otherwise we do not get a complete space.
Let us also explain why we want our compacts to contain
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
.
Essentially, the behaviour of compacts on
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}

will not be important, and we will not distinguish between two compacts that differ only on
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
.
So imposing the compacts to contain
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
is a way to choose a representative in each equivalence class.

Given
K\in\mathcal{Q}
, we will denote

\rho(K)=\max\{t\geq 0:\exists\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1},(t,\omega)\in K\}=\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K,\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}).

(3.9)

Given
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
K
a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
we will denote

K-t=\{(s,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}:(s+t,\omega)\in K\}.

The space
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{E}})
.

In what follows we will consider sequences (infinite countable families)
of compacts in
\mathcal{Q}
.
Actually we will consider the family of compacts up to permutation,
except the first one in the family, which will be marked.
Let
\mathfrak{P}_{0}(\mathbb{N})
denote the permutations
\sigma
of

\mathbb{N}
such that
\sigma(0)=0
.
Given
(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
we will identify this sequence with all the

(K_{\sigma(i)})_{i\geq 0}
for
\sigma\in\mathfrak{P}_{0}(\mathbb{N})
,
and we will denote by
\sim
the corresponding equivalence relation.
We will denote by
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{N}}/\sim
the quotient space.
By an abuse of notation, we will still denote by

(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}
and element of
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{N}}/\sim
.
Let be

\mathcal{E}=\{(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{N}}/\sim~:\lim_{i\to+\infty}\rho(K_{i})=0\}.

Note that the condition
\lim_{i\to+\infty}\rho(K_{i})=0

is invariant under the permutations
\sigma\in\mathfrak{P}_{0}(\mathbb{N})
.
We endow
\mathcal{E}
with the following distance:

\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{E}}((K_{i})_{i\geq 0},(K_{i}^{\prime})_{i\geq 0})=\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K_{0},K_{0}^{\prime})+\inf_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{P}_{0}(\mathbb{N})}\sup_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K_{i},K_{i}^{\prime}).

Lemma 3.4
.

The metric space
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{E}})
is Polish,
i.e. separable and complete.

Proof.

The fact that
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{E}})
is Polish is a consequence of

(\mathcal{Q},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}})
being Polish,
and does not rely on the specific nature of
(\mathcal{Q},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}})
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}^{\#}
be a countable dense subset of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}^{\#}
be the subset of
\mathcal{E}
made of families
where each element is in
\mathcal{Q}^{\#}
, and only finitely many elements are different from
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
.
Then
\mathcal{E}^{\#}
is countable and dense in
\mathcal{E}
for

\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{E}}
.
To see that
(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{E}})
is complete,
order the elements of
(K_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, for some element
(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}
,
in a non-increasing order of
\rho(K_{i})
, and use the fact that

(\mathcal{Q},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{Q}})
is complete.
∎

From now on we will fix a value
\theta\in(0,1/2]
and
consider a Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}
of intensity parameter
\theta
in
the open cylinder
\mathtt{C}_{0}
.
For
t>0
, we set

\mathtt{C}_{t}=(t,+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1},\qquad\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{t}=[t,+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1},

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}=\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}:\operatorname{Range}(\wp)\cap(0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\neq\emptyset\},\qquad\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{t}=\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}:\operatorname{Range}(\wp)\subset\mathtt{C}_{t}\}.

The collections of loops
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}
and

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{t}
are independent and

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}\cup\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{t}=\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{t}
has the same distribution as
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}

up to a translation by
t
along the cylinder. The filtration we will consider is then defined by

\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma\{\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t]}\},\quad\quad t\geq 1.

(3.10)

A Markov chain of clusters

Given
(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}
, we will denote by

(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}
(by abuse of notation)
the family formed by the compacts
(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}
and the loops in

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}
,
where the Brownian loops are identified to their range and considered as compact subsets of
\mathtt{C}_{0}
.
Let
\sim_{t}
be the following equivalence relation on the elements of

(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}
.

•

Given
\wp,\wp^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}
,
whenever
\operatorname{Range}(\wp)\cap\operatorname{Range}(\wp^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
,
we have
\wp\sim_{t}\wp^{\prime}
.

•

Given
\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}
and

i\geq 0
,
whenever
\wp\cap K_{i}\neq\emptyset
,
we have
\operatorname{Range}(\wp)\sim_{t}K_{i}
.

•

Given
i,j\geq 1
,
whenever
(K_{i}\cap K_{j})\setminus\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}\neq\emptyset
,
we have
K_{i}\sim_{t}K_{j}
.
We would like to emphasize that
K_{i}\cap K_{j}
always contains

\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
, but our condition is that this intersection is strictly larger than
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
.

•

Further, we define
\sim_{t}
to be the minimal
equivalence relation satisfying the above three conditions.
That is to say, we identify into the same equivalence class the finite chains
of objects where each couple of consecutive elements satisfy one of the above three rules.

In essence, the equivalence relation
\sim_{t}
corresponds to clusters of

(K_{i}\setminus\partial\mathtt{C}_{0})_{i\geq 0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,t]}
.
Note that a set
K_{i}\setminus\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
is not necessarily connected,
however, by definition, we put all its connected components into the same cluster.
Given an equivalence class
A
of
\sim_{t}
,
we will consider the associated cluster
\mathcal{C}
seen as a connected compact subset of

\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}
:

\mathcal{C}=\overline{\bigcup_{\wp\in A}\operatorname{Range}(\wp)\cup\bigcup_{K_{i}\in A}K_{i}}.

Let
\mathfrak{C}_{t}
denote the family of such clusters induced by
\sim_{t}
,
where each cluster is seen as a compact connected subset of
\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{0}
.
We will denote by
\mathcal{C}_{0,t}\in\mathfrak{C}_{t}
the marked cluster containing
K_{0}
.
Next, we will denote by
(K_{i}(t))_{i\geq 0}
the following family of compacts:

\big(((\mathcal{C}\cap\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{t})-t)\cup\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}\big)_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{t},\mathcal{C}\cap\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{t}\neq\emptyset}.

That is to say, among the compacts of
\mathfrak{C}_{t}
, we take those that intersect

\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{t}
,
consider only the parts inside
\overline{\mathtt{C}}_{t}
, translate them by
-t

to make them adjacent to
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
,
and add the circle
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
for the normalization.
Also,
K_{0}(t)
will be the compact obtained from the marked cluster

\mathcal{C}_{0,t}
(the one containing
K_{0}
).
We see the family
(K_{i}(t))_{i\geq 0}
as an element of
\mathcal{E}
.
By convention, we set
(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}=(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}
.

Now, we consider
t
to be an integer
n
and take the process

(K_{i}(n))_{i\geq 0,n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
We see the latter as a stochastic process in
\mathcal{E}
,
parametrized by discrete time
n
.
This process is actually time-homogeneous Markov with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(
3.10
).
This is because for each
n\geq 1
,
the loop soup
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{n}
is independent from
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,n]}
,
and
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{n}
has the same law as
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}

up to a longitudinal translation by
n
.

3.4
Coupling conditioned clusters in a Brownian loop soup

We consider the state space
\mathcal{E}
and the Markov chain of Brownian loop soup clusters introduced in Section
3.3
.
Let
\dagger
be the subset

\dagger=\{(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}:K_{0}=\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}\}.

Note that the subset
\dagger
is absorbing, i.e. is preserved by the Markov chain.
The subset
\dagger
corresponds to the extinction of the marked cluster
K_{0}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}^{\ast}=\mathcal{E}\setminus\dagger
.
We will denote by
T_{\dagger}
the first hitting time of
\dagger
.
Given an initial condition

(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
,
for every
T\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,

\mathbb{P}(\{T_{\dagger}>T)>0
.
Indeed, it is enough to have a single Brownian loop in
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}

that connects
K_{0}(0)
to
\mathtt{C}_{T}
.
Given
T\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
we will denote by

(K_{i}^{T}(n))_{i\geq 0,0\leq n\leq T}
the Markov chain of clusters
conditioned on
\{T_{\dagger}>T\}
.

In this section we will prove Theorem
3.2
(actually a stronger version; see Theorem
3.3
) by showing that the conditioned Markov chain
(K_{i}^{T}(n))_{i\geq 0,0\leq n\leq T}

satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
3.3
.
For this we will need characteristic times

\mathbf{T}
and favourable configurations

\mathtt{F}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
for
n\geq 1
.
We set

\mathbf{T}((K_{i})_{i\geq 0})=\sup_{i\geq 0}\lceil\rho(K_{i})\rceil\in\mathbb{N},

where
\lceil~\rceil
is the integer ceiling.
Let
c_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
,
c_{1}\geq 5
. We will tune the value of

c_{1}
later; see (
3.14
).
We also set
c_{2}=c_{1}+1
and

\mathtt{F}_{n}=\big\{(K_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}:\rho(K_{0})\geq 2n\text{ and }\forall i\geq 0,\rho(K_{i})\leq(c_{1}-2)n\big\},\quad n\geq 1.

Lemma 3.5
.

There is
p_{0}\in(0,1]
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
for every
T\in\mathbb{N}
with
T\geq n
,
and for every initial condition

(K_{i}^{T}(0))_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
,

\mathbb{P}(\rho(K^{T}_{0}(n))\geq 2n)\geq p_{0}.

Proof.

Let
E_{1,n}
be the event

E_{1,n}=\big\{\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0},\partial\mathtt{C}_{n/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wp}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{3n}\big\}.

Let
E_{2,n}
be the event that there is a loop in
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}
,
contained in the cylinder
(n/2,n)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and that surrounds this cylinder, i.e. is non-contractible in the topological sense.
Then

\mathbb{P}(\rho(K^{T}_{0}(n))\geq 2n)\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n}\cap E_{2,n},T_{\dagger}>T)/\mathbb{P}(T_{\dagger}>T).

The three events
E_{1,n}
,
E_{2,n}
and
\{T_{\dagger}>T\}

are increasing for the Poisson point process
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}
.
By the FKG inequality for Poisson point processes,

\mathbb{P}(\rho(K^{T}_{0}(n))\geq 2n)\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n}\cap E_{2,n})\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n})\mathbb{P}(E_{2,n}).

By Lemma
2.6
,

\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n})\geq p_{1}

for some
p_{1}\in(0,1)
.
Moreover,

\mathbb{P}(E_{2,n})\geq 1-p_{2}^{\theta n}

for some
p_{2}\in(0,1)
(Lemma
2.5
).
Thus we get a uniform lower bound for

\mathbb{P}(\rho(K^{T}_{0}(n))\geq 2n)
.
∎

By Lemma
2.6
, the probability that a single loop crosses
[1,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
decays like
t^{-1}
as
t\to\infty
. In the next lemma, we show that if one conditions on the event that a cluster makes a large crossing, then this probability decays like
t^{-\theta+o(1)}
. This will in particular ensure that loops stay small (although larger) after this conditioning.

Lemma 3.6
.

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,\theta)
,
there is a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
,
such that for every initial condition

(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}

for the Markov chain,
for every
T\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
for every
t_{1}\geq 1
and every

t_{2}\geq 2t_{1}
,

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0},\partial\mathtt{C}_{t_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wp}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{t_{2}}\big|T_{\dagger}>T\big)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\Big(\dfrac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\Big)^{\theta-\varepsilon}.

(3.11)

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be as in the statement.
Let
\underline{\tau}t_{1}
(resp.
\overline{\tau}t_{1}
) be the left most (resp. right most) level reached by the loops that cross
[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
:

\underline{\tau}:=\inf\{u\in(0,1):\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}:\partial\mathtt{C}_{ut_{1}}\overset{\wp}{\longleftrightarrow}\partial\mathtt{C}_{t_{2}}\},\quad\bar{\tau}:=\sup\{v>t_{2}/t_{1}:\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}:\partial\mathtt{C}_{t_{1}}\overset{\wp}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathtt{C}_{vt_{1}}\}.

The levels
\underline{\tau}t_{1}
and
\overline{\tau}t_{1}
can be reached either by the same loop or by two distinct loops. In both cases, one can use Lemma
2.6
to show that there exists
C>0
such that for all
u\in(0,1)
and
v\geq t_{2}/t_{1}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\underline{\tau}\leq u,\overline{\tau}\geq v\right)\leq C(u/v).

(3.12)

With these notations, the left hand side of (
3.11
) is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{t_{2}/t_{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\underline{\tau}\in\mathrm{d}u,\bar{\tau}\in\mathrm{d}v\right)\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(T_{\dagger}>T|\underline{\tau}=u,\overline{\tau}=v\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(T_{\dagger}>T\right)}.

(3.13)

If
vt_{1}<T
, the above ratio on the right hand side is at most

\mathbb{P}\left(T_{\dagger}>ut_{1},\mathtt{C}_{vt_{1}}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathtt{C}_{T}\right)/\mathbb{P}\left(T_{\dagger}>T\right).

If
vt_{1}\geq T
, this ratio can be bounded by

\mathbb{P}\left(T_{\dagger}>ut_{1}\right)/\mathbb{P}\left(T_{\dagger}>T\right).

In both cases and by FKG inequality, the ratio in (
3.13
) is bounded from above by the inverse of the product of the probabilities of three events: 1) that there is a cluster joining
\partial\mathtt{C}_{ut_{1}/2}
to
\partial\mathtt{C}_{2vt_{1}}
, 2) there is a non contractible loop in
[ut_{1}/2,ut_{1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, 3) there is a non contractible loop in
[vt_{1},2vt_{1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. By Lemma
2.5
and Theorem
2.4
, the ratio in (
3.13
) is therefore bounded by
C_{\varepsilon}(v/u)^{1-\theta+\varepsilon}
. Plugging this estimate in (
3.13
) and performing two integrations by part, we obtain that the left hand side of (
3.11
) is bounded by

\displaystyle C\mathbb{P}\left(\underline{\tau}\leq 1,\overline{\tau}\geq\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right)^{1-\theta+\varepsilon}+C\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}\left(\underline{\tau}\leq u,\overline{\tau}\geq\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\right)^{1-\theta+\varepsilon}\frac{1}{u^{2-\theta+\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle+C\int_{t_{2}/t_{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\underline{\tau}\leq 1,\overline{\tau}\geq v\right)\frac{v^{-\theta+\varepsilon}}{u^{1-\theta+\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}v+C\int_{0}^{1}\int_{t_{2}/t_{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\underline{\tau}\leq u,\overline{\tau}\geq v\right)\frac{v^{-\theta+\varepsilon}}{u^{2-\theta+\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

(
3.12
) then concludes the proof.
∎

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
an integer time
T\geq c_{2}n
,
and an initial condition

(K_{i}^{T}(0))_{i\geq 0}\in\mathcal{E}^{\ast}
for the conditioned Markov chain,
with
\mathbf{T}((K_{i}^{T}(0))_{i\geq 0})\leq n
,
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big((K_{i}^{T}(n))_{i\geq 0}\in\mathtt{F}_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\rho(K_{0}^{T}(n))\geq 2n\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0},\partial\mathtt{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wp}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}-1)n}\big|T_{\dagger}>T\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle p_{0}-C_{\varepsilon}(c_{1}-1)^{-(\theta-\varepsilon)},

where
p_{0}
is given by Lemma
3.5
,

\varepsilon\in(0,\theta)
and
C_{\varepsilon}

is given by Lemma
3.6
.
We chose
\varepsilon=\theta/2
,
and we take an integer
c_{1}\geq 5
such that

p_{0}-C_{\theta/2}(c_{1}-1)^{-\frac{\theta}{2}}>0.

(3.14)

With this choice of
c_{1}
we are sure that Condition
2

of Theorem
3.3
is satisfied.
Let us also check that Condition
3
of Theorem
3.3

is satisfied.
Indeed,

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbf{T}((K_{i}^{T}(c_{1}n))_{i\geq 0})\geq un\big)\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0},\partial\mathtt{C}_{c_{1}n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wp}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}+u)n}\big|T_{\dagger}>T\big)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\Big(\dfrac{c_{1}}{c_{1}+u}\Big)^{\theta-\varepsilon},

where
\varepsilon\in(0,\theta)
and
C_{\varepsilon}

is given by Lemma
3.6
.
It remains to check Condition
1
of Theorem
3.3
.

For
t_{2}>t_{1}>0
, we will denote

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[t_{1},t_{2}]}=\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}:\operatorname{Range}(\wp)\cap[t_{1},t_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\neq\emptyset\}.

Lemma 3.7
.

There is
\hat{p}_{0}\in(0,1]
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
and for every
T\geq c_{2}n
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\partial\mathtt{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\Big|\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}-1)n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\Big)\geq\hat{p}_{0}.

Proof.

This is similar to Lemma
3.5
.
Let
E_{1,n}
be the event

E_{1,n}=\{\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0},\partial\mathtt{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wp}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{c_{1}n}\}.

Let
E_{2,n}
be the event that there is a loop in

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}
,
contained in the cylinder

((c_{1}-1)n,c_{1}n)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
that surrounds the cylinder.
Then

E_{1,n}\cap E_{2,n}\cap\Big\{\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}-1)n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\Big\}\subset\Big\{\partial\mathtt{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\Big\}.

The three events
E_{1,n}
,
E_{2,n}
and

\big\{\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}-1)n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\big\}

are increasing for the Poisson point process
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}
,
so one can apply the FKG inequality.
Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\big(\partial\mathtt{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\big|\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}-1)n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\big)\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n}\cap E_{2,n})\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n})\mathbb{P}(E_{2,n}).

By Lemma
2.6
,

\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n})\geq 1-\hat{p}_{1}^{\theta}

for some
\hat{p}_{1}\in(0,1)
.
Moreover,
\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n})\geq 1-\hat{p}_{2}^{\theta n}

for some
\hat{p}_{2}\in(0,1)
.
This gives the desired lower bound uniform in
n
and
T
.
∎

In the sequel, given a random variable
X
and an event A,
we will denote by
\operatorname{Law}(X|A)

the law of
X
conditioned on the event
A
.

Lemma 3.8
.

There is
q\in(0,1]
such that
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
for every
T\geq c_{2}n
and
and for every two initial conditions

(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}

and
(\widehat{K}_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}
in
\mathtt{F}_{n}
,
we have

1-\dfrac{1}{2}d_{\rm TV}(L_{n,T},\widehat{L}_{n,T})\geq q,

where

L_{n,T}=\operatorname{Law}\big((K_{i}((c_{1}-1)n))_{i\geq 0}\big|T_{\dagger}>T-n\big),\qquad\widehat{L}_{n,T}=\operatorname{Law}\big((\widehat{K}_{i}((c_{1}-1)n))_{i\geq 0}\big|T_{\dagger}>T-n\big),

and where
(K_{i}(m))_{i\geq 0,m\geq 0}
,
resp.
(\widehat{K}_{i}(m))_{i\geq 0,m\geq 0}

are the Markov chains of clusters starting from the corresponding initial conditions.

Proof.

Consider the following events for the loop soup
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,T-n]}
.
Let
E_{1,n,T}
be the event

E_{1,n,T}=\Big\{\partial\mathtt{C}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\Big\}.

Let
E_{2,n}
be the event that there is a loop of

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,T-n]}
contained in the cylinder

(n,2n)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
that surrounds the cylinder.
Let
E_{3,n}
be the event that there is a loop of

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap(0,T-n]}
contained in the cylinder

((c_{1}-2)n,(c_{1}-1)n)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
that surrounds the cylinder.
See Figure
3.2
.
Note that the events
E_{1,n,T}
,
E_{2,n}
and
E_{3,n}

are independent since they involve disjoint types of loops.

Figure 3.2:
Schematic representation of the events considered in the proof of Lemma
3.8
. Only the marked compact
K_{0}(0)
and
\widehat{K}_{0}(0)
of the initial conditions
(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}
and
(\widehat{K}_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}
have been depicted (light and dark grey respectively). The existence of the blue cluster corresponds to the event
E_{1,n,T}
whereas the existence of the two red loops corresponds to the events
E_{2,n,T}
and
E_{3,n,T}
.

Now we consider the Markov chains
(K_{i}(m))_{i\geq 0,m\geq 0}

and
(\widehat{K}_{i}(m))_{i\geq 0,m\geq 0}
on the same probability space,
obtained from the same Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{0}
.
Note that in this coupling, neither of the two Markov processes is conditioned
on
T_{\dagger}>T-n
. Not yet.

Since
(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}\in\mathtt{F}_{n}
and
(\widehat{K}_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}\in\mathtt{F}_{n}
, we have

E_{1,n,T}\cap E_{2,n}\subset\{T_{\dagger}((K_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n\}\quad\text{and}\quad E_{1,n,T}\cap E_{2,n}\subset\{T_{\dagger}((\widehat{K}_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n\};

see Figure
3.2
.
Further, on the event
E_{1,n,T}\cap E_{2,n}\cap E_{3,n}
,
we have
(K_{i}((c_{1}-1)n))_{i\geq 0}=(\widehat{K}_{i}((c_{1}-1)n))_{i\geq 0}
a.s.
Indeed, if two Brownian loops
\wp
and
\wp^{\prime}
intersecting

\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}-1)n}
are connected by a finite chain that uses

(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}

and
(\widehat{K}_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}
,
then this chain has to intersect the non-contractible loops
in
((c_{1}-2)n,(c_{1}-1)n)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
that exist on the event
E_{3,n}
,
and thus the connection between
\wp
and
\wp^{\prime}

can also be done without using
(K_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}

or
(\widehat{K}_{i}(0))_{i\geq 0}
,
by using just the Brownian loop; see Figure
3.2
.

Thus, we get some overlap in total variation between
the non-conditioned laws

\operatorname{Law}\big((K_{i}((c_{1}-1)n))_{i\geq 0}\big)
and

\operatorname{Law}\big((\widehat{K}_{i}((c_{1}-1)n))_{i\geq 0}\big)
.
Moreover, this overlap happens on the event

\{T_{\dagger}((K_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n\}\cap\{T_{\dagger}((\widehat{K}_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n\}.

Therefore, we get a non-trivial overlap for the conditioned laws too:

1-\dfrac{1}{2}d_{\rm TV}(L_{n,T},\widehat{L}_{n,T})\geq\dfrac{\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n,T}\cap E_{2,n}\cap E_{3,n})}{\mathbb{P}(T_{\dagger}((K_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n)\vee\mathbb{P}(T_{\dagger}((\widehat{K}_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n)}.

Further,

\{T_{\dagger}((K_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n\}\cup\{T_{\dagger}((\widehat{K}_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n\}\subset E_{4,n,T},

where
E_{4,n,T}
is the event

E_{4,n,T}=\Big\{\partial\mathtt{C}_{(c_{1}-1)n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{\cap[(c_{1}-1)n,T-n]}}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}\partial\mathtt{C}_{T-n}\Big\}.

Thus,

\mathbb{P}(T_{\dagger}((K_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n)\vee\mathbb{P}(T_{\dagger}((\widehat{K}_{i})_{i\geq 0})>T-n)\leq\mathbb{P}(E_{4,n,T}).

Thus,

1-\dfrac{1}{2}d_{\rm TV}(L_{n,T},\widehat{L}_{n,T})\geq\dfrac{\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n,T}\cap E_{2,n}\cap E_{3,n})}{\mathbb{P}(E_{4,n,T})}\geq\dfrac{\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n,T})}{\mathbb{P}(E_{4,n,T})}\mathbb{P}(E_{2,n})\mathbb{P}(E_{3,n}).

By Lemma
3.7
,

\dfrac{\mathbb{P}(E_{1,n,T})}{\mathbb{P}(E_{4,n,T})}\geq\hat{p}_{0}>0,

where the lower bound is uniform in
n
and
T
.
Further,

\mathbb{P}(E_{2,n})=\mathbb{P}(E_{3,n})=1-\hat{p}_{2}^{\theta n}
,
for some
\hat{p}_{2}\in(0,1)
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma
3.8
is exactly Condition
1
of
Theorem
3.3
.
So it applies to our Markov chains of clusters in a Brownian loop soup and concludes the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

3.5
Convergence of the ratio of survival probabilities

We finish this section by proving Theorem
3.1
. We first state and prove a corollary of Theorem
3.2
.
We use the same notations as the ones introduced above Theorem
3.2
.

Corollary 3.9
.

For every initial conditions
K_{0}
and
\hat{K}_{0}
not identical to
\partial\mathtt{C}_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T)/\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)
converges as
T\to\infty
.

Remark 3.10
.

By FKG inequality, it is clear that the ratio
\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T)/\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)
remains uniformly bounded away from zero and from infinity. Indeed, recalling the notation
\rho(K_{0})
(
3.9
), let
E
be the event that there is a loop intersecting
\hat{K}_{0}
that disconnects
\partial\mathtt{C}_{\rho(K_{0})}
and
\partial\mathtt{C}_{\rho(K_{0})+1}
. Since
E\cap\{T^{\dagger}>T\}\subset\{\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T\}
, by FKG inequality we get that

\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T)/\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)\leq 1/\mathbb{P}\left(E\right).

The lower bound is similar. On the other hand, showing that this ratio actually converges is much more involved and relies on the coupling results we developed.

Proof.

Let
T\gg t\gg 1
be large integers and let
E_{t,T}
be the event that under the coupling of Theorem
3.2
, the coalescence time
\mathcal{T}^{T}
is smaller than
t
. By Theorem
3.2
,
\mathbb{P}\left(E_{t,T}\right)\to 1
as
T\to\infty
followed by
t\to\infty
.
In this proof, we will denote by
(\mathcal{F}_{s})_{s\geq 0}
the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{s}=\sigma(K_{0}(u),\hat{K}_{0}(u),u\in\{0,\dots,s\})
,
s\geq 0
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T

\displaystyle|T^{\dagger}>t)=\mathbb{E}\left[P(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t})\mathbf{1}_{E_{t,T}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t})\mathbf{1}_{E_{t,T}^{c}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T|\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t})\mathbf{1}_{E_{t,T}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t})\mathbf{1}_{E_{t,T}^{c}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T|\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t)+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t})-\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T|\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t})\right)\mathbf{1}_{E_{t,T}^{c}}\right].

The rest of the proof is dedicated to showing that the second right hand side term is much smaller than the first one.
Let
p,q>1
be such that
1/p+1/q=1
. By Hölder’s inequality and the above inequality,
\left|\mathbb{P}(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t)-\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T|\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t)\right|
is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(E_{t,T}^{c}\right)^{1/p}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)^{q}+\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T|\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t})^{q}\right]^{1/q}.

(3.15)

Let us denote by

\tau:=\sup\{s>0:\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{\cap(0,t]}^{\theta}:\operatorname{Range}(\wp)\cap\{s\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\neq\varnothing\}.

Conditionally on
\tau,\mathcal{F}_{t},T^{\dagger}>t
, in order to have
T^{\dagger}>T
, there must be a cluster of loops included in
(t,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
that crosses
[\tau,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. By scaling, this probability is equal to
\mathbb{P}\Big(\tau-t\overset{\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}T-t|\tau\Big)
. We obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)^{q}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left(\tau>T\right)+\int_{t}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left(\tau\in\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathbb{P}\left(s-t\overset{\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}T-t\right)^{q}.

(3.16)

Let
E_{1},E_{2}
and
E_{3}
be the following events:

•

E_{1}
: there is a cluster of
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}
crossing
[t-1,s-t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
;

•

E_{2}
: there is a non contractible loop inside
[t-1,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
;

•

E_{3}
: there is a non contractible loop inside
[s-t,s-t+1]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

We have
E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap\{T^{\dagger}>t\}\cap\{s-t\overset{\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}T-t\}\subset\{T^{\dagger}>T-t\}
. The five events on the left hand side are increasing events. By FKG inequality we deduce that the product of the five individual probabilities is at most
\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)
. By Lemma
2.5
,
\mathbb{P}\left(E_{2}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(E_{3}\right)\geq c
and by Theorem
2.4
,
\mathbb{P}\left(E_{1}\right)\geq c_{\varepsilon}(s/t)^{-1+\theta-\varepsilon}
. This leads to

\mathbb{P}\Big(s-t\overset{\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}T-t\Big)\leq C(s/t)^{1-\theta+\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)/\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right).

Plugging this estimate back in (
3.16
) and integrating by parts (differentiating
s^{q(1-\theta+\varepsilon)}
and integrating
\mathbb{P}\left(\tau\in\mathrm{d}s\right)
) gives the following upper bound for the left hand side of (
3.16
):

\displaystyle C(1/t)^{q(1-\theta+\varepsilon)}\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)^{q}/\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)^{q}\int_{t}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left(\tau\geq s\right)s^{q(1-\theta+\varepsilon)-1}\mathrm{d}s.

By Lemma
2.6
,
\mathbb{P}\left(\tau\geq s\right)\leq Ct/s
. With the change of variable
s=xt
, we have obtained that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)^{q}\right]\leq C\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)^{q}/\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)^{q}\int_{1}^{\infty}x^{q(1-\theta+\varepsilon)-2}\mathrm{d}x.

We choose
1<q<1/(1-\theta+\varepsilon)
to make sure that the integral above converges. We have overall obtained that
\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)^{q}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T|T^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)^{q}\right]\leq C\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)^{q}
. Since
\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t)/\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)\in[1/C,C]
, we also obtain that

\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)^{q}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T|\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)^{q}\right]\leq C\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)^{q}.

Multiplying (
3.15
) by
\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)/\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)}-\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t)}\right|\leq C\mathbb{P}\left(E_{t,T}^{c}\right)^{1/p}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)}.

Using FKG inequality and the fact that the probability of crossing
[T-t,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
tends to 1 as
T\to\infty
(
t
fixed), it can be checked that
\limsup_{T\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T-t\right)/\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)
is bounded uniformly in
t
; see Remark
3.10
for a very similar statement. By then sending
t\to\infty
, we obtain that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\limsup_{T\to\infty}\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)}-\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>t\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>t)}\right|=0.

This shows that
\mathbb{P}\left(T^{\dagger}>T\right)/\mathbb{P}(\hat{T}^{\dagger}>T)
converges as
T\to\infty
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

The proof of the convergence of the first ratio in (
3.1
) follows quickly from Corollary
3.9
and from conformal invariance of the loop soup. Indeed, let
f:D\to\mathbb{D}
be a conformal map with
f(x)=0
. By conformal invariance of the loop soup, the first ratio in (
3.1
) is equal to

\mathbb{P}\Big(f(K)\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}f(D(x,r))\Big)\Big/\mathbb{P}\Big(f(\hat{K})\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}f(D(x,r))\Big).

There exists a constant
C>1
such that if
r
is small enough
C^{-1}r\mathbb{D}\subset f(D(x,r))\subset Cr\mathbb{D}
. Because the probability of connecting
K
to
C^{-1}r\mathbb{D}
is asymptotically equivalent to that of connecting
K
to
Cr\mathbb{D}
, we deduce that the first ratio in (
3.1
) is equal to

(1+o(1))\mathbb{P}\Big(f(K)\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}r\mathbb{D}\Big)\Big/\mathbb{P}\Big(f(\hat{K})\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}r\mathbb{D}\Big).

By Corollary
3.9
, the above ratio converges as
r\to 0
(
r=e^{-T}
in the notations of Corollary
3.9
).

We now move to the proof of the convergence of the second ratio in (
3.1
) and the identification of the limit. Let us denote by
\ell
the limit of the first ratio in (
3.1
).
Let
\delta>0
and let
\varepsilon\in(0,\mathrm{d}(z,\partial D))
be small enough so that for all
r\in(0,\varepsilon)
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(z,r)\Big)\Big/\mathbb{P}\Big(\hat{K}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(z,r)\Big)\leq(1+\delta)\ell.

As in (
2.18
), we can replace
\Xi_{a}^{z}
by a disc centred at
z
with random radius
\left\|\Xi_{a}^{z}-z\right\|_{\infty}
:

\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z}}{\longleftrightarrow}z\Big)=(1+o(1))\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(z,\left\|\Xi_{a}^{z}-z\right\|_{\infty})\Big).

By Lemma
2.9
, the probability that
\left\|\Xi_{a}^{z}-z\right\|_{\infty}>\varepsilon
is at most
Ca=C\gamma^{2}
which is much smaller than the probability we are looking at (it behaves like
\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
, see Theorem
1.2
). By Lemma
2.9
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z}}{\longleftrightarrow}z\Big)=(1+o(1))\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}r\frac{ar^{a-1}}{\CR(z,D)^{a}}\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(z,r)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))(1+\delta)\ell\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}r\frac{ar^{a-1}}{\CR(z,D)^{a}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\hat{K}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(z,r)\Big)\leq(1+o(1))(1+\delta)\ell\mathbb{P}\Big(\hat{K}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z}}{\longleftrightarrow}z\Big)

where in the last inequality we simply reversed the above procedure.
This proves that

\limsup_{a\to 0}\mathbb{P}\Big(K\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z}}{\longleftrightarrow}z\Big)\Big/\mathbb{P}\Big(\hat{K}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z}}{\longleftrightarrow}z\Big)\leq\ell.

The lower bound is proved similarly. This concludes the proof.
∎

4
Construction of
h_{\theta}
: proof of Theorems
1.2
,
1.11
and
1.12

We start by stating the intermediate results needed in order to prove Theorems
1.2
,
1.11
and
1.12
, and then use them to prove these theorems. The rest of the section then consists of the proofs of these intermediate results.

We start with a lemma that shows that computing the second moment of
h_{\gamma}
boils down to computing the crossing probabilities between
x
and
y
in a soup whose loops come from
1) an unconditioned Brownian loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
,
2) a thick loop
\Xi_{a}^{x}
at
x
and
3) a thick loop
\Xi_{a}^{y}
at
y
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
F:D\times D\times\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable admissible function such that for all
x,y\in D
,
F(x,y,\cdot)
does not depend on loops with diameter smaller than
\varepsilon
. For all
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in(0,\sqrt{2})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{D\times D}F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})h_{\gamma}(x)h_{\gamma^{\prime}}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\right]=C(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon)\frac{\gamma^{2}{\gamma^{\prime}}^{2}}{Z_{\gamma}Z_{\gamma^{\prime}}}

(4.1)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}Z_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\int_{D\times D}\mathbb{E}\Big[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})\mathbf{1}\Big\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big\}\Big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

where the three collections of loops
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
,
\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}
and
\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}
are independent and distributed as in Lemma
2.3
.
The constant
C(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon)
is bounded uniformly w.r.t.
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
in compact subsets of
[0,\sqrt{2})
by a quantity which may depend on
\varepsilon
.

The next two results are then dedicated to estimating the crossing probability appearing in Lemma
4.1
. The first result has a very similar flavour as
[
JLQ23
, Proposition 4.1]
. On the other hand, the second result is novel and crucially relies on Theorem
3.1
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
\eta>0
, there exists
C>0
such that for all
\gamma\in(0,1]
and
x,y\in D
,

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\right)\leq C|\log|x-y||^{2(1-\theta)+\eta}.

(4.2)

Proposition 4.3
.

For any distinct points
x,y\in D
, the limit

C_{\theta}(x,y):=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\Big(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big)

(4.3)

exists and

C_{\theta}(x,y)\leq\liminf_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma_{1}}Z_{\gamma_{2}}}\mathbb{P}\Big(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a_{1}}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a_{2}}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big).

(4.4)

Moreover,

C_{\theta}(x,y)=\left(\log\frac{1}{|x-y|}\right)^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}\quad\quad\text{as}\quad x-y\to 0.

(4.5)

More generally, let
\varepsilon>0
and
F:\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable function that does not depend on loops with diameter smaller than
\varepsilon>0
. Then the following two limits exist and are identical

\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{r}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[F(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\mathbf{1}\Big\{D(x,r)\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}D(y,r)\Big\}\right]=\lim_{a\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[F(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\mathbf{1}\Big\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big\}\right].

(4.6)

Notation 4.4
.

If we want to keep track of the domain
D
, we will denote by
C_{\theta,D}(x,y)
the limit of (
4.3
).

We can now show Theorems
1.2
,
1.11
and
1.12
.

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

It follows directly from (
4.6
). Indeed,
let
x,y\in D
be two distinct points.
By a density-type argument, to prove Theorem
1.11
, it is enough to show that for any
\varepsilon>0
and any bounded measurable function
F:\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
which does not depend on loops with diameter smaller than
\varepsilon
,

\mathbb{E}_{x\leftrightarrow y,r}\left[F(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right]

converges as
r\to 0
. This is a mere restatement of the convergence (
4.6
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Let
f:D\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded measurable function. By decomposing
f
into its positive and negative parts, we can assume without loss of generality that
f
is nonnegative. We want to show that
((h_{\gamma},f),\gamma\in(0,\sqrt{2})
is Cauchy in
L^{2}
, i.e. that

\mathbb{E}\left[|(h_{\gamma_{1}},f)-(h_{\gamma_{2}},f)|^{2}\right]\to 0

as
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\to 0
.
By Lemma
4.1
, this second moment is equal to

o(1)+\int_{D\times D}f(x)f(y)\Bigg(\frac{\mathbb{P}\Big(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a_{1}}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a_{1}}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big)}{Z_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}}+\frac{\mathbb{P}\Big(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a_{2}}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a_{2}}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big)}{Z_{\gamma_{2}}^{2}}-2\frac{\mathbb{P}\Big(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a_{1}}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a_{2}}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big)}{Z_{\gamma_{1}}Z_{\gamma_{2}}}\Bigg).

Proposition
4.3
together with dominated convergence theorem and Lemma
4.2
(and Fatou’s lemma for the crossed term involving both
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
) then shows that

\limsup_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[|(h_{\gamma_{1}},f)-(h_{\gamma_{2}},f)|^{2}\right]\leq 0
.
This concludes the
L^{2}
convergence of
(h_{\gamma},f)
.

We now explain how one can lift this convergence to a convergence in the Sobolev space
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})
, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
is arbitrary small. Recall that
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})
is the set of tempered distribution
f
such that

\left\|f\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{C}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{-\varepsilon}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty.

(4.7)

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in(0,\sqrt{2})
. Denoting
C_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\gamma_{1}}(x)-h_{\gamma_{2}}(x))(h_{\gamma_{1}}(y)-h_{\gamma_{2}}(y))\right]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|h_{\gamma_{1}}-h_{\gamma_{2}}\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left|\hat{h}_{\gamma_{1}}(\xi)-\hat{h}_{\gamma_{2}}(\xi)\right|^{2}}{(1+\left|\xi\right|^{2})^{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}\xi\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left|\hat{h}_{\gamma_{1}}(\xi)-\hat{h}_{\gamma_{2}}(\xi)\right|^{2}}{\left|\xi\right|^{2\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}\xi

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\int_{D\times D}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y~C_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,y)\int\mathrm{d}\xi~\frac{e^{2\pi i\xi\cdot(x-y)}}{\left|\xi\right|^{2\varepsilon}}=C_{\varepsilon}\int_{D\times D}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y~\frac{C_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(x,y)}{|x-y|^{2-2\varepsilon}}

where we used the fact that the Fourier transform of
|\cdot|^{-2\varepsilon}
is
C_{\varepsilon}|\cdot|^{-2+2\varepsilon}
for some constant
C_{\varepsilon}
depending on
\varepsilon
.
We can then conclude as before using Proposition
4.3
that
h_{\gamma}
is Cauchy in
L^{2}(H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{P})
.

Finally, the proof that the normalising constant
Z_{\gamma}
(
1.3
) satisfies
Z_{\gamma}=\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
as
\gamma\to 0
can be found in Section
2.4
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

By a simple density-type argument, it is enough to check the identity statement in Theorem
1.12
for a function
F:\mathfrak{L}\to\mathbb{R}
depending only on loops with diameter larger than a given threshold. For such functions, it then follows from Lemma
4.1
, Theorem
1.11
and (
4.6
).
∎

4.1
Proof of Lemma
4.1

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

By definition of
h_{\gamma}
, the left hand side of (
4.1
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}Z_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\int_{D\times D}\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}^{+}(\mathrm{d}y)-\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}y-\mathrm{d}x\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}^{+}(\mathrm{d}y)+\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y)\right].

By (
2.12
),

\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1})\right]\mathrm{d}x

where the loops
\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}
are as defined above Lemma
2.3
. A similar result holds at
y
.
To handle the term
\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}^{+}(\mathrm{d}y)\right]
, we first control the contribution of soups containing at least one loop which visits both
x
and
y
. By triangle inequality and then by
(
2.13
) and (
2.14
) applied to
F\equiv 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}^{+}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}:x,y\in\wp\right\}}\right]\right|\leq\left\|F\right\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}:x,y\in\wp\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle=4\left\|F\right\|_{\infty}\left(\left(2\pi\sqrt{aa^{\prime}}G_{D}(x,y)\right)^{1-\theta}\Gamma(\theta)I_{\theta-1}\left(4\pi\sqrt{aa^{\prime}}G_{D}(x,y)\right)-1\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=O(\gamma^{2}{\gamma^{\prime}}^{2}).

(4.9)

On the other hand, by the Girsanov-type transform (
2.14
), the contribution with no such loop is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\nexists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}:x,y\in\wp\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}\Big[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})\mathbf{1}\{\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})\}\Big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

We then notice that, conditionally on
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}
, the probability that both
x
and
y
belong to positive clusters of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}
is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\Big(1-\mathbf{1}\Big\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big\}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}\Big\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{1}\Big\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big\}.

Putting everything together, we have obtained that the left hand side of (
4.1
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}Z_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\int_{D\times D}\mathbb{E}\left[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})\mathbf{1}\Big\{x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big\}\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+O(1)\frac{\gamma^{2}{\gamma^{\prime}}^{2}}{Z_{\gamma}Z_{\gamma^{\prime}}}+\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}Z_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\int_{D\times D}\mathbb{E}[F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})-F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1})

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~-F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})+F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

To bound the last integral above, we need to recall that
F
does not depend on loops with diameter smaller than a given threshold
\varepsilon
. Hence, if
\wedge_{i\geq 1}\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}
(concatenation of
\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x},i\geq 1
) or
\wedge_{i\geq 1}\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}
has diameter smaller than
\varepsilon
, then

F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})-F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}\}_{i\geq 1})-F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\{\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}\}_{i\geq 1})+F(x,y,\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})=0.

The probability that both
\wedge_{i\geq 1}\Xi_{a_{i}}^{x}
and
\wedge_{i\geq 1}\Xi_{a_{i}^{\prime}}^{y}
have diameter at least
\varepsilon
is of order
\gamma^{2}{\gamma^{\prime}}^{2}
(Lemma
2.9
) concluding the proof.
∎

4.2
Proof of Lemma
4.2
and Proposition
4.3

We start by proving Lemma
4.2
.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Let
\eta>0
be small and
x,y\in D
. If
|x-y|\leq e^{-a^{-1+\eta}}
, we simply bound the probability in (
4.2
) by 1. Using that
Z_{\gamma}=\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}=a^{1-\theta+o(1)}
, we obtain that the left hand side of (
4.2
) is at most

a^{-2(1-\theta)+o(1)}\leq C|\log|x-y||^{2(1-\theta)/(1-2\eta)}.

We therefore only need to consider the case where
x
and
y
are not too close to each other, i.e.
|x-y|\geq e^{-a^{-1+\eta}}
.
For
z\in\{x,y\}
, we will denote by
D_{z}=D(z,|x-y|/2)
.
By Lemma
2.9
, the minimal disc centred at
x
containing
\Xi_{a}^{x}
has typically a radius of order
e^{-O(1)/a}
. It is therefore very unlikely that
\Xi_{a}^{x}
exits
D_{x}
. We are first going to show that this is still the case conditionally on the existence of a cluster joining
x
and
y
:

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,\exists z\in\{x,y\}:\Xi_{a}^{z}\not\subset D_{z}\right)\to 0\quad\quad\text{as~}\gamma\to 0

(4.10)

and also that the left hand side can be bounded by
C|\log|x-y||^{\frac{2(1-\theta)}{1-2\eta}}
, uniformly in
\gamma
.
Thanks to a union bound, we can focus on the event that
\Xi_{a}^{x}\not\subset D_{x}
.
With some abuse of notation, we will view
\Xi_{a}^{x}
both as the collection of excursions in a Poisson point process with intensity
a\mu_{D}^{x,x}
and as the loop formed by the concatenation of all these excursions.
Let
E_{x}^{1}
(resp.
E_{x}^{2}
) be the event that there is a unique excursion (resp. at least two distinct excursions) of
\Xi_{a}^{x}
that exits
D_{x}
.
By Lemma
2.9
,

\mu_{D}^{x,x}(\{\wp\not\subset D_{x}\})=\log\frac{2\CR(x,D)}{|x-y|}.

So

\mathbb{P}\left(E_{x}^{2}\right)\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{\wp\neq\wp^{\prime}\in\Xi_{a}^{x}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\wp\not\subset D_{x},\wp^{\prime}\not\subset D_{x}\right\}}\Big]=a^{2}\mu_{D}^{z,z}(\{\wp\not\subset D_{x}\})^{2}\leq Ca^{2}|\log|x-y||^{2}.

In that case, we simply bound

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{x}^{2}\right)\leq a^{-2(1-\theta)+o(1)}\mathbb{P}\left(E_{x}^{2}\right)\leq Ca^{2\theta+o(1)}|\log|x-y||^{2}\leq C|\log|x-y||^{\frac{2(1-\theta)}{1-2\eta}}.

The probability of the event
E_{x}^{1}
is at most
Ca|\log|x-y||
. On that event, let
R_{x}
be the minimal distance between
y
and the unique excursion of
\Xi_{a}^{x}
that exists
D_{x}
. Let also
R_{y}
be the maximal
r>0
such that there exists a cluster of loops of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}
that connects
y
to
\partial D(y,r)
.
On the event
E_{1}^{x}
, in order to have a cluster joining
x
and
y
, we need to have
R_{x}\leq R_{y}
.
Since
R_{y}
and
R_{x}
are independent, we get that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{x}^{1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(E_{x}^{1}\right)}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{x}\leq R_{y}|E_{x}^{1}\right)

(4.11)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Ca|\log|x-y||}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\int_{0}^{|x-y|}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{y}\in\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathbb{P}\left(R_{x}\leq r|E_{x}^{1}\right).

For all
r\in(0,|x-y|)
, the probability that
R_{x}
is smaller than
r
can be compared with the probability that a Brownian path starting on the circle
\partial D_{x}
hits
D(y,r)
before exiting the domain
D
. Hence for all
r\in(0,|x-y|)
,

\mathbb{P}\left(R_{x}\leq|x-y|\Big|E_{x}^{1}\right)\leq C\frac{|\log|x-y||}{|\log r|}.

Injecting this estimate in (
4.11
) and then integrating by parts leads to

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{x}^{1}\right)\leq\frac{Ca|\log|x-y||^{2}}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\int_{0}^{|x-y|}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(R_{y}\geq r\right)}{r|\log r|^{2}}\mathrm{d}r.

Now, by Lemma
2.11
(and conformal invariance to get back to the unit disc),

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{y}\geq r\right)\leq C|\log r|^{1-\theta+\eta},\quad\quad r\in(0,|x-y|).

We thus have

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{x}^{1}\right)\leq\frac{Ca|\log|x-y||^{2}}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{|x-y|}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r|\log r|^{1+\theta-\eta}}\leq Ca^{\theta+o(1)}|\log|x-y||^{2-\theta+\eta}.

In the last inequality we used that
a/Z_{\gamma}=a^{\theta+o(1)}
.
This concludes the proof of (
4.10
). This also shows that the left hand side of (
4.10
) is bounded by
C|\log|x-y||^{\frac{2(1-\theta)}{1-2\eta}}
because
|x-y|\geq e^{-a^{-1+\eta}}
.

We can now work on the event that
\Xi_{a}^{z}\subset D_{z}
for
z=x,y
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{D,x,y}^{\theta}
be the subset of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
consisting of the loops that are not included in
D_{x}\cup D_{y}
. Let

r_{z}=\inf\{r>0:\exists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D,x,y}^{\theta}:\wp\cap D(z,r)\neq\varnothing\},\quad\quad z=x,y.

By the restriction property of
\Xi_{a}^{x}
(see Lemma
2.8
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,\forall z=x,y,\Xi_{a}^{z}\subset D_{z}\right)\leq\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\forall z=x,y,z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z}}{\longleftrightarrow}\partial D(z,r_{z}),\Xi_{a}^{z}\subset D_{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\frac{\CR(x,D_{x})^{a}\CR(y,D_{y})^{a}}{\CR(x,D)^{a}\CR(y,D)^{a}}\mathbb{P}\left(\forall z=x,y,z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,D_{z}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\partial D(z,r_{z})\right).

In what follows, we will simply bound the ratio of conformal radii by 1 (which is also a good approximation since
a\to 0
).
By scale invariance of
\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}
and
\Xi_{a}^{z,D_{z}}
and by Lemma
2.11
,

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,D_{z}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\partial D(z,r_{z})\Big|r_{z}\right)\leq C\left(1+\log\frac{|x-y|}{2r_{z}}\right)^{1-\theta+\eta},\quad\quad z=x,y.

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,\forall z=x,y,\Xi_{a}^{z}\subset D_{z}\right)\leq C\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{z=x,y}\left(\log\frac{|x-y|}{2r_{z}}\right)^{1-\theta+\eta}\right].

In
[
JLQ23
]
, we show that the above quantity is upper bounded by
C|\log|x-y||^{2(1-\theta)+2\eta}
(see (4.16) in Remark 4.5 therein) concluding the proof of Lemma
4.2
.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
4.3
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Let
x
and
y
be two distinct points in
D
. Compare to the upper bound, the two points
x
and
y
are fixed and we wish to take the limit as
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\to 0
. It is therefore important for the proof to keep in mind that
x
and
y
are at macroscopic distance to each other and that we can assume
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
to be as small as desired. We start by proving (
4.3
). We will explain at the end of the proof what needs to be changed in order to get (
4.4
) and (
4.6
).

Let
\eta>0
be small and let
D_{x}=D(x,\eta|x-y|/2)
and
D_{y}=D(y,\eta|x-y|/2)
. By taking
\eta>0
small enough, we can ensure that
D_{x}
and
D_{y}
are subsets of
D
. We will denote
\mathcal{L}_{D,x,y}^{\theta}=\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\setminus(\mathcal{L}_{D_{x}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{L}_{D_{y}}^{\theta})
the collection of loops which are not included in
D_{x}
or
D_{y}
.
We now introduce the following events:

•

E_{1}
: there is a loop in
\mathcal{L}_{D,x,y}^{\theta}
that disconnects
D_{x}
from
D_{y}
;

•

E_{2}
: for
z=x,y
,
\Xi_{a}^{z}\subset D_{z}
.

We first claim that we can work on
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
. Indeed, by Lemma
2.5

\mathbb{P}\left(E_{1}^{c}\right)\to 0
as
\eta\to 0
and, by FKG inequality,

\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{1}^{c}\right)\to 0\quad\quad\text{as}\quad\eta\to 0.

Moreover, in the proof of the upper bound, we showed that

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{2}^{c}\right)=0.

See (
4.10
).
We can therefore work on the event
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
. On the event
E_{1}
, there is at most one cluster
\mathcal{C}_{x,y}
of loops in
\mathcal{L}_{D,x,y}^{\theta}
that intersects both
D_{x}
and
D_{y}
(we set
\mathcal{C}_{x,y}=\varnothing
if there is no such cluster).
By the restriction property (
2.16
) of
\Xi_{a}^{z}
, we have
