\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{1}\cap E_{2}\right)=\frac{\CR(x,D_{x})^{a}\CR(y,D_{y})^{a}}{\CR(x,D)^{a}\CR(y,D)^{a}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{E_{1}}\prod_{z=x,y}\mathbb{P}\left(z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,D_{z}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{x,y}\Big|\mathcal{C}_{x,y}\right)\right].

The ratio of conformal radii converges to 1. Moreover, for
z=x
or
y
, we can rewrite

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\Big(z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,D_{z}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{x,y}|\mathcal{C}_{x,y}\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,D_{z}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{x,y}|\mathcal{C}_{x,y}\Big)\Big/\mathbb{P}\Big(z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D_{z}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,D_{z}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\partial D(z,e^{-1}\eta|x-y|/2)\Big)

and we can replace

\mathcal{C}_{x,y}
by the closure of
(\mathcal{C}_{x,y}\cap D_{z})\cup\partial D_{z}
which is a.s. a connected compact subset of
\overline{D_{z}}\setminus\{z\}
. We are now in the setting of Theorem
3.1
which shows that the above ratio converges as
a\to 0
, a.s. with respect to
\mathcal{C}_{x,y}
.
The upper bound (
4.2
) provides the bound necessary to apply dominated convergence in order to exchange the expectation w.r.t.
\mathcal{C}_{x,y}
and the limit. We have proved that

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y,E_{1}\cap E_{2}\right)

(4.12)

exists and that

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\Big(x\overset{\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y}}{\longleftrightarrow}y\Big)

converges as
\gamma\to 0
towards the nondecreasing limit of (
4.12
) as
\eta\to 0
. This shows (
4.3
).

The proof of (
4.4
) concerning the mixed case
(a_{1},a_{2})
follows along the same lines. The main difference comes from the fact that we only need a lower bound, so we can add for free the extra events
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
.
The proof of (
4.6
) is obtained similarly. Indeed, because the function
F
does not depend on loops that have diameter smaller than a given threshold
\varepsilon
,
F
does not depend on loops included in
D_{x}
and
D_{y}
provided
\eta
is small enough. We then repeat the above procedure and use Theorem
3.1
.

Finally, the upper bound of (
4.5
) follows from Lemma
4.2
. The lower bound is much easier to prove and follows from Theorem
2.4
and FKG inequality. We omit the details.
∎

5
h_{\theta}
as a sum of Minkowski measures: proof of Theorems
1.9
and
1.10

This section is devoted to the proofs of Theorems
1.9
and
1.10
. We start by proving Theorem
1.9
. Recall the notations introduced above Theorem
1.9
.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Let
k\geq 1
.
Let
B\subset D
be some Borel set. It is enough to show that
(\mu_{k,r}(B))_{r>0}
and
(\mu_{k,\gamma}(B))_{\gamma>0}
are Cauchy in
L^{2}
and that
\mu_{k,r}(B)-\mu_{k,\gamma}(B)
converges to 0 in
L^{2}
as
\gamma,r\to 0
. We already have all the ingredients to prove this. Indeed,

\displaystyle\limsup_{r,r^{\prime}\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[(\mu_{k,r}(B)-\mu_{k,r^{\prime}}(B))^{2}\right]=\limsup_{r,r^{\prime}\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{r}Z_{r^{\prime}}}\int_{B\times B}\Big(\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{k}\cap D(x,r)\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{C}_{k}\cap D(y,r)\neq\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle~~~+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{k}\cap D(x,r^{\prime})\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{C}_{k}\cap D(y,r^{\prime})\neq\varnothing\right)-2\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{C}_{k}\cap D(x,r)\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{C}_{k}\cap D(y,r^{\prime})\neq\varnothing\right)\Big)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

The analogue of Lemma
4.2
with small discs instead of thick loops (and Fatou’s lemma to deal with the mixed term
r
-
r^{\prime}
) provides the necessary domination to exchange limit and integral. To show that the integrand converges pointwise to zero, we then use the same approach as in the proof of Proposition
4.3
. Eventually, the proof of Theorem
1.9
boils down to Theorem
3.1
. We omit the details.

The above line of argument also shows that the second moment of
\mu_{k}(D)
is positive. In particular, the probability that
\mu_{k}(D)>0
is positive. We are going to show that this probability is actually equal to 1.
Without loss of generality assume that
D=[0,1]^{2}
.
Let
\wp
be some fixed macroscopic loop of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
, e.g. the loop with the largest diameter. Let
n\geq 1
be a large integer and divide
D
into disjoint squares
Q_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,4^{n}
, of side length
2^{-n}
. For each
i=1,\dots,4^{n}
, let
\mathcal{L}_{i}
be the set of loops included in
Q_{i}
. Conditionally on
\{\text{diam}(\wp)>\sqrt{2}2^{-n}\}
, the collections
\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,4^{n}
, are independent from
\wp
and independent loop soups in
D_{i}
.
For
i=1,\dots,4^{n}
, let
Q_{i}^{\prime}
be the square with side length
2^{-n-1}
with the same centre as
Q_{i}
and let
E_{i}
be the event that there is a cluster
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
with non-zero Minkowski content and such that
\mathcal{C}_{i}
disconnects
Q_{i}^{\prime}
from
\partial Q_{i}
. By scale invariance,
p=\mathbb{P}(E_{i})
is positive and independent of
n
and
i
.
Let
I=\{i=1,\dots,4^{n}:\wp\cap Q_{i}^{\prime}\neq\varnothing\}
. On the event
E_{i}\cap\{i\in I\}
, the cluster of
\wp
contains
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Hence, the probability that the Minkowski content of the cluster of
\wp
vanishes is at most

\mathbb{P}\left(\text{diam}(\wp)\leq\sqrt{2}2^{-n}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left[(1-p)^{\#I}\right]\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

This concludes the proof.
∎

The main item that remains to be checked is that, as stated in Theorem
1.10
,
\mu_{k}
corresponds to the restriction of
|h_{\theta}|
to
\mathcal{C}_{k}
. The rest of this section is dedicated to showing this.

As usually, we will denote by
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
the topological closure of

\mathcal{C}_{k}
, seen as a compact subset of
D
.
We will denote

D_{k}=D\setminus\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{j}.

Then
D_{k}
is an open subset of
D
that has infinitely many connected components,
among which finitely many (at most
k
) are multiply connected and the rest are simply connected, the total number of holes being
k
.
We will denote by

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}=\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D}:\wp\subset D_{k}\}.

We will denote by
\sigma_{k}
the sign
\sigma_{\mathcal{C}_{k}}
of the cluster

\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
We will denote by
\mathcal{F}_{k}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the loops contained in

\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k}
(including their time parametrization)
and the signs
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Fix
k\geq 1
.
Let
O_{i}
,
i\geq 0
be the connected components of
D_{k}
.
Conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, we have:

•

The collections of loops
\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}:\wp\subset O_{i}\},i\geq 0
, are independent;

•

For all
i\geq 0
such that
O_{i}
is simply connected,

\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}:\wp\subset O_{i}\}
has the law of a Brownian loop soup in
O_{i}
with intensity
\theta
;

•

For all
i\geq 0
such that
O_{i}
is not simply connected,

\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}:\wp\subset O_{i}\}
has the law of a Brownian loop soup in
O_{i}
with intensity
\theta
conditioned on not having clusters surrounding an inner hole of
O_{i}
.

Proof.

This result is folklore and has a very similar flavour as Lemma
8.5
and follows from the same proof. We refer to Section
8
for more details.
∎

In particular, the collection of loops
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
is a Brownian loop soup in

D_{k}
conditioned on an event that has a positive probability.
So for
\gamma\in(0,2)
, one can define a multiplicative chaos measure associated to

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
.
The fact that
D_{k}
is not simply connected is non essential: see Remark
2.2
.
The multiplicative chaos measure associated to
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}

is nothing else than

\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in D_{k}\right\}}e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(dx),

where
G_{D}
is the Dirichlet Green’s function on
D

and
G_{D_{k}}
is the Dirichlet Green’s function on
D_{k}
.
Note that
G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x)<+\infty
.
We get a density and not just a restriction to
D_{k}
because our convention

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(dx)]=2dx
makes the normalization for the multiplicative chaos domain-dependent: how we normalize around a point
x
depends also on the global shape of the domain and not just the Brownian loops we see around
x
. With the normalisation used in
[
ABJL23
]
, we would not get this extra density: see (
2.5
) for the relation between these two normalisations.
One can further consider the measure
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}\mathbf{1}_{D_{k}}

and the field

h_{\theta,k}=\lim_{\gamma\to 0^{+}}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in D_{k}\right\}}(e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(dx)-dx),

which is a well defined random element of
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})

for
\varepsilon>0
(simply by working in the domain
D_{k}
).

Further, one can consider the conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]
.
Since
\mathbb{E}[\|h_{\theta}\|^{2}_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}]<+\infty

for
\varepsilon>0
,
the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]

is a well defined random element of
H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})
,
with

\|\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]\|^{2}_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}\leq\mathbb{E}[\|h_{\theta}\|^{2}_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]<+\infty~~\text{a.s.}

See
[
HvNVW16
, Section 2.6]
for a reference on conditional expectation of random variables in a Banach space.
We define
\nu_{1}=\sigma_{1}\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{1}]
,
and for
k\geq 2
,

\nu_{k}=\sigma_{k}(\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]-\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]),\quad\quad\text{so~that}\quad\quad\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{j}\nu_{j}.

Lemma 5.2
.

The following holds.

1.

For every
k\geq 1
,
conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
h_{\theta,k}
has the same law as
-h_{\theta,k}
,
and in particular

\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta,k}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0
.

2.

A.s., for every
k\geq 1

and every
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C})
, such that

\operatorname{Supp}(f)\cap(D\setminus D_{k})=\varnothing
,

(h_{\theta},f)=(h_{\theta,k},f)
.

3.

For every
k\geq 1
,

h_{\theta}=\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]+h_{\theta,k}
a.s.

4.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, as
k\to+\infty
,

\|h_{\theta}-\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]\|_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}\to 0
a.s. and in
L^{2}
.

5.

For every
k\geq 2
,

h_{\theta,k-1}=\sigma_{k}\nu_{k}+h_{\theta,k}
a.s. and

\nu_{k}=\sigma_{k}\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta,k-1}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]~~\text{a.s.}

6.

\nu_{1}
and
\sigma_{1}
are independent,
and for every
k\geq 2
,

\nu_{k}
and
\sigma_{k}
are independent conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
.

7.

A.s., for every
k\geq 1

and every
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C})
such that

\operatorname{Supp}(f)\cap\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k}=\varnothing
,

(\nu_{k},f)=0
,
that is to say
\nu_{k}
is supported on
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
.

Proof.

1. The proof is the same as for
h_{\theta}
(see Section
7.1
).

2. Note that we also deal with test functions that are not compactly supported in
D
.
Let
k\geq 1
and
n\geq 1
.
Let
\mathbb{C}_{k,n}
be the open subset of
\mathbb{C}

formed by points
x\in\mathbb{C}
such that

d(x,D\setminus D_{k})>1/n
.
By construction, for every
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C}_{k,n})
,

(h_{\theta},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0^{+}}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{D_{k}}f(x)(\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(dx)-dx),

where the convergence holds in
L^{2}
for a given Sobolev norm

H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})

(and thus for all test functions simultaneously).
Further, since
G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x)
is bounded on
\mathbb{C}_{k,n}
,
the convergence and the limit do no change if we replace
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}

by

e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(dx).

3. By 1., we only need to show that
h_{\theta}-h_{\theta,k}
is
measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}_{k}
.
Actually will see that
h_{\theta}-h_{\theta,k}
is
measurable w.r.t. the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{k}
augmented by the negligible events (i.e. with probability
0
),
which also implies the desired result.

Consider the sets
\mathbb{C}_{k,n}
defined above.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{k,n}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{F}_{k}

as well as by the loops of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
that are in the clusters at distance at most
1/n
from
D\setminus D_{k}
, and the signs of these clusters.
By definition,
\mathcal{F}_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}_{k,n}
and
\mathcal{F}_{k,n+1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{k,n}
.
Denote

\mathcal{F}_{k,\infty}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{k,n}.

We have
\mathcal{F}_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}_{k,\infty}
.

For
k\geq 1
and
n\geq 1
, let
\chi_{k,n}
be a cutoff function
such that
\chi_{k,n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
,
\chi_{k,n}
takes values in
[0,1]
,

\chi_{k,n}
equals
0
on
\mathbb{C}_{k,n}
and
1
on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{C}_{k,n+1}
.
We also want
\chi_{k,n}
to be, as a random variable, measurable w.r.t.

D_{k}
.
We omit the detailed construction of such a cutoff function, which is standard.
Fix
k\geq 1
and
n\geq 1
. For
m\geq 1
we have

h_{\theta}=h_{\theta}\chi_{k,m}+h_{\theta}(1-\chi_{k,m})
,
and by 2.,
h_{\theta}(1-\chi_{k,m})=h_{\theta,k}(1-\chi_{k,m})
.
Moreover, for
m\geq n
,
h_{\theta}\chi_{k,m}

is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}_{k,n}
.
Further, for
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|h_{\theta,k}\chi_{k,m}\|_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0~~\text{a.s.}

by a computation similar to (
4.8
).
Therefore,

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|(h_{\theta}-h_{\theta,k})-h_{\theta}\chi_{k,m}\|_{H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{C})}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0~~\text{a.s.}

So in particular,
h_{\theta}-h_{\theta,k}
is the limit in probability of
h_{\theta}\chi_{k,m}
as
m\to+\infty
.
Therefore,
h_{\theta}-h_{\theta,k}
is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}_{k,n}
.
By letting
n\to+\infty
, we get that

h_{\theta}-h_{\theta,k}
is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}_{k,\infty}
.

To conclude, it is enough to check that the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{k,\infty}

is the same as
\mathcal{F}_{k}
up to negligible events.
This is what is sketched below, by omitting some details.

•

Step 1: Let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,n}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{F}_{k}

as well as by the loops of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
that are in the clusters at distance at most
1/n
from
D\setminus D_{k}
.
Contrary to
\mathcal{F}_{k,n}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,n}
does not contain any information on the
sign of clusters of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
. Denote

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,\infty}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,n}.

By construction,
\mathcal{F}_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,\infty}\subset\mathcal{F}_{k,\infty}
.
We further claim that
\mathcal{F}_{k,\infty}
is the same as

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,\infty}
up to negligible events.
Indeed, the signs of clusters are sampled independently
of the loops in
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
and are i.i.d.
Moreover, as
n
increases,
\mathcal{F}_{k,n}
contains information about less clusters and their signs, and in particular,
\mathcal{F}_{k,\infty}
contains
information only on the tail of the sequence of signs.
So essentially this follows from the
0-1
law of the tails of sequences of
independent r.v.s.

•

Step 2: Let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,n}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{F}_{k}

as well as by the loops of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
that are at distance at most
1/n
from
D\setminus D_{k}
.
So contrary to
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,n}
, we look only at individual loops and do not consider how they are grouped into clusters.
By construction,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,n}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,n}
.
Denote

\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,\infty}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,n}.

We claim that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,\infty}
is the same as

\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,\infty}
up to negligible events.
For
m>n\geq 1
, let
A_{k,m,n}
be the event that all the clusters of

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
that are at distance at most
1/m
from
D\setminus D_{k}
,
are actually made of loops that stay in the
1/n
-neighborhood of

D\setminus D_{k}
.
Then
A_{k,m,n}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,n}
and for every
A\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,m}
,
we have
A\cap A_{k,m,n}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,n}
. Moreover,

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}(A_{k,m,n}^{\rm c})=0.

Thus,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{k,\infty}
is contained in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,n}
augmented by the negligible
events, whatever the value of
n
.

•

Step 3: We have that
\mathcal{F}_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,\infty}
.
We claim that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{k,\infty}
is the same as
\mathcal{F}_{k}
up to negligible events.
If the conditional distribution of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D,k}
given
\mathcal{F}_{k}

were exactly Poisson, this would have been immediate by
0-1
law.
However, by Lemma
5.1
,
this conditional distribution is Poisson conditioned on an event with positive probability, so the
0-1
law still holds.

4. This is simply due to the fact that the
\sigma
-algebra

\bigvee_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{k}
generated by the union of the
\mathcal{F}_{k}

is the total
\sigma
-algebra generated by the loop soup

\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D}
and the signs clusters.

5. Since

\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]+h_{\theta,k-1}=\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]+\sigma_{k}\nu_{k}+h_{\theta,k}=h_{\theta},

we get that
\sigma_{k}\nu_{k}=h_{\theta,k-1}-h_{\theta,k}
.
Since
\sigma_{k}\nu_{k}
is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-measurable
and
\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta,k}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0
,
we conclude by taking the conditional expectation w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}_{k}
.

6. Since
-h_{\theta}
has the same law as
h_{\theta}
, we have that

\nu_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sigma_{1}=-1\right\}}=-\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{1}]\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sigma_{1}=-1\right\}}=\mathbb{E}[-h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{1}]\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sigma_{1}=-1\right\}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(d)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{1}]\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sigma_{1}=1\right\}}=\nu_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sigma_{1}=1\right\}},

which gives the independence between
\nu_{1}
and
\sigma_{1}
.
For
k\geq 2
, we use the same reasoning, by relying on points 5. and 1.

7. From 3. and 2. follows that a.s. for every

f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1})
,

(\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta}|\mathcal{F}_{1}],f)=0
.
So this concludes in the case
k=1
.
Now take
k\geq 2
. By 5., for every

f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C})
,

\sigma_{k}(\nu_{k},f)=(h_{\theta,k-1}-h_{\theta,k},f).

Then, similarly to 2., we get that

(h_{\theta,k-1},f)=(h_{\theta,k},f)

for every
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k})
.
∎

By convention, we set
D_{0}=D
.
Let
D_{k}^{\ast}
be the open subset of
D_{k}
obtained as the union of the
non-simply connected components of
D_{k}
, which are finitely many.

Lemma 5.3
.

The measure

\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in D_{k}^{\ast}\right\}}dx-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{x\in D_{k}^{\ast}}e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(dx))|\mathcal{F}_{k}])

(5.1)

is a.s. a positive finite measure that converges a.s. to
0
for the weak topology of measures on

\overline{D_{k}^{\ast}}
as
\gamma\to 0
.

Proof.

We start by proving that (
5.1
) is a positive measure.
Let
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D_{k}^{\ast}}
be the collection of Brownian loops,
distributed conditionally on
D_{k}
,
as the Brownian loop soup in
D_{k}^{\ast}
with intensity
\theta

(that is without any topological conditioning).
Let
\mathcal{M}_{D_{k}^{\ast},\gamma}
denote the multiplicative chaos
of
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D_{k}^{\ast}}
, normalized by

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{M}_{D_{k}^{\ast},\gamma}|D_{k}]=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in D_{k}^{\ast}\right\}}2dx
.
Let
E_{k}
be the event that no cluster of loops in
\mathcal{L}^{\theta}_{D_{k}^{\ast}}

surrounds an inner hole of
D_{k}^{\ast}
,
which is an event with positive probability.
Then

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{x\in D_{k}^{\ast}}e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(dx))|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=\dfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{D_{k}^{\ast},\gamma}(dx)\Big|D_{k}^{\ast},E_{k}\Big].

Note that the Dirichlet Green’s function on
D_{k}^{\ast}

is the restriction of
G_{D_{k}}
to

D_{k}^{\ast}\times D_{k}^{\ast}
.
The event
E_{k}
is a decreasing event.
Therefore, by FKG inequality for Poisson point processes, a.s.

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{x\in D_{k}^{\ast}}e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{+}(dx))|\mathcal{F}_{k}]\\
\leq\dfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}_{D_{k}^{\ast},\gamma}(dx)\Big|D_{k}^{\ast}\Big]=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in D_{k}^{\ast}\right\}}e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}dx.

Thus, for every
\gamma\in(0,2)
,
(
5.1
) is a positive finite measure.

The rest of the proof is then dedicated to showing that (
5.1
) goes to zero as
\gamma\to 0
. Define
f(x)=e^{2\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}
.
We can write the total mass of (
5.1
) as

\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{D_{k}^{\ast}}(1-e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))})dx-\dfrac{1}{2Z_{\gamma}\mathbb{P}(E_{k})}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big((\mathcal{M}_{D_{k}^{\ast},\gamma},f^{\gamma^{2}/2})-2\int_{D_{k}^{\ast}}f^{\gamma^{2}/2}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{E_{k}}\Big|D_{k}^{\ast}\Big].

(5.2)

We are going to show separately that each of these two terms vanishes as
\gamma\to 0
. By Cauchy–Schwarz and then by (
2.13
), the absolute value of the second term is at most

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2Z_{\gamma}\mathbb{P}(E_{k})^{1/2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big((\mathcal{M}_{D_{k}^{\ast},\gamma},f^{\gamma^{2}/2})-2\int_{D_{k}^{\ast}}f^{\gamma^{2}/2}\Big)^{2}\Big|D_{k}^{\ast}\Big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{Z_{\gamma}\mathbb{P}(E_{k})^{1/2}}\left(\int_{D_{k}^{\ast}\times D_{k}^{\ast}}\big((\gamma^{2}\pi G_{D_{k}^{\ast}}(x,y))^{1-\theta}\Gamma(\theta)I_{\theta-1}(2\gamma^{2}\pi G_{D_{k}^{\ast}}(x,y))-1\big)f(x)^{\gamma^{2}/2}f(y)^{\gamma^{2}/2}\,dx\,dy\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=O(\gamma^{2}/Z_{\gamma}).

The last estimate can be obtained by expanding
I_{\theta-1}
(see (
2.11
)).
Hence the second term of (
5.2
) vanishes as
\gamma\to 0
.

To conclude the proof, it remains to show that

\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{D_{k}^{\ast}}(1-e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))})dx\to 0\quad\quad\text{as}\quad\gamma\to 0.

(5.3)

For fixed
x\in D_{k}^{\ast}
, the density

1-e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))})

is of order
\gamma^{2}
.
Since
Z_{\gamma}\gg\gamma^{2}
, the density converges to
0
,
actually uniformly on compact subsets of
D_{k}^{\ast}
.
However, this is not enough for our purpose and we need to control what happens
near the boundary
\partial D_{k}^{\ast}
.

The boundary
\partial D_{k}^{\ast}
has exactly
k+1
connected components,
one of them being
\partial D
, and the
k
others being the
outer boundaries of
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k}
.

\partial D
and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k-1}
lie outside both
D_{k-1}
and
D_{k}
. Thus
G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x)
stays bounded in a neighborhood of

\partial D\cup\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathcal{C}_{k-1}
. Therefore,
the uniform convergence of the density to
0

is also true on compact neighborhoods of these
k
boundary components of

\partial D_{k}^{\ast}
.
On the other hand,
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is contained in
D_{k-1}
and lies outside of
D_{k}
. We will therefore have to treat this case separately.

Let us denote by
\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k}
the outer boundary of
\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
Near
\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k}
,
G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x)
explodes to
+\infty
.
But there is a constant
c(D_{k-1})\in(0,1)
, depending on
D_{k-1}
,
such that for every
x\in D_{k}^{\ast}
,

e^{2\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\geq c(D_{k-1})\dfrac{d(x,\partial D_{k}^{\ast})}{d(x,\partial D_{k-1})}.

This is in a way a generalization of Koebe quarter theorem to domains with holes; see
[
ALS20a
, Lemma 5.13]
.
Fix
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,d(\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k},\partial D_{k}^{\ast}\setminus\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k}))
.
Then for every
x\in D_{k}^{\ast}
such that

d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})<1\wedge(\varepsilon_{0}/2)
, we have

\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}(1-e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))})\leq\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\Big(1-c(D_{k-1})^{\gamma^{2}/2}\dfrac{d(x,\partial D_{k}^{\ast})^{\gamma^{2}/2}}{d(x,\partial D_{k-1})^{\gamma^{2}/2}}\Big)\\
\leq\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\Big(1-c(D_{k-1})^{\gamma^{2}/2}\dfrac{d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2}}{\operatorname{diam}(D)^{\gamma^{2}/2}}\Big)=\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}(1-d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2})+\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\Big(1-\dfrac{c(D_{k-1})^{\gamma^{2}/2}}{\operatorname{diam}(D)^{\gamma^{2}/2}}\Big)d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2}

Since

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\Big(1-\dfrac{c(D_{k-1})^{\gamma^{2}/2}}{\operatorname{diam}(D)^{\gamma^{2}/2}}\Big)=0,

we only need to consider the term

(1-d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2})/Z_{\gamma}
.
Further,

\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})<1}(1-d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2})\,dx=\dfrac{\gamma^{2}}{2Z_{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{1}dr\,r^{\gamma^{2}/2-1}\int_{d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})<r}dx

(5.4)

Since
\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k}
is an SLE
κ(θ)
type curve,
it is of dimension
1+\kappa(\theta)/8\leq 3/2<2

[
Bef08
]
.
In particular, for
\beta\in(0,1/2)
, there is a.s. a (random) constant

c(\beta)>0
such that for every
r\in(0,1)
,

\int_{d(x,\partial_{o}\mathcal{C}_{k})<r}dx\leq c(\beta)r^{\beta}.

Thus, (
5.4
) is bounded by

c(\beta)\dfrac{\gamma^{2}}{2Z_{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{1}r^{\gamma^{2}/2+\beta-1}\,dr,

which tends to
0
as
\gamma
to
0
. This concludes.
∎

We now have all the ingredients to prove Theorem
1.10
.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

The only thing that remains to be proved is that the random generalised function

\nu_{k}
agrees a.s. with the measure
\mu_{k}
from Theorem
1.9
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C})
. It is enough to show that

(\nu_{k},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{d(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})<1}f(x)(1-d(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2})\,dx,

(5.5)

in probability.
We have that

(\nu_{k},f)=\sigma_{k}\mathbb{E}[(h_{\theta,k-1},f)|\mathcal{F}_{k}],

where by convention,
h_{\theta,0}=h_{\theta}
.
Since
\nu_{k}
and
\sigma_{k}
are independent conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
we get that

(\nu_{k},f)=-\mathbb{E}[(h_{\theta,k-1},f)|\mathcal{F}_{k},\{\sigma_{k}=-1\}]

(5.6)

Since

(h_{\theta,k-1},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{D_{k-1}}f(x)(e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}(dx)-dx),

where the convergence is a.s. in
L^{2}
conditionally on

\mathcal{F}_{k-1}

(that is to say
\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{E}[(\cdot)^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]=0
a.s.).
Therefore, in (
5.6
) one can interchange the conditional expectation and the limit in
\gamma
and get

(\nu_{k},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\Big(\int_{D_{k-1}}f(x)\,dx-\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{D_{k-1}}f(x)e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}(dx)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{k},\{\sigma_{k}=-1\}\Big]\Big),

where the convergence is a.s. in
L^{2}
conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
.
By Theorem
2.4
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
has a.s. zero Lebesgue measure (the expectation of the Lebesgue measure of its
r
-neighbourhood vanishes as
r\to 0
). So
\int_{D_{k-1}}f(x)\,dx=\int_{D_{k}}f(x)\,dx
a.s.
Moreover,
since on the event
\{\sigma_{k}=-1\}
,

\mathbf{1}_{\left\{D_{k-1}\right\}}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{D_{k}\right\}}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}
,
and since
\sigma_{k}
is independent from
\mathbf{1}_{\left\{D_{k}\right\}}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}

conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
we get that

(\nu_{k},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\Big(\int_{D_{k}}f(x)\,dx-\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{D_{k}}f(x)e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}(dx)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]\Big).

Now we partition
D_{k}=D_{k}^{0}\cup D_{k}^{\ast}
,
where
D_{k}^{0}
is the union of the simply connected components of

D_{k}
(there are infinitely many of them) and
D_{k}^{\ast}

is the union of the multiply connected components of

D_{k}
(there are finitely many of them).
Then Lemma
5.3
ensures that

(\nu_{k},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\Big(\int_{D_{k}^{0}}f(x)\,dx-\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{D_{k}^{0}}f(x)e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}(dx)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]\Big).

Since conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
the loops in
D_{k}^{0}
form a Poisson point process without conditioning,
we get that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{D_{k}^{0}}f(x)e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\mathcal{M}^{+}_{\gamma}(dx)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{k}\Big]=\int_{D_{k}^{0}}f(x)e^{\gamma^{2}\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\,dx.

The function
G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k}}(x,x)
is bounded for
x

away from
\mathcal{C}_{k}
and explodes when
x
approaches
\partial\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
Further,
[
ALS20a
, Lemma 5.13]
provides a comparison with the Euclidean
distance:
there is a constant
c(D_{k-1})\in(0,1)

depending on
D_{k-1}

such that for every
x\in D_{k}^{0}
,

c(D_{k-1})\dfrac{d(x,\partial D_{k}^{0})}{d(x,\partial D_{k-1})}\leq e^{2\pi(G_{D_{k}}(x,x)-G_{D_{k-1}}(x,x))}\leq 4\dfrac{d(x,\partial D_{k}^{0})}{d(x,\partial D_{k-1})}.

Then using these bounds,
similarly to the proof of (
5.3
),
we get that

(\nu_{k},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in D_{k}^{0}\\
d(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})<1\end{subarray}}f(x)(1-d(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2})\,dx.

Since the dimension of the outer boundary of

\mathcal{C}_{k}
is strictly smaller than
2
,
we get that (see the proof of (
5.3
))

(\nu_{k},f)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\dfrac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}d(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})<1\end{subarray}}f(x)(1-d(x,\mathcal{C}_{k})^{\gamma^{2}/2})\,dx,

which concludes.
∎

6
Excursions from the boundary of a cluster

In this section, we will obtain estimates about the excursions induced by the Brownian loops that touch the outer boundary of a loop soup cluster.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a loop soup with intensity
\theta\in(0,1/2]
in the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{0}
be the outermost cluster in
\mathcal{L}
that encircles the origin. Let
O
be the domain encircled by the outermost boundary of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.
We will denote by
\mathcal{L}_{O}
the subset of
\mathcal{L}
consisting in loops included in
\overline{O}
.
The set of loops in
\mathcal{L}_{O}
that touch
\partial O
induce a collection
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{L}_{O})
of excursions in
O
with endpoints in
\partial O
. If one maps
O
onto
\mathbb{H}
by some conformal map
f
, then we say that
f(\mathcal{L}_{O})
is a
loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
with wired boundary conditions
. It was shown in
[
QW19
]
that the law of
f(\mathcal{L}_{O})
is conformally invariant, and does not depend on the choice of
f
. Moreover, the following properties were proved in
[
QW19
,
QW18
]
.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
QW19
,
QW18
]
)
.

The law of
f(\mathcal{L}_{O})
is independent from
\partial O
and the distribution of
\mathcal{L}
outside of
O
.
Inside
f(\mathcal{L}_{O})
(a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
at intensity
\theta\in(0,1/2]
with wired boundary conditions), the set of loops that touch
\mathbb{R}
are independent from the set of loops that stay in
\mathbb{H}
. The latter set is distributed as a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
(with free boundary conditions). Let
\mathcal{E}
be the collection of excursions induced by the loops in
f(\mathcal{L}_{O})
that touch
\mathbb{R}
. Then at
\theta=1/2
,
\mathcal{E}
is distributed as a Poisson point process of excursions of intensity
1/4
.

As one knows precisely the law of
\mathcal{E}
for
\theta=1/2
, much less is known for the subcritical intensities
\theta\in(0,1/2)
. However,
[
Qia19
]
proved that the excursions attached to the boundary of a cluster satisfy a conformal restriction property that we describe now (we give some background on conformal restriction measures in Section
6.1
).
One can explore the outer boundaries of the outermost clusters in
\mathcal{L}
progressively, instead of discovering an entire cluster at once, using the CLE exploration
[
SW12
]
. This exploration process allows one to define a
loop soup wired on a portion of its boundary
(see
[
Qia19
]
for a precise definition). Such a configuration was shown to be conformally invariant (with two marked points on the boundary). As a consequence, it is sufficient to consider a loop soup in the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}
which is wired on
\mathbb{R}^{-}
and free on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Theorem 6.2

(
[
Qia19
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
be a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
with intensity
\theta\in(0,1/2]
which is wired on
\mathbb{R}^{-}
and free on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. The set of loops in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
that touch
\mathbb{R}^{-}
are independent from the set of loops that stay in
\mathbb{H}
. The latter set is distributed as a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
(with free boundary conditions). Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
be the collection of excursions induced by the loops in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
that touch
\mathbb{R}
. Then the filling of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
(namely the complement in
\mathbb{C}
of the unbounded connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus K
, where
K
is the closure of the union of all the excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
) satisfies one-sided restriction in
\mathbb{H}
with exponent
\alpha(\theta):=(6-\kappa)/(2\kappa)
, where
\kappa\in(8/3,4]
is related to
\theta
via (
1.1
).

Throughout this section, we use the following notations:

Notation 6.3
.

For any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, denote by
\mathcal{L}_{I}
a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
wired on
I
and free elsewhere. We will use
\mathcal{E}_{I}
to denote the boundary-touching excursions induced by
\mathcal{L}_{I}
.

For
z\in\mathbb{H}
, let
f_{z}(u)=(u-z)/(u-\bar{z})
be the conformal map from
\mathbb{H}
onto
\mathbb{D}
that sends
z
to
0
. For all
r\in(0,1)
, let
U(z,r):=f_{z}^{-1}(D(0,r))
.

Our main result on the boundary excursions in a cluster with any intensity
\theta\in(0,1/2]
is encapsulated in Proposition
6.4
below. This proposition will be crucial to define and study the field
h_{\theta}
with wired boundary condition.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
be a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
with intensity
\theta\in(0,1/2]
with wired boundary conditions. Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
be the Brownian excursions in
\mathbb{H}
attached on
\mathbb{R}
, induced by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
For all
\eta>0
, there exists
C>0
, such that the following holds. For all
z\in\mathbb{H}
and
r\in(0,1-\eta)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(z,r)\not=\emptyset]\leq C|\log r|^{-1}.

(6.1)

For all
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{H}
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1-\eta)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(z_{1},r_{1})\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset]\leq C(1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2}))|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}.

(6.2)

In the special case of
\theta=1/2
,
Proposition
6.4
can be obtained directly using the Poisson point process description of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We would like to emphasise that, contrary to the restriction property stated in Theorem
6.2
, Proposition
6.4
is not concerned with the outer boundary of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
and requires a deeper understanding of these excursions.
Our approach to prove Proposition
6.4
is to extract information from the restriction property (Theorem
6.2
), and transform this property about the outer boundary of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
to a property about the endpoints of the excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
More concretely, we deduce the following identities which are of independent interest.

Proposition 6.5
.

For each excursion
e
, let
a(e)
and
b(e)
be the left and right endpoints of
e
. For all
r>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}}\frac{(b(e)-a(e))^{2}r^{2}}{(r-b(e))^{2}(r-a(e))^{2}}\right]=\alpha(\theta)

where
\alpha(\theta)
is the restriction exponent from Theorem
6.2
.

A similar but more complicated “second-moment” result is also stated in Proposition
6.16
. Note that Propositions
6.5
and
6.16
are exact identities, not only bounds. Nevertheless, a complete description of the laws of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
remains an open question.

Remark 6.6
.

In Section
6.3
, we prove Propositions
6.5
and
6.16
using only two properties of the set
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
: it satisfies conformal restriction (Theroem
6.2
) and is a locally finite point process of Brownian excursions (Lemma
6.15
). Therefore, Propositions
6.5
and
6.16
also hold for any locally finite point process
\mathcal{E}
of Brownian excursions in
\mathbb{H}
with endpoints on
\mathbb{R}^{-}
, such that the filling of
\mathcal{E}
satisfies one-sided conformal restriction. Then in Sections
6.4
and
6.5
, we prove Proposition
6.4
using the additional property that we can obtain
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
by exploring the outer boundary of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
in a Markovian way.

6.1
Properties and estimates about restriction measures

We will recall some properties of chordal restriction measures introduced in
[
LSW03b
]
, and deduce some estimates which will be useful later.

Fix a simply connected domain
D
which is not the whole plane. Fix two points
a,b\in\partial D
. Let
\Omega
be the collection of all simply connected compact sets
K\subset\overline{D}
, such that
K\cap\partial D=\{a,b\}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the collection of all compact sets
A\subset\overline{D}
such that
D\setminus A
is simply connected and
a,b\not\in A
. For all
A\in\mathcal{Q}
, let
f_{A}
be a conformal map from
D\setminus A
onto
D
that leaves
a,b
fixed.
A probability law on
K\in\Omega
is said to satisfy chordal conformal restriction if

1.

The law of
K
is invariant under conformal maps from
D
to itself that fixes
a,b
.

2.

For any
A\in\mathcal{Q}
, conditionally on
K\cap A=\emptyset
,
f_{A}(K)
is distributed as
K
.

It was shown in
[
LSW03b
]
that the family of chordal restriction measures is characterized by one parameter
\alpha\geq 5/8
. A chordal restriction measure in
D
with parameter
\alpha
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(K\cap A=\emptyset)=(f_{A}^{\prime}(a)f_{A}^{\prime}(b))^{\alpha}.

(6.3)

The simplest example of chordal restriction measure is provided by:

Proposition 6.7

(
[
LSW03b
]
, Proposition 4.1)
.

Let
D
be a simply connected domain different from
\mathbb{C}
. Let
a,b\in\partial D
be two distinct boundary points. Then the law of the filling of a Brownian excursion from
a
to
b
is the chordal restriction measure in
D
from
a
to
b
with exponent
\alpha=1
.

In the following, we obtain an estimate about a chordal restriction measure in
\mathbb{H}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
a,b,r\in\mathbb{R}
be three distinct boundary points. Let
K
be a chordal restriction measure in
\mathbb{H}
between
a
and
b
with exponent
\alpha
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]=\alpha\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{(r-a)^{2}(r-b)^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{3},

where the
O(1)
term is uniformly bounded for all
\varepsilon\leq 1
by some function of
a,b,r
.
In particular, if
K
is a Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{H}
between
a
and
b
, then
\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]
is given by the above formula with
\alpha=1
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(a,b,r)>0
be small enough so that
\overline{D(r,\varepsilon_{0})}
does not contain
a
or
b
. Since the result is clear if
\varepsilon\in[\varepsilon_{0},1)
, we can assume that
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
.
We define the following conformal map
f_{r,\varepsilon}
from
\mathbb{H}\setminus D(r,\varepsilon)
onto
\mathbb{H}
, and compute its derivative

\displaystyle f_{r,\varepsilon}(z)=z+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{z-r},\quad f_{r,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(z)=1-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(z-r)^{2}}.

(6.4)

If we rescale
f_{r,\varepsilon}
by
(b-a)(f_{r,\varepsilon}(b)-f_{r,\varepsilon}(a))^{-1}
and then do a proper translation, then we obtain a conformal map from
\mathbb{H}\setminus D(r,\varepsilon)
onto
\mathbb{H}
that fixes
a
and
b
.
By (
6.3
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r,\varepsilon)=\emptyset]=\left(f_{r,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(a)f_{r,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(b)\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{(f_{r,\varepsilon}(b)-f_{r,\varepsilon}(a))^{2}}\right)^{\alpha}=\left(1-\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{(r-a)^{2}(r-b)^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{3}\right)^{\alpha},

where the
O(1)
term is bounded by some constant
C(a,b,r)
for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]=1-\mathbb{P}[B\cap D(1,\varepsilon)=\emptyset]
, the lemma follows. The statement concerning Brownian excursion follows from Proposition
6.7
.
∎

Next, we need to obtain the following two-point estimate for chordal restriction measures.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
a,b,r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
be four distinct points. Let
K
be a chordal restriction measure in
\mathbb{H}
between
a
and
b
with exponent
\alpha
. Then

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,K\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)(a-b)^{4}\varepsilon^{4}}{(r_{1}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-a)^{2}(r_{1}-b)^{2}(r_{2}-b)^{2}}\\
+\frac{\alpha(a-b)^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}{(r_{1}-b)^{2}(r_{2}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+\frac{\alpha(a-b)^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}{(r_{1}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-b)^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+O(1)\varepsilon^{5},\end{split}

(6.5)

where the
O(1)
term is uniformly bounded for all
\varepsilon\leq 1
by some function of
a,b,r_{1},r_{2}
.

There can be several ways to deduce Lemma
6.9
. One can apply (
6.3
) to the conformal maps that map out either one or both of the balls
D(r_{1},\varepsilon)
and
D(r_{2},\varepsilon)
. However, a direct computation seems long and complicated.
We opt for a proof of Lemma
6.9
that relates to the trichordal restriction measures introduced in
[
Qia18
]
, which are measures on random sets in a simply connecte domain
D
with three marked points
a,b,c\in\partial D
. Let
\Omega_{3}
be the collection of all simply connected compact set
K\subset\overline{D}
, such that
K\cap\partial D=\{a,b,c\}
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
be the collection of all compact sets
A\subset\overline{D}
such that
D\setminus A
is simply connected and
a,b,c\not\in A
. For all
A\in\mathcal{Q}_{3}
, let
\widetilde{f}_{A}
be the unique conformal map from
D\setminus A
onto
D
that leaves
a,b,c
fixed. A probability law on
K\in\Omega
is said to satisfy trichordal conformal restriction if for any
A\in\mathcal{Q}_{3}
, conditionally on
K\cap A=\emptyset
,
\widetilde{f}_{A}(K)
is distributed as
K
.
It was shown in
[
Qia18
]
that the family of trichordal restriction measures is characterized by three parameter
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
. A trichordal restriction measure in
D
with parameters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
is characterized by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(K\cap A=\emptyset)=\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(a)^{\alpha}\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(b)^{\beta}\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(c)^{\gamma}.

(6.6)

The connection to trichordal restriction measures comes from the following lemma.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
be three distinct points. Suppose that
K
is a chordal restriction measure in
\mathbb{H}
with marked points
a,b
and exponent
\alpha
, then the law of
K
conditionally on
K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset
converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
to that of a trichordal restriction measure in
\mathbb{H}
with marked points
a,b,c
and exponents
\alpha,\alpha,2
.
Moreover, if
a<b<c<r
and
A=D(r,\delta)
, then there exist
\varepsilon_{0},\delta_{0}>0
and a constant
C(a,b,c,r)
such that for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0},\delta\leq\delta_{0}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{P}[K\cap A=\emptyset\mid K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]-\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(a)^{\alpha}\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(b)^{\alpha}\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(c)^{2}\right|\leq C(a,b,c,r)\varepsilon\delta^{2}.

(6.7)

Proof.

Here
D=\mathbb{H}
. For
A\in\mathcal{Q}_{3}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}[K\cap A=\emptyset\mid K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]=\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\mid K\cap A=\emptyset]\frac{\mathbb{P}[K\cap A=\emptyset]}{\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]}\\
&=\mathbb{P}[K\cap\widetilde{f}_{A}(D(c,\varepsilon))\not=\emptyset]\frac{\mathbb{P}[K\cap A=\emptyset]}{\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]}=\frac{\mathbb{P}[K\cap\widetilde{f}_{A}(D(c,\varepsilon))\not=\emptyset]}{\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]}\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(a)^{\alpha}\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(b)^{\alpha}.\end{split}

(6.8)

Since
\widetilde{f}_{A}
is analytic in a neighbourhood of
c
, there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
C=\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime\prime}(c)
, such that for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,

\displaystyle B\big(c,\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(c)\varepsilon-C\varepsilon^{2}\big)\subset\widetilde{f}_{A}(D(c,\varepsilon))\subset B\big(c,\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(c)\varepsilon+C\varepsilon^{2}\big).

By Lemma
6.8
, we can deduce

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}[K\cap\widetilde{f}_{A}(D(c,\varepsilon))\not=\emptyset]}{\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]}=\widetilde{f}_{A}^{\prime}(c)^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon,

(6.9)

where
O(1)
is uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
by a function of
a,b,c,A
.
A trichordal restriction measure
\widetilde{K}
in
\mathbb{H}
with marked points
a,b,c
and exponents
\alpha,\alpha,2
is characterized by the formula
\mathbb{P}[\widetilde{K}\cap A=\emptyset]=f_{A}^{\prime}(a)^{\alpha}f_{A}^{\prime}(b)^{\alpha}f_{A}^{\prime}(c)^{2}
for all
A\in\mathcal{Q}_{3}
. Plugging (
6.9
) back into (
6.8
) proves the weak convergence of the law of
K
conditionally on
K\cap D(c,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset
.
Now, suppose
A=D(r,\delta)
, then
C=C(a,b,c,r)\delta^{2}
for all
\delta\leq(r-c)/2:=\delta_{0}
. One can compute using Lemma
6.8
that the
O(1)
term in (
6.9
) is bounded by
2C(a,b,c,r)\delta^{2}
for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and
\delta\leq\delta_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
K
be a trichordal restriction measure in
\mathbb{H}
with marked points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
and respective exponents
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
. Let
A\in\mathcal{Q}_{3}
and
f:\mathbb{H}\setminus A\to\mathbb{H}
be any conformal map. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap A=\emptyset]=\prod_{i=1}^{3}\left[f^{\prime}(x_{i})f^{\prime}(x_{i+1})\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i+1})^{2}}{(f(x_{i})-f(x_{i+1}))^{2}}\right]^{\frac{\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}-\alpha_{i+2}}{2}},

(6.10)

where we let
x_{4}=x_{1}
,
\alpha_{4}=\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{5}=\alpha_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{A}
be the unique conformal map from
\mathbb{H}\setminus A
onto
\mathbb{H}
that fixes
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
. By (
6.6
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap A=\emptyset]=f_{A}^{\prime}(x_{1})^{\alpha_{1}}f_{A}^{\prime}(x_{2})^{\alpha_{2}}f_{A}^{\prime}(x_{3})^{\alpha_{3}}=\prod_{i=1}^{3}\left(f_{A}^{\prime}(x_{i})f_{A}^{\prime}(x_{i+1})\right)^{\frac{\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}-\alpha_{i+2}}{2}}.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
be the unique conformal map that maps
f(x_{i})
to
x_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, so that
f_{A}=\varphi\circ f
. With explicit computations, we obtain that for
i=1,2,3
,

\varphi^{\prime}(f(x_{i}))=\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i+1})(x_{i}-x_{i+2})(f(x_{i+1})-f(x_{i+2}))}{(x_{i+1}-x_{i+2})(f(x_{i})-f(x_{i+1})(f(x_{i})-f(x_{i+2}))}.

We thus get that, for
i=1,2,3
,

\displaystyle f_{A}^{\prime}(x_{i})f_{A}^{\prime}(x_{i+1})=f^{\prime}(x_{i})f^{\prime}(x_{i+1})\varphi^{\prime}(f(x_{i}))\varphi^{\prime}(f(x_{i+1}))=f^{\prime}(x_{i})f^{\prime}(x_{i+1})\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i+1})^{2}}{(f(x_{i})-f(x_{i+1}))^{2}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

We can now return to the proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.8
, we can assume that
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
are small enough so that
\overline{D(r_{1},\varepsilon_{1})}\cup\overline{D(r_{2},\varepsilon_{2})}
does not contain
a
or
b
.
Note that
\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\not=\emptyset,K\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon_{2})\not=\emptyset]
is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\not=\emptyset]\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon_{2})\not=\emptyset\mid K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\not=\emptyset].

(6.11)

By Lemma
6.8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\not=\emptyset]=\alpha\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{(r_{1}-a)^{2}(r_{1}-b)^{2}}\varepsilon_{1}^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon_{1}^{3}.

(6.12)

By Lemma
6.10
, we know that the law of
K
conditioned on
K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\not=\emptyset
converges as
\varepsilon_{1}\to 0
to the law of a trichordal restriction measure
\widetilde{K}
in
\mathbb{H}
with marked points
a,b,r_{1}
and respective exponents
\alpha,\alpha,2
. More quantitatively, there exist
\varepsilon_{0},\delta_{0}\geq 0
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon_{2})\not=\emptyset\mid K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\not=\emptyset]=\mathbb{P}[\widetilde{K}\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon_{2})\not=\emptyset]+O(1)\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}^{2},

(6.13)

where
O(1)
is bounded by
C(a,b,r_{1},r_{2})
for all for all
\varepsilon_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{2}\leq\delta_{0}
. Let
f_{r_{2},\varepsilon}:\mathbb{H}\setminus D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{H}
be the map defined in (
6.4
). By (
6.4
), we have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(a)f^{\prime}_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(b)\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{(f_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(a)-f_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(b))^{2}}=1-\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{(r_{2}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-b)^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4},

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(a)f^{\prime}_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(r_{1})\frac{(a-r_{1})^{2}}{(f_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(a)-f_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(r_{1}))^{2}}=1-\frac{(a-r_{1})^{2}}{(r_{2}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4},

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(r_{1})f^{\prime}_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(b)\frac{(r_{1}-b)^{2}}{(f_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(r_{1})-f_{r_{2},\varepsilon}(b))^{2}}=1-\frac{(r_{1}-b)^{2}}{(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}(r_{2}-b)^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4}.

Plugging them into Lemma
6.11
, we can compute
\mathbb{P}[\widetilde{K}\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon_{2})=\emptyset]
, and then deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\widetilde{K}\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon_{2})\not=\emptyset]=\frac{(\alpha-1)(a-b)^{2}}{(r_{2}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-b)^{2}}\varepsilon_{2}^{2}+\frac{(a-r_{1})^{2}}{(r_{2}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}\varepsilon_{2}^{2}+\frac{(r_{1}-b)^{2}}{(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}(r_{2}-b)^{2}}\varepsilon_{2}^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon_{2}^{4}.

(6.14)

Combining (
6.11
), (
6.12
), (
6.13
), (
6.14
) and letting
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}
proves (
6.5
) where the
O(1)
term therein is uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\leq\min(\varepsilon_{0},\delta_{0})
. It is clear that the
O(1)
term is also bounded for
\varepsilon\in[\min(\varepsilon_{0},\delta_{0}),1]
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We immediately get the following estimate on Brownian excursions.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
a,b,r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
be four distinct points. Let
K
be a Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{H}
between
a
and
b
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,K\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{(r_{1}-a)^{2}(r_{2}-b)^{2}(r_{1}-r_{2})^{2}}\varepsilon^{4}+\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{(r_{2}-a)^{2}(r_{1}-b)^{2}(r_{1}-r_{2})^{2}}\varepsilon^{4}+O(1)\varepsilon^{5},

where the
O(1)
term is uniformly bounded for all
\varepsilon\leq 1
by some function of
a,b,r_{1},r_{2}
.

Proof.

The formula follows from (
6.5
) applied to
\alpha=1
. In the case of a Brownian motion, one can alternatively compute
\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,K\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]
using the heat kernel. The latter computation will show that
\mathbb{P}[K\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,K\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]
is analytic in
\varepsilon
near
0
. Therefore the
o(\varepsilon^{4})
term in (
6.5
) is in fact
O(\varepsilon^{5})
.
∎

6.2
Estimates about Brownian excursions

In this subsection, we aim to obtain estimates about the probability that a Brownian excursion gets close to one interior point (Lemma
6.13
) and two interior points (Lemma
6.14
). We derive these standard estimates for ease of reference.
First recall some definitions from
[
Law05
, Section 5]
.

Green’s function and Poisson kernel

For any domain
D
, let
G_{D}
be the Green’s function in
D
as defined in Section
2.1
. In our normalisation,

\forall x,y\in\mathbb{D},G_{\mathbb{D}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|1-x\bar{y}|}{|x-y|}\quad\text{and}\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{H},G_{\mathbb{H}}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|x-\bar{y}|}{|x-y|}.

(6.15)

Suppose that
\partial D
is smooth. Then for
x\in D
and
z\in\partial D
, the Poisson kernel is given by

H_{D}(x,z)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}G(x,z+\varepsilon\mathbf{n}_{z}),

where
\mathbf{n}_{z}
is the inward unit normal vector of
\partial D
at
z
.
We have

\forall x\in\mathbb{D},\forall z\in\partial\mathbb{D},H_{\mathbb{D}}(x,z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{|x-z|^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{H},\forall z\in\partial\mathbb{H},H_{\mathbb{H}}(x,z)=\frac{\mathrm{Im}(x)}{\pi|x-z|^{2}}.

(6.16)

For
z,w\in\partial D
, let the boundary Poisson kernel be

H_{D}(z,w)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}H_{D}(z+\varepsilon\mathbf{n}_{z},w).

Under this normalisation, we have

\forall z,w\in\partial\mathbb{D},H_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)=\frac{1}{\pi|z-w|^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\forall z,w\in\partial\mathbb{H},H_{\mathbb{H}}(z,w)=\frac{1}{\pi(z-w)^{2}}.

(6.17)

Finally recall the definitions introduced in Notation
6.3
of the conformal map
f_{z}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
mapping
z
to
0
and
U(z,r)=f_{z}^{-1}(D(0,r))
.

Lemma 6.13

(Getting close to one interior point)
.

For all
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exists
C=C(\eta)>0
such that the following holds.
Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
be two distinct boundary points and let
B
be a Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{H}
between
a
and
b
. For all
r\in(0,1-\eta)
and
z=x+yi\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[B\cap U(z,r)\not=\emptyset]\leq C\frac{(a-b)^{2}y^{2}}{|a-z|^{2}|b-z|^{2}}\left|\log r\right|^{-1}.

(6.18)

Proof.

Consider the conformal map
f_{z}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
that sends
z
to 0.
Let
\beta_{0}\in[0,2\pi)
be the angle between
f_{z}(a)
and
f_{z}(b)
.
By conformal invariance, the left hand side of (
6.18
) is equal to the probability
p(\beta_{0},r)
that a Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{D}
between
1
and
e^{i\beta_{0}}
visits
D(0,r)
.
Let
\mu
be the infinite measure on excursions
B
in
\mathbb{D}
started from
1
, defined by
\mu=\int_{0}^{2\pi}H_{\mathbb{D}}(1,e^{i\beta})\mu_{\mathbb{D}}^{\#}(1,e^{i\beta})d\beta
. Let
T
be the total time length of
B
.
We have

\displaystyle p(\beta,r)H_{\mathbb{D}}(1,e^{i\beta})d\beta=\mu(B\cap D(0,r)\not=\emptyset,\arg B_{T}\in(\beta,\beta+d\beta))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\arg B_{T}\in(\beta,\beta+d\beta)\mid B\cap D(0,r)\not=\emptyset\right)\mu(B\cap D(0,r)\not=\emptyset).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mu(B\cap D(0,r)\not=\emptyset)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}_{1-\varepsilon}\left(\tau_{D(0,r)}<\tau_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\right)=|\log r|^{-1},

where the probability above stands for the probability that a Brownian motion starting at
1-\varepsilon
hits
D(0,r)
before
\partial\mathbb{D}
.
For fixed
\eta\in(0,1)
and
r\in(0,\eta)
, for an excursion
B
started from
1
and conditioned to hit
D(0,r)
,
there exists a constant
C(\eta)>0
, such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\arg B_{T}\in(\beta,\beta+d\beta)\mid B\cap D(0,r)\not=\emptyset\right)\leq C(\eta)d\beta.

Therefore

\displaystyle p(\beta,r)\leq C(\eta)H_{\mathbb{D}}(1,e^{i\beta})^{-1}|\log r|^{-1}\quad\text{ for all }r\leq 1-\eta.

Finally, by (
6.17
),

H_{\mathbb{D}}(1,e^{i\beta_{0}})=\frac{1}{\pi|f_{z}(a)-f_{z}(b)|^{2}}=\frac{|a-z|^{2}|b-z|^{2}}{4\pi y^{2}(a-b)^{2}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.14

(Getting close to two interior points)
.

For all
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exist
\nu=\nu(\eta)>0
and
C=C(\eta)>0
such that the following holds.
Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
be two distinct boundary points and let
B
be a Brownian excursion in
\mathbb{H}
between
a
and
b
.
For all
z_{j}=x_{j}+y_{j}i\in\mathbb{H}
,
j=1,2
, and
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1-\eta)
such that
r_{j}\mathrm{Im}(z_{j})\leq\nu|z_{1}-z_{2}|
,
j=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[B\cap U(z_{1},r_{1})\not=\emptyset,B\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset]

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{(a-b)^{2}y_{1}y_{2}}{|a-z_{1}|^{2}|b-z_{2}|^{2}}+\frac{(a-b)^{2}y_{1}y_{2}}{|a-z_{2}|^{2}|b-z_{1}|^{2}}\right)(1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2}))\left|\log r_{1}\right|^{-1}\left|\log r_{2}\right|^{-1}.

The condition that
r_{j}\mathrm{Im}(z_{j})\leq\nu|z_{1}-z_{2}|
,
j=1,2
ensures that
U(z_{1},r_{1})
and
U(z_{2},r_{2})
do not intersect and are at distance of order
|z_{1}-z_{2}|
to each other.

Proof.

We start by recording two facts for ease of future reference:

1.
For all
w\in\partial\mathbb{D}
and
r\in(0,1)
,

\int_{\partial D(0,r)}H_{\mathbb{D}\setminus D(0,r)}(w,z)\mathrm{d}z=|\log r|^{-1}.

(6.19)

Indeed, the left hand side is equal to

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial D(0,r)}H_{\mathbb{D}\setminus D(0,r)}(w+\varepsilon\mathbf{n}_{w},z)\mathrm{d}z=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}_{w+\varepsilon\mathbf{n}_{w}}\left(\tau_{D(0,r)}<\tau_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\right)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\log(1-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon\log r}=\frac{1}{|\log r|},

where the probability appearing in the above display stands for the probability that a Brownian motion starting from
w+\varepsilon\mathbf{n}_{w}
hits
D(0,r)
before hitting
\partial\mathbb{D}
.

2. There exists
C_{0}>0
such that for all
z_{3}\in\partial U(z_{1},r_{1})
and
z_{4}\in\partial U(z_{2},r_{2})
,

G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{3},z_{4})\leq C_{0}(1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2})).

(6.20)

This follows from the fact that there exists
\nu=\nu(\eta)>0
such that for all
z_{2j}\in U(z_{j},r_{j})
,
|z_{2j}-z_{j}|\leq(10\nu)^{-1}r_{j}\mathrm{Im}(z_{j})
,
j=1,2
. The assumption that
r_{j}\mathrm{Im}(z_{j})\leq\nu|z_{1}-z_{2}|
,
j=1,2
, then ensures that the points of
U(z_{1},r_{1})
stay much closer to
z_{1}
than to any other point of
U(z_{2},r_{2})
. A direct computation using (
6.15
) then concludes the proof of (
6.20
).

Having collected these two initial facts, we can now proceed with the proof of Lemma
6.14
.
The event
B\cap U(z_{1},r_{1})\not=\emptyset,B\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset
is the disjoint union of the two events
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
:

•

Let
E_{1}
be the event that
B
first visits
U(z_{1},r_{1})
before
U(z_{2},r_{2})
.

•

Let
E_{2}
be the event that
B
first visits
U(z_{2},r_{2})
before
U(z_{1},r_{1})
.

On
E_{1}
, we can decompose
B
into an excursion from
a
to
z_{3}\in\partial U(z_{1},r_{1})
in
\mathbb{H}\setminus(U(z_{1},r_{1})\cup U(z_{2},r_{2}))
, an excursion from
z_{3}
to
z_{4}\in\partial U(z_{2},r_{2})
in
\mathbb{H}
and an excursion from
z_{4}
to
b
in
\mathbb{H}\setminus U(z_{2},r_{2})
. This results in the following identity

\displaystyle H_{\mathbb{H}}(a,b)\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]=\int_{\partial U(z_{1},r_{1})}\mathrm{d}z_{3}\int_{\partial U(z_{2},r_{2})}\mathrm{d}z_{4}H_{\mathbb{H}\setminus(U(z_{1},r_{1})\cup U(z_{2},r_{2}))}(a,z_{3})G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{3},z_{4})H_{\mathbb{H}\setminus U(z_{2},r_{2})}(b,z_{4}).

(6.21)

Increasing the domain can only increase the pointwise values of the Poisson kernel. We can thus bound
H_{\mathbb{H}\setminus(U(z_{1},r_{1})\cup U(z_{2},r_{2}))}(a,z_{3})\leq H_{\mathbb{H}\setminus U(z_{1},r_{1})}(a,z_{3})
for all
z_{3}\in\partial U(z_{1},r_{1})
. Using further (
6.20
), we deduce that

\displaystyle H_{\mathbb{H}}(a,b)\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]\leq C_{0}(1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2}))\int_{\partial U(z_{1},r_{1})}\mathrm{d}z_{3}H_{\mathbb{H}\setminus U(z_{1},r_{1})}(a,z_{3})\times\int_{\partial U(z_{2},r_{2})}\mathrm{d}z_{4}H_{\mathbb{H}\setminus U(z_{2},r_{2})}(b,z_{4}).

With a change of variable and conformal covariance of the boundary Poisson kernel, this is further equal to

\displaystyle C_{0}(1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2}))f_{z_{1}}^{\prime}(a)f_{z_{2}}^{\prime}(b)\int_{\partial D(0,r_{1})}H_{\mathbb{D}\setminus D(0,r_{1})}(f_{z_{1}}(a),w_{3})\mathrm{d}w_{3}\int_{\partial D(0,r_{2})}H_{\mathbb{D}\setminus D(0,r_{2})}(f_{z_{2}}(b),w_{4})\mathrm{d}w_{4}.

By (
6.19
), the product of the last two integrals equals
|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}.
Finally, using that (see (
6.17
))

\displaystyle H_{\mathbb{H}}(a,b)=\frac{1}{\pi(a-b)^{2}},\quad f_{z_{1}}^{\prime}(a)=\frac{2y_{1}}{|a-z_{1}|^{2}},\quad f_{z_{2}}^{\prime}(b)=\frac{2y_{2}}{|b-z_{2}|^{2}},

we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]\leq\pi^{-1}C_{1}(1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2}))\frac{(a-b)^{2}y_{1}y_{2}}{|a-z_{1}|^{2}|b-z_{2}|^{2}}\left|\log r_{1}\right|^{-1}\left|\log r_{2}\right|^{-1}.

To bound
\mathbb{P}[E_{2}]
simply exchange the roles of
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6.3
Boundary excursions in a loop soup with mixed boundary conditions

In this subsection, we aim to prove Propositions
6.5
and
6.16
which provide quantitative controls on the number of excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
. Proposition
6.5
can be understood as a first moment estimate, and Propositions
6.16
can be understood as a second moment estimate.

The proofs of Propositions
6.5
and
6.16
rely essentially on the restriction property of
\mathcal{E}
(Theorem
6.2
) and the independence of the excursions conditionally on their endpoints (Lemma
6.15
).
Lemma
6.15
is a variation of
[
QW18
, Lemma 9]
which states that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a locally finite point process of Brownian excursions. Changing
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
to
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
only modifies the setup of the proof, not the essential arguments, so we omit the proof of Lemma
6.15
.

Lemma 6.15
.

The collection
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
is a locally finite point process of Brownian excursions in
\mathbb{H}
.

The “point process of Brownian excursions” property means that, conditionally on the endpoints of the excursions,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
is distributed as the collection of independent Brownian excursions between the given endpoints.
Throughout, for each excursion
e
, we assign an i.i.d. orientation with probability
1/2
. Let
a(e)
and
b(e)
be respectively the starting and ending endpoints of an excursion
e
.
The “locally finite” property means that for each
\delta>0
, the collection of excursions
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
with
|a(e)-b(e)|\geq\delta
is a.s. finite.

Let us first prove Proposition
6.5
.

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

We write
\mathcal{E}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
for simplicity.
We define for each
e\in\mathcal{E}
and
r>0
,

\displaystyle p_{r}(e):=\frac{(b(e)-a(e))^{2}}{(r-b(e))^{2}(r-a(e))^{2}}.

By Lemma
6.15
and Lemma
6.8
, we know that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[e\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\mid a(e),b(e)\right]=p_{r}(e)\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4},

(6.22)

where the
O(1)
term depends on
a(e),b(e)
and
r
, but is uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\leq 1
. Note that for all
a(e)<b(e)<0
and
r>0
, we have
p_{r}(e)\leq r^{-2}
.
For each
M>0
, let
\mathcal{E}^{M}
denote the collection of the
M
excursions in
\mathcal{E}
with the largest
p_{r}(e)
. Then
\mathcal{E}=\cup_{M>0}\mathcal{E}^{M}
.
Let us first show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}^{M}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\mid\{(a(e),b(e))\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}\right]=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}p_{r}(e)\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4},

(6.23)

where the
O(1)
term depends on
r,M
and the random set
\{(a(e),b(e))\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}
, but is uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\leq 1
.
The
\leq
direction in (
6.23
) directly follows from Lemma
6.15
and (
6.22
). To prove the
\geq
direction, note that by the inclusion-exclusion principle, the left-hand side of (
6.23
) is at least

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}\mathbb{P}[e\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]-\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}\mathbb{P}[e_{1}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,e_{2}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}p_{r}(e)\varepsilon^{2}-\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}p_{r}(e_{1})p_{r}(e_{2})\varepsilon^{4}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}p_{r}(e)\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4}.

In the last equality, we have used the bound
\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}p_{r}(e_{1})p_{r}(e_{2})\leq M^{2}r^{-4}
.
This completes the proof of (
6.23
).
Note that the
O(1)
term in (
6.23
) has finite expectation, since
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}^{M}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]\in[0,1]
.
Taking expectations on both sides of (
6.23
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}^{M}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}p_{r}(e)\bigg]\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4},

(6.24)

where the
O(1)
term depends on
M
and
r
, but is uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\leq 1
.

On the other hand, by Theorem
6.2
, we know that
\mathcal{E}
is distributed as a restriction measure of exponent
\alpha(\theta)
. Applying Lemma
6.8
to
a=\infty
and
b=0
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]=\alpha(\theta)\varepsilon^{2}/r^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4}.

(6.25)

Since
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}^{M}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]
, together with (
6.24
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}p_{r}(e)\bigg]\leq\alpha(\theta)/r^{2}.

Letting
M\to\infty
, by monotone convergence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}p_{r}(e)\bigg]\leq\alpha(\theta)/r^{2}.

(6.26)

Let
M\to\infty
in both sides of (
6.24
). The left-hand side of (
6.24
) tends to
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]
which is less than
1
. In the right-hand side of (
6.24
), the coefficient in front of
\varepsilon^{2}
is increasing as
M\to\infty
, and tends to the same expected sum over
\mathcal{E}
instead of
\mathcal{E}^{M}
, which is less than
\alpha(\theta)/r^{2}
by (
6.26
). Therefore, the
O(1)
term in (
6.24
) should also be bounded as
M\to\infty
.
This implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}\cap D(r,\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}p_{r}(e)\bigg]\varepsilon^{2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{4},

where the
O(1)
term depends only on
r
. Comparing with (
6.25
) yields the lemma.
∎

Let us now deduce a “second moment” estimate.

Proposition 6.16
.

For all
0<r_{1}<r_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}}\frac{(b(e)-a(e))^{2}}{(r_{1}-b(e))^{2}(r_{2}-a(e))^{2}(r_{1}-r_{2})^{2}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}}\frac{(b(e)-a(e))^{2}}{(r_{1}-a(e))^{2}(r_{2}-b(e))^{2}(r_{1}-r_{2})^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}}\frac{(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}}{(r_{1}-b(e_{1}))^{2}(r_{1}-a(e_{1}))^{2}}\frac{(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}}{(r_{2}-b(e_{2}))^{2}(r_{2}-a(e_{2}))^{2}}\right]

(6.27)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha(\theta)}{r_{1}^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+\frac{\alpha(\theta)}{r_{2}^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+\frac{\alpha(\theta)(\alpha(\theta)-1)}{r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}}.

Remark 6.17
.

When
\theta=1/2
, the endpoints of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
are distributed according to a Poisson point process with intensity
1/4
(see Theorem
6.1
for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
instead of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
). At this value of
\theta
, the restriction exponent
\alpha(\theta)
equals
1/4
and thus also corresponds to the intensity of the point process. With a simple Poisson point process computation, one can deduce from Proposition
6.5
that the third expectation on the left hand side of (
6.27
) is equal to the third term on the right hand side. There is
a priori
no reason to believe that this still holds for other values of
\theta
. However, we will only need to upper bound the terms on the left hand side of (
6.27
).

Proof.

We write
\mathcal{E}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}
for simplicity.
By Theorem
6.2
and Lemma
6.9
applied to
a=\infty
and
b=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]=\frac{\alpha(\theta)(\alpha(\theta)-1)\varepsilon^{4}}{r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}}+\frac{\alpha(\theta)\varepsilon^{4}}{r_{1}^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+\frac{\alpha(\theta)\varepsilon^{4}}{r_{2}^{2}(r_{2}-r_{1})^{2}}+O(1)\varepsilon^{5}.

(6.28)

The event
\{\mathcal{E}\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\}
is equal to the union of the two following events:

•

Let
E_{1}
be the event that
D(r_{1},\varepsilon)
and
D(r_{2},\varepsilon)
are visited by a same excursion in
\mathcal{E}
.

•

Let
E_{2}
be the event that
D(r_{1},\varepsilon)
and
D(r_{2},\varepsilon)
are visited by two different excursions in
\mathcal{E}
.

We first evaluate
\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]
. Let

\displaystyle s_{r_{1},r_{2}}(e):=\frac{(b(e)-a(e))^{2}}{(r_{1}-b(e))^{2}(r_{2}-a(e))^{2}(r_{1}-r_{2})^{2}}+\frac{(b(e)-a(e))^{2}}{(r_{1}-a(e))^{2}(r_{2}-b(e))^{2}(r_{1}-r_{2})^{2}}.

By Lemma
6.15
and Corollary
6.12
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[e\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,e\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset]=s_{r_{1},r_{2}}(e)\varepsilon^{4}+O(1)\varepsilon^{5},

where the
O(1)
term depends on
a(e),b(e)
and
r_{1},r_{2}
, but is uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\leq 1
. Note that for all
a(e)<b(e)<0
and
r_{1},r_{2}>0
, we have
p_{r}(e)\leq(r_{1}-r_{2})^{-2}(r_{1}^{-2}+r_{2}^{-2})
.

For each
M>0
, let
\mathcal{E}^{M}
be the set of the
M
excursions in
\mathcal{E}
with the largest
s_{r_{1},r_{2}}(e)
. Then
\mathcal{E}=\cup_{M>0}\mathcal{E}^{M}
.
Let
E_{1}^{M}
and
E_{2}^{M}
be defined respectively as
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
where we replace
\mathcal{E}
by
\mathcal{E}^{M}
in their definitions.
By Lemma
6.15
, Corollary
6.12
, and a use of the inclusion-exclusion principle as in the proof of
6.5
, we can deduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[E_{1}^{M}\mid\{(a(e),b(e))\right]=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}s_{r_{1},r_{2}}(e)\varepsilon^{4}+O(1)\varepsilon^{5},

where the
O(1)
term depends on
r_{1},r_{2}
and the random set
\{(a(e),b(e))\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}^{M}}
, but is uniformly bounded for
\varepsilon\leq 1
. Moreover, this
O(1)
term has finite expectation, since
\mathbb{P}[E_{1}^{M}]\in[0,1]
. A similar reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
6.5
will allow us to take the expectations on both sides of the previous equality and then let
M\to\infty
. We will get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}s_{r_{1},r_{2}}(e)\right]\varepsilon^{4}+O(1)\varepsilon^{5}.

Let us now evaluate
\mathbb{P}[E_{2}]
.
By Lemmas
6.15
and
6.8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[E_{2}^{M}\mid\{(a(e),b(e))\right]=\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{(r_{1}-b(e_{1}))^{2}(r_{1}-a(e_{1}))^{2}}\frac{(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{(r_{2}-b(e_{2}))^{2}(r_{2}-a(e_{2}))^{2}}+O(\varepsilon^{5}).

The same reasoning will again allow us to conclude that
\mathbb{P}[E_{2}]
is equal to the line (
6.27
) times
\varepsilon^{4}
plus
O(1)\varepsilon^{5}
.

Finally, one can see that
\mathbb{P}[E_{1}\cap E_{2}]
is of smaller order than
\varepsilon^{4}
, because on
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
, there needs to be one excursion
e_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
that visits both
D(r_{1},\varepsilon)
and
D(r_{2},\varepsilon)
and a different excursion
e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
that visits
D(r_{1},\varepsilon)
or
D(r_{2},\varepsilon)
. This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}\cap D(r_{1},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}\cap D(r_{2},\varepsilon)\not=\emptyset\right]=\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]+\mathbb{P}[E_{2}]+o(\varepsilon^{4}).

Combining with (
6.28
) and the previous evaluations of
\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]
and
\mathbb{P}[E_{2}]
, we obtain the lemma.
∎

6.4
Boundary excursions in a loop soup with wired boundary conditions

In this section, we will use the results from Section
6.3
to obtain quantitative controls on excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
, i.e., from a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
which is wired on the entire boundary. The main step is to prove Lemma
6.18
below. After that, we will deduce Lemmas
6.19
and
6.20
as consequences.

Lemma 6.18
.

We have

\displaystyle S_{1}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]<\infty.

(6.29)

\displaystyle S_{2}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}};\,a(e_{1}),b(e_{1}),a(e_{2}),b(e_{2})\in[0,1]}(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}\right]<\infty.

(6.30)

Proof.

We use
\mathcal{E}
to denote
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
for simplicity. We apply the conformal map
z\in\mathbb{H}\mapsto 1/(1-z)\in\mathbb{H}
that maps
\mathbb{R}^{-}
to
[0,1]
to Proposition
6.5
(with
r=1
), and get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[0,1]}}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]=\alpha(\theta).

By scaling invariance and translation invariance, for all
R>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[-R,R]}}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]=4R^{2}\alpha(\theta).

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[-R,R]},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]\leq 4R^{2}\alpha(\theta).

In a loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{[-R,R]}
in
\mathbb{H}
wired on
[-R,R]
and free elsewhere, let
\eta
be the curve from
-R
to
R
which is the outer boundary of the cluster glued at
[-R,R]
. Then
\eta
is distributed as a SLE
κ
from
-R
to
R
. Let
g_{t}
be the conformal map from
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
onto
\mathbb{H}
that fixes
0,1,\infty
. Let
E
be the event that
\eta\cap D(0,10)=\emptyset
. We can take
R
big enough so that
\mathbb{P}(E)\geq 1/2
. Let us first prove that on the event
E
,

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[-R,R]},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(g_{t}(a(e))-g_{t}(b(e)))^{2}\leq C\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[-R,R]},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}.

(6.31)

There is an infinite measure
\mu
on Brownian excursions in
\mathbb{H}
between a point
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\infty
, so that the endpoint
x
is distributed according to the Lebesgue measure
[
LSW03b
]
. For a given
g_{t}
, there is a constant
C
(which depends only on
g_{t}
) such that for all
-R<a<b<R
,
g_{t}(b)-g_{t}(a)
is equal to
C
times the
\mu
-measure of excursions in
\mathbb{H}
between
\infty
and an endpoint in
[a,b]
which do not intersect
\eta([0,t])
. On the event
E
, an excursion in
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
from
\infty
to
[a,b]
can further be decomposed as the concatenation of an excursion
\xi_{1}
between
z_{0}\in\partial D(0,10)
in
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
and an excursion
\xi_{2}
between
z_{0}
and a point in
[a,b]
in
D(0,10)
. The distribution of
z_{0}
depends on
\eta([0,t])
, and we denote it by
\mathbb{P}_{0}
. However, given
z_{0}
, the distribution of
\xi_{2}
is independent from
\eta([0,t])
. For all
0\leq a<b\leq 1
, the ratio

\displaystyle\frac{g_{t}(b)-g_{t}(a)}{g_{t}(1)-g_{t}(0)}=\mathbb{E}_{0}\left[\frac{\int_{a}^{b}H_{D(0,10)}(z_{0},x)dx}{\int_{0}^{1}H_{D(0,10)}(z_{0},x)dx}\right].

(6.32)

Since
\partial D(0,10)
has positive distance from
[0,1]
, there exist
C_{1}>c_{1}>0
such that for all
z_{0}\in\partial D(0,10)
and
x\in[0,1]
, we have
c_{1}\leq H_{D(0,10)}(z_{0},x)\leq C_{1}
. Therefore (
6.32
) is at most
(C_{1}/c_{1})(b-a)
. This implies that
g_{t}(b)-g_{t}(a)\leq C(b-a)
for all
0\leq a<b\leq 1
where
C
is some universal constant, hence (
6.31
) follows.

As
t\to\infty
,
g_{t}
converges to the conformal map
g_{\infty}
from the bounded connected component of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta([0,\infty])
onto
\mathbb{H}
that sends
0,1,R
to
0,1,\infty
. The left-hand side of (
6.31
) is at least

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[-R,R]},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1],e\cap\eta=\emptyset}(g_{t}(a(e))-g_{t}(b(e)))^{2},

which converges a.s. to

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(a(e)-b(e))^{2},

(6.33)

where
\mathcal{E}
is the set of excursions induced from
g_{\infty}(\mathcal{L}_{[-R,R]})
, a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
with wired boundary conditions.
By Theorem
6.1
, the law of (
6.33
) is independent from
\eta
, hence remains unchanged if we condition on
E
. Taking the conditional expectation on
E
of both sides of (
6.31
), letting
t\to\infty
, and using the dominated convergence where the dominator is given by the right-hand side of (
6.31
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]\leq C\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[-R,R]},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\mid E\right]

(6.34)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{[-R,R]},a(e),b(e)\in[0,1]}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]\mathbb{P}(E)^{-1}\leq 8CR^{2}\alpha(\theta).

(6.35)

This completes the proof of (
6.29
).

The proof of (
6.30
) follows a similar line.
From Proposition
6.16
, we can deduce that for
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}}\frac{(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}}{(1-b(e_{1}))^{2}(1-a(e_{1}))^{2}}\frac{(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}}{(1-b(e_{2}))^{2}(1-a(e_{2}))^{2}}\right]

(6.36)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}}}\frac{(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}}{(r_{1}-b(e_{1}))^{2}(r_{1}-a(e_{1}))^{2}}\frac{(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}}{(r_{2}-b(e_{2}))^{2}(r_{2}-a(e_{2}))^{2}}\right]<\infty.

Under the conformal map
f(z)=1/(1-z)
,
f(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}^{-}})
is distributed as
\mathcal{E}_{[0,1]}
, and (
6.36
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{[0,1]}}(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}\right]<\infty.

Following the same strategy as before, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e_{1}\not=e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{\infty};\,a(e_{1}),b(e_{1}),a(e_{2}),b(e_{2})\in[0,1]}(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}\right]<\infty.

To deal with the case
e_{1}=e_{2}
, we simply bound
(b(e)-a(e))^{4}\leq(b(e)-a(e))^{2}
and rely on (
6.29
).
This completes the proof of (
6.30
).
∎

Lemma 6.19
.

For all
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, let

\displaystyle X(\varepsilon):=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}};\,a(e)\in[0,\varepsilon);b(e)\in[0,1]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}.

Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[X(\varepsilon)\right]\leq C\varepsilon
and
\mathbb{E}\left[X(\varepsilon)^{2}\right]\leq C\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
\varepsilon=1/N
for some integer
N\geq 1
.
Note that
[0,1)
is the disjoint union of the sets
I_{n}
for
0\leq n\leq N-1
, where
I_{n}:=[n/N,(n+1)/N)
. Let

\displaystyle X_{n}(\varepsilon):=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}};\,a(e)\in I_{n};\,b(e)\in[n/N,n/N+1]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}.

By translation invariance,
X_{n}(\varepsilon)
has the same distribution as
X(\varepsilon)
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}};\,a(e),b(e)\in[0,2]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right]\geq\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{n}(\varepsilon)\right]\geq N\mathbb{E}[X(\varepsilon)].

By (
6.29
), this implies that
\mathbb{E}[X(\varepsilon)]\leq CN^{-1}=C\varepsilon
.
The upper bound on
\mathbb{E}[X(\varepsilon)^{2}]
is obtained similarly using (
6.30
) and
