\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}};\,a(e),b(e)\in[0,2]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right)^{2}\right]\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}X_{n}(\varepsilon)\right)^{2}\right]\geq\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}[X_{n}(\varepsilon)^{2}]\geq N\mathbb{E}[X(\varepsilon)^{2}].

∎

Lemma 6.20
.

For all intervals
I_{1},I_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}
with respective lengths
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, there is a universal constant
C>0
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}},\,a(e)\in I_{1},b(e)\in I_{2}}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right]\leq Cr_{1}r_{2}.

Proof.

By decomposing
I_{1}=(I_{1}\setminus I_{2})\cup(I_{1}\cap I_{2})
, it is enough to treat two cases:
I_{1}\subset I_{2}
and
I_{1}\cap I_{2}=\varnothing
.

Case 1.

I_{1}\subset I_{2}
. We can decompose
I_{2}
into the union of two subintervals sharing an endpoint with
I_{1}
. We can therefore assume that
I_{2}
shares an endpoint with
I_{1}
. By scaling and translation, this case reduces to Lemma
6.19
.

Case 2.

I_{1}\cap I_{2}=\varnothing
.
By scaling and translation, it is enough to consider the case where
I_{1}=[0,x]
and
I_{2}=[y,1]
, where
0<x\leq y<1
. If
I_{1}
or
I_{2}
has length at least
1/4
, then the result is a direct consequence of Lemma
6.19
. We can thus suppose that both
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
have length less than
1/4
. We apply the conformal map

f:z\in\mathbb{H}\mapsto\frac{(1-x)z}{(1-2x)z+x}\in\mathbb{H}
that fixes
0
and
1
and sends
x
to
1/2
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{a(e)\in[0,x],b(e)\in[y,1]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{a(e)\in[0,f(x)],b(e)\in[f(y),1]}(f^{-1}(b(e))-f^{-1}(a(e)))^{2}\right].

(6.37)

Note that

\displaystyle y-x+2x(1-y)\geq y-x\geq 1/2\quad\text{and}\quad 1-f(y)=\frac{x(1-y)}{y-x+2x(1-y)}\leq 2x(1-y)\leq 1/8.

This implies that for all
a\in[0,x]
and
b\in[y,1]
, we have
f(b)-f(a)\geq 3/8\geq 3/8(b-a)
. Conversely, for all
a\in[0,f(x)]
and
b\in[f(y),1]
, we have
f^{-1}(b)-f^{-1}(a)\leq 8/3(b-a)
. Therefore (
6.37
) is at most

\displaystyle\frac{64}{9}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{a(e)\in[0,f(x)],b(e)\in[f(y),1]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right]\leq\frac{64}{9}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{a(e)\in[0,1],b(e)\in[f(y),1]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right]

which is bounded by
C(1-f(y))\leq 2Cx(1-y)
by Lemma
6.19
. This concludes Case 2 and the proof of Lemma
6.20
.
∎

6.5
Proof of Proposition
6.4

We have now gathered all the ingredients to prove Proposition
6.4
.
In this section, we will only deal with
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
, so will write
\mathcal{E}
for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let us first prove the one-point estimate (
6.1
).

Proof of (
6.1
).

By conformal invariance, it is enough to prove (
6.1
) for
z=i
. Fix
\eta>0
. By Lemma
6.13
, there exists
C>0
, such that for all
r\in(0,1-\eta)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}\cap U(i,r)\not=\emptyset]\leq C\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{(a(e)-b(e))^{2}}{|a(e)-i|^{2}|b(e)-i|^{2}}\right]|\log r|^{-1}.

(6.38)

Let
I_{-1}=[-1,1]
and for each
n\geq 0
, let
I_{n}:=[-2^{n+1},-2^{n})\cup(2^{n},2^{n+1}]
so that
\mathbb{R}=\bigcup_{n\geq-1}I_{n}
. Note that for all
n,m\geq-1
, if
a(e)\in I_{n}
and
b(e)\in I_{m}
, then
|a(e)-i|\geq 2^{n}
and
|b(e)-i|\geq 2^{m}
. We can thus bound the expectation in (
6.38
) by

\displaystyle\sum_{n,m\geq-1}2^{-2n-2m}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E},a(e)\in I_{n},b(e)\in I_{m}}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right].

By Lemma
6.20
, the expectation above is bounded by
C2^{n+m}
. This shows that the expectation in (
6.38
) is bounded by some constant concluding the proof.
∎

Let us now prove the two-point estimate (
6.2
).

Proof of (
6.2
).

Suppose
z_{1}=x_{1}+iy_{1}
and
z_{2}=x_{2}+iy_{2}
.
Let
\nu=\nu(\eta)
be as in Lemma
6.14
. Let us first assume that the condition
r_{i}y_{i}\leq\nu|z_{1}-z_{2}|
,
i=1,2
, from Lemma
6.14
is violated. For instance, assume that
r_{1}y_{1}>\nu|z_{1}-z_{2}|
. In that case, we simply bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}\cap U(z_{1},r_{1})\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset]\leq C|\log r_{2}|^{-1}

by (
6.1
). Since
r_{1}y_{1}>\nu|z_{1}-z_{2}|
, we further have

|\log r_{2}|^{-1}\leq C\log\frac{y_{1}}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|}|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}\leq CG_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2})|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}.

This concludes the proof of (
6.2
) in this case.

Let us now assume that
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are not too close to each other so that
r_{i}y_{i}\leq\nu|z_{1}-z_{2}|
,
i=1,2
. This will allow us to apply Lemma
6.14
.
The event
\mathcal{E}\cap U(z_{1},r_{1})\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset
is the union of the following two events:

•

Let
E_{1}
be the event that
U(z_{1},r_{1})
and
U(z_{2},r_{2})
are both visited by a same excursion in
\mathcal{E}
.

•

Let
E_{2}
be the event that
U(z_{1},r_{1})
and
U(z_{2},r_{2})
are visited by two different excursions in
\mathcal{E}
.

We are going to show that

\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]\leq C(1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2}))|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}[E_{2}]\leq C|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}

which will conclude the proof of (
6.2
).

Contribution of
E_{1}
.

By Lemma
6.14
,
\mathbb{P}[E_{1}]
is at most

\displaystyle C\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\frac{(a(e)-b(e))^{2}y_{1}y_{2}}{|a(e)-z_{1}|^{2}|b(e)-z_{2}|^{2}}+\frac{(a(e)-b(e))^{2}y_{1}y_{2}}{|a(e)-z_{2}|^{2}|b(e)-z_{1}|^{2}}\right)\right](1+G_{\mathbb{H}}(z_{1},z_{2}))|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}.

By symmetry, it is enough to prove that there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{(a(e)-b(e))^{2}y_{1}y_{2}}{|a(e)-z_{1}|^{2}|b(e)-z_{2}|^{2}}\right]\leq C.

(6.39)

For
j=1,2
and
n\geq 0
, let

I_{j,-1}:=[x_{j}-y_{j},x_{j}+y_{j}]\quad\text{and}\quad I_{j,n}:=[x_{j}-y_{j}2^{n+1},x_{j}-y_{j}2^{n})\cup(x_{j}+y_{j}2^{n},x_{j}+y_{j}2^{n+1}].

(6.40)

As in the proof of (
6.1
), we can bound the left hand side of (
6.39
) by

\displaystyle y_{1}y_{2}\sum_{n,m\geq-1}(y_{1}2^{n})^{-2}(y_{2}2^{n})^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E};a(e)\in I_{1,n},b(e)\in I_{2,m}}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]\leq C\sum_{n,m\geq-1}2^{-n-m}

using Lemma
6.20
in the last inequality.

Contribution of
E_{2}
.

By Lemma
6.13
,
\mathbb{P}[E_{2}]
is upper bounded by a constant times

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e_{1}\in\mathcal{E}}\sum_{e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}y_{1}^{2}}{|z_{1}-b(e_{1})|^{2}|z_{1}-a(e_{1})|^{2}}\frac{(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}y_{2}^{2}}{|z_{2}-b(e_{2})|^{2}|z_{2}-a(e_{2})|^{2}}\right]|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}.

(6.41)

Let
I_{j,n}
,
j=1,2
,
n\geq-1
, be as in (
6.40
). We can bound the above expectation by

\displaystyle y_{1}^{-2}y_{2}^{-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},m_{1}\geq-1\\
n_{2},m_{2}\geq-1\end{subarray}}2^{-2(n_{1}-m_{1}-n_{2}-m_{2})}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{1}\in\mathcal{E};a(e_{1})\in I_{1,n_{1}},b(e_{1})\in I_{1,m_{1}}\\
e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{2};a(e_{2})\in I_{2,n_{2}},b(e_{2})\in I_{2,m_{2}}\end{subarray}}(b(e_{1})-a(e_{1}))^{2}(b(e_{2})-a(e_{2}))^{2}\right].

By Cauchy-Schwarz, the expectation inside the sum is at most
\sqrt{X_{1}X_{2}}
where

X_{j}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E};a(e)\in I_{j,n_{j}},b(e)\in I_{j,m_{j}}}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right)^{2}\right],\quad j=1,2.

It is enough to show that
X_{j}\leq Cy_{j}^{4}2^{3n_{j}+3m_{j}}
to conclude the proof.
If
n=m
, then by scaling,
X_{j}
is at most
Cy_{j}^{4}2^{4n_{j}}S_{2}
, where
S_{2}
is defined in (
6.30
).
By symmetry, we can now suppose that
m_{j}>n_{j}
. By scaling, we have

\displaystyle X_{j}\leq y_{j}^{4}2^{4m_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E};a(e)\in[-2^{n_{j}-m_{j}},2^{n_{j}-m_{j}}],b(e)\in[-2,2]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right)^{2}\right]\leq Cy_{j}^{4}2^{4m_{j}}2^{n_{j}-m_{j}}

by Lemma
6.19
. This shows that
X_{j}\leq Cy_{j}^{4}2^{3m_{j}+n_{j}}\leq Cy_{j}^{4}2^{3m_{j}+3n_{j}}
as desired.
∎

The previous proofs already indicate that the probability of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
intersecting a neighborhood of
z\in\mathbb{H}
is mainly contributed by the excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
which are “close” to
z
. We formulate this precisely in the following lemmas, which will be useful in the proof of Proposition
7.3
; see Section
7.4
.

Lemma 6.21
.

Fix
R>0
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}
be the set of excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
with endpoints in
[-R,R]
.
For all
\eta>0
and
r\in(0,1-\eta)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(i,r)\not=\emptyset]-\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap U(i,r)\not=\emptyset]=o(1)|\log r|^{-1}.

where the
o(1)
term goes to
0
as
R\to\infty
, uniformly for all
r\in(0,1-\eta)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.13
, there exists
C>0
, such that for all
r\in(0,1-\eta)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(i,r)\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap U(i,r)=\emptyset]\leq C\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{0}}\frac{(a(e)-b(e))^{2}}{|a(e)-i|^{2}|b(e)-i|^{2}}\right]|\log r|^{-1}.

(6.42)

By monotonicity, it suffices to prove the result for
R=2^{N}
and
N\to\infty
. Using the intervals
I_{-1}=[-1,1]
and
I_{n}=[-2^{n+1},-2^{n})\cup(2^{n}2^{n+1}]
as in the proof of (
6.1
) and with the help of Lemma
6.20
, the expectation in the right-hand side of (
6.42
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq N,m\geq-1}2^{-2(n+m)}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{a(e)\in I_{m},b(e)\in I_{n}}(a(e)-b(e))^{2}\right]\leq C\sum_{n\geq N,m\geq-1}2^{-n-m}\leq C^{\prime}2^{-N}\to 0

as
N\to\infty
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.22
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and
\nu\in(0,1)
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{2}
) be the set of excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
with both endpoints in
[-\varepsilon^{\nu},\varepsilon^{\nu}]
(resp.
[1-\varepsilon^{\nu},1+\varepsilon^{\nu}]
).
Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
. For all
z_{1}\in D(0,\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{H}
,
z_{2}\in D(1,\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{H}
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1-\eta)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(z_{1},r_{1})\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset]-\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap U(z_{1},r_{1})\not=\emptyset,\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap U(z_{2},r_{2})\not=\emptyset]

(6.43)

\displaystyle=o(1)|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1},

where the
o(1)
term goes to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly for all
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1-\eta)
.

Proof.

As we explained in details, the proof of Lemma
6.21
followed from our approach to proving (
6.1
). Similarly,
the proof of Lemma
6.22
follows from the method we developed in the proof of (
6.2
). We omit the details.
∎

7
Properties of
h_{\theta}
and height gap

In this section, we prove Theorems
1.3
,
1.7
and
1.8
, starting with Theorem
1.3
.
Note that the spatial Markov property in Theorem
1.7
, as well as Theorem
1.8
, follow directly from the corresponding spatial Markov property of the loop soup, proved respectively in
[
QW19
]
and
[
Qia19
]
, as we explained before the statement of these theorems.

7.1
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Point
1
: See Lemma
4.1
and Proposition
4.3
.

Point
2
: The conformal invariance of the field follows directly from the conformal covariance of the approximation procedure used to define it, together with the fact that the conformal anomaly disappears when
\gamma\to 0
(derivative of the conformal map to the power
\gamma^{2}/2
). See
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Point
3
:
By definition,

h_{\theta}+h_{\theta}^{-}=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}]).

Because the second term in the expansion of
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}
is
\gamma^{2}
times the occupation measure of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
(see Theorem
9.2
) and because
Z_{\gamma}\geq\gamma^{2(1-\theta)+o(1)}
, this limit equals zero.

Point
4
: Let
f:D\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth test function, not identically zero. By Theorem
1.10
,

(h_{\theta},f)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sigma_{k}(\mu_{k},f)\quad\quad\text{a.s.}

and by Theorem
1.9
, there is a.s. an infinite number of
k
such that
(\mu_{k},f)\neq 0
. Lemma
7.1
below then concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of non zero real numbers and let
(\sigma_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be i.i.d. spins taking values in
\{\pm 1\}
with equal probability 1/2. Assume that
\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}
converges almost surely. Then the distribution of
\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}
is non-atomic.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We are going to show that
\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}\neq x
a.s. which will conclude the proof. The conditional probability
\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}=x|\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k-1}\right)
converges a.s. to
\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}=x\right\}}
as
k\to\infty
. But, by flipping the
k
-th spin and using the fact that
a_{k}\neq 0
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}=x\big|\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{n\neq k}\sigma_{n}a_{n}-\sigma_{k}a_{k}\neq x\big|\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}\neq x\big|\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\Big).

Rearranging, this shows that
\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}=x|\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k-1}\right)\leq 1/2
a.s. Therefore,
\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}=x\right\}}
is at most
1/2
a.s., meaning that
\sum_{n}\sigma_{n}a_{n}\neq x
a.s.
∎

7.2
First moment of
h_{\theta}
with wired boundary conditions

We now move to the proof of (
1.7
) in Theorem
1.7
.

Proof of (
1.7
) in Theorem
1.7
.

Let
f
be a smooth test function compactly included in
\mathbb{D}
.
We will denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}
the multiplicative chaos associated to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
respectively. The definition of the latter has been recalled in Section
2.2
. The former is more complicated since it involves the interaction of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
. This has been extensively studied in
[
Jeg22
]
and
[
ABJL23
]
. We will only need to use the simple fact that

\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}z)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\notin R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}=\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}z)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\notin R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}\quad\quad\text{a.s.},

(7.1)

where we denoted by
R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})
the range of
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
, i.e. the set of points visited by some trajectory in
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
.
This property is simply saying that a point not visited by
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
is thick for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
if and only if it is thick for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\right]=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{D}}f(z)\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}z)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}-\mathrm{d}z\big|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\right]

where the convergence holds in
L^{2}
. This follows simply from Cauchy–Schwarz and Theorem
1.2
.
Let us consider first the case
\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1
. If
z
belongs to the range of
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
, its sign necessarily agrees with
\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}
. Together with (
7.1
), this gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}z)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}\big|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}z)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\notin R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}\big|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right].

By Lemma
2.3
, this is further equal to

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{P}\left(z\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,\mathbb{D}}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}),z\notin R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right)\mathrm{d}z.

Since a Lebesgue-typical point is a.s. not visited by
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
, the above expression further reduces to

\Big(1-\mathbb{P}\Big(z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\Big)\Big)\mathrm{d}z=\Big(1-\mathbb{P}\Big(0\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\Big)\Big)\mathrm{d}z.

The last equality is obtained by noticing that the probability is conformally invariant.
Altogether, we have obtained that

\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right]=-\Big(\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\Big(0\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\Big)\Big)\int_{\mathbb{D}}f(z)\mathrm{d}z.

This proves Theorem
1.7
in the case
\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1
with

c(\theta)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{P}\left(0\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\right)\big/Z_{\gamma}.

(7.2)

The convergence of the right hand side of (
7.2
) follows from the above line of arguments. It could also be obtained directly from Theorem
3.1
and from the estimate (
6.1
) in Proposition
6.4
on
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
.

To obtain the result when
\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=+1
, we use Theorem
1.3
, Point
3
. With the notations therein, and because
(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}})^{-}=-h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}}
a.s.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=1\right]=-\mathbb{E}\left[((h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}})^{-},f)|-\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right].

From the case of negative sign on
\partial\mathbb{D}
that we have just treated, we know that the expectation on the right hand side equals
-c(\theta)\int_{\mathbb{D}}f(z)\mathrm{d}z
a.s. This concludes the proof of the case
\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=+1
.
∎

7.3
Second moment of
h_{\theta}
with wired boundary conditions

The goal of this section is to prove (
1.8
) in Theorem
1.7
. We start with a preliminary result that relates the second moment of
h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}}
to connectivity probabilities (see Lemma
4.1
for the analogous result in a domain with zero-boundary condition).
As in Notation
4.4
and conditionally given
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
, we will denote by
C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}
the covariance of
h_{\theta}
built out of a loop soup in the domain
\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
with zero-boundary condition. Since the loops that are in different connected components of
\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
are independent of each other, if
x
and
y
belong to different connecting component of
\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
, then
C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(x,y)=0
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
f:\mathbb{D}\to[0,\infty)
be a smooth nonnegative function with compact support in
\mathbb{D}
. Then
\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)^{2}\right]
is equal to

\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(x,y)\right]+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\right)\right)f(x)f(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

(7.3)

Proof.

By symmetry,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f)^{2}|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right]=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}f(x)f(y)\times

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left(\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}-\mathrm{d}x\right)\left(\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{y\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}-\mathrm{d}y\right)\right|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right].

(7.4)

We expand the product in the expectation on the right hand side as a sum of four terms. In Section
7.2
we already dealt with the crossed terms:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}z)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}\right|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right]=(1-p_{\gamma})\mathrm{d}z\quad\text{where}\quad p_{\gamma}:=\mathbb{P}\left(z\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\right).

Let us recall that, by conformal invariance,
p_{\gamma}
does not depend on
z
.
We now focus on

\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x,y\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}\right|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right].

(7.5)

As in Section
7.2
, because
\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1
,
x
and
y
cannot belong to
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
allowing us to replace
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}
by
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}
. Moreover, by Lemma
2.3
, the contribution of soups
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
with a loop visiting both
x
and
y
vanishes as
\gamma\to 0
(this is the same computation as in (
4.9
)). (
7.5
) is therefore equal to

\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}x)\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}y)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x,y\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}),\nexists\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}:\{x,y\}\subset\wp\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x,y\notin R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\right\}}\right|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right]+o(Z_{\gamma}^{2})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Now, by Lemma
2.3
, this is further equal to

\displaystyle 4\mathbb{P}\left(x,y\in\mathfrak{C}^{+}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\cup\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}),x,y\notin R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}=-1\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+o(Z_{\gamma}^{2})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Since Lebesgue-typical points are almost surely not visited by
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
, the event
\{x,y\notin R(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta})\}
has full probability. Then density of the first measure above is then equal to

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{P}\left(\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}},\{\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\}^{c}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\{\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\}^{c},\{\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}},\{\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\}^{c}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\{\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}},\{\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\}^{c}\right)+1-2p_{\gamma}+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\right).

By the restriction properties of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
and
\Xi_{a}^{z,\mathbb{D}}
,
z=x,y
, one can show that

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}},\{\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}}\cup\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\}^{c}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(x,y)\right]

where the expectation is w.r.t.
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
.
The term
1-2p_{\gamma}
cancels out with the three other terms in the expansion of the product inside the expectation in (
7.4
).
Putting things together leads to (
7.3
).
∎

We now state another key intermediate result (Proposition
7.3
below) for the proof of (
1.8
) that we will prove in Section
7.4
below.
By Theorem
3.1
, we can define

u(z):=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{z,\mathbb{D}}\right|\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{D}.

(7.6)

By conformal invariance, the law of
u(z)
does not depend on
z
. The constant
c(\theta)
appearing in Theorem
1.7
corresponds exactly to the expectation of
u(z)
; see (
7.2
). Proposition
7.3
below shows that
u(x_{\varepsilon})
and
u(y_{\varepsilon})
become asymptotically uncorrelated when
(x_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
and
(y_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
are targeting two distinct fixed boundary points.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
x,y\in\partial\mathbb{D}
be two distinct boundary points.
Let
(x_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
and
(y_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be two sequences of points in
\mathbb{D}
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
,
|x-x_{\varepsilon}|<\varepsilon
and
|y-y_{\varepsilon}|<\varepsilon
.
Then

\max(\mathbb{E}\left[u(x_{\varepsilon})u(y_{\varepsilon})\right]-c(\theta)^{2},0)\to 0\quad\quad\text{as}\quad\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

Moreover, for all
\delta>0
fixed, the convergence is uniform in
x,y,(x_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon},(y_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon},
provided that
|x-y|\geq\delta
.

The proof of Proposition
7.3
is written in Section
7.4
. When
\theta=1/2
, one can use that
\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}
is a Poisson point process of Brownian excursions to prove Proposition
7.3
fairly painlessly. No such description is available for general intensities
\theta\leq 1/2
and we will rely on estimates and techniques developed in Section
6
.
We can now prove:

Proof of (
1.8
) in Theorem
1.7
.

Let
(f_{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon<1}
be a sequence of functions satisfying Assumption
1.6
.
By (
1.7
), for any
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f_{\varepsilon})|\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\right]=\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}c(\theta)
a.s. To show that
(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f_{\varepsilon})-\sigma_{\partial\mathbb{D}}c(\theta)\to 0
in
L^{2}
, it is therefore enough to show that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f_{\varepsilon})^{2}\right]\leq c(\theta)^{2}.

By Lemma
7.2
,
\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[(h_{\theta}^{\mathrm{wired}},f_{\varepsilon})^{2}\right]
is at most

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\right)f_{\varepsilon}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(7.7)

plus

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\mathbb{E}\left[C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(x,y)\right]f_{\varepsilon}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

(7.8)

Let us first show that (
7.8
) vanishes. To this end, notice that
C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(x,y)
vanishes as soon as
x
and
y
belong to different connected components of
\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
and notice also that
C_{\theta,D}(x,y)
is nondecreasing with the domain
D
(this follows from the expression (
4.3
)). We can thus bound

\mathbb{E}\left[C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}(x,y)\right]

by
C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}}(x,y)

times the probability
p_{xy}
that
x
and
y
belong to the same connected component of
\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
. Let
\delta>0
. If
|x-y|<\delta
, we simply bound
p_{xy}\leq 1
. Otherwise,
p_{xy}
vanishes uniformly in
x,y
with
\mathrm{d}(x,\partial\mathbb{D}),\mathrm{d}(y,\partial\mathbb{D})\to 0
and
|x-y|\geq\delta
. (
7.8
) is therefore at most

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{|x-y|<\delta\right\}}C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}}(x,y)f_{\varepsilon}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}o(1)\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{|x-y|\geq\delta\right\}}C_{\theta,\mathbb{D}}(x,y)f_{\varepsilon}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

where
o(1)
may depend on
\delta
and vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\delta>0
fixed. By (
1.5
), the second integral above is bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon
, so the second term vanishes. We can now let
\delta\to 0
and observe that the first term goes to 0 by (
1.6
). This shows that (
7.8
) vanishes.

The rest of the proof consists in showing that (
7.7
) is at most
c(\theta)^{2}
. Let
\varepsilon>0,\gamma>0,x,y\in\mathbb{D}
. Let us first show that

\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\right)\leq C\max(1,-\log|x-y|).

(7.9)

Let

R_{z}:=\sup\{R>0:\partial D(z,R)\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{z,\mathbb{D}}\},\quad\quad z\in\{x,y\}.

(7.10)

We have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\right)\leq\int_{(0,2)^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{z}\in\mathrm{d}r_{z},z=x,y\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cap D(z,r_{z}),z=x,y\right).

(7.11)

By Proposition
6.2
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cap D(z,r_{z}),z=x,y\right)\leq\frac{C\max(1,-\log|x-y|)}{\max(1,\log(\mathrm{d}(x,\partial\mathbb{D})/r_{x}))\max(1,\log(\mathrm{d}(y,\partial\mathbb{D})/r_{y}))}.

Plugging this in (
7.11
) and integrating by parts with respect to both
r_{x}
and
r_{y}
leads to the following upper bound for the left hand side of (
7.11
):

\displaystyle C\max(1,-\log|x-y|)\int_{0}^{e^{-1}\mathrm{d}(y,\partial\mathbb{D})}\mathrm{d}r_{y}\int_{0}^{e^{-1}\mathrm{d}(x,\partial\mathbb{D})}\mathrm{d}r_{x}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(R_{z}\geq r_{z},z=x,y\right)}{r_{x}r_{y}(\log\mathrm{d}(x,\partial\mathbb{D})/r_{x})^{2}(\log(\mathrm{d}(y,\partial\mathbb{D})/r_{y}))^{2}}.

The fact that we integrate over the intervals
(0,e^{-1}\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D}))
,
z=x,y
, stems from the fact that the derivative of
r_{z}\mapsto 1/\max(1,\log(\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D})/r_{z})
vanishes outside of this interval.
A slight variant of the argument of the proof of Lemma
4.2
shows that for
\eta
arbitrary small (for our purposes,
\eta=\theta/2
would do), there exists
C=C(\eta)>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(R_{z}\geq r_{z},z=x,y\right)\leq CZ_{\gamma}^{2}\prod_{z=x,y}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D})}{r_{z}}\right)^{1-\theta+\eta},\quad\quad r_{z}\in(0,e^{-1}\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D})),\quad z=x,y.

(7.12)

We omit these details. Putting things together and noticing that

\int_{0}^{e^{-1}\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D})}\frac{\mathrm{d}r_{z}}{r_{z}(\log\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D})/r_{z})^{1-\theta+\eta}}\leq C,\quad\quad\text{if}~\eta<\theta,\quad\quad z=x,y,

proves (
7.9
).

We now continue the proof that (
7.7
) is at most
c(\theta)^{2}
.
By (
7.9
) and (
1.6
), the contribution to (
7.8
) of points
x,y
with
|x-y|<\delta
vanishes as
\delta\to 0
. More precisely, (
7.8
) is at most

\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{|x-y|\geq\delta\right\}}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\right)f_{\varepsilon}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(y)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Let
\delta>0
be fixed. Recalling that
\int_{\mathbb{D}}f_{\varepsilon}=1
for all
\varepsilon
, in order to conclude that (
7.8
) is at most
c(\theta)^{2}
, it is enough to show that

\max\left(\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x,\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y,\mathbb{D}}\right)-c(\theta)^{2},0\right)

(7.13)

converges uniformly to
0
as
\mathrm{d}(x,\partial\mathbb{D}),\mathrm{d}(y,\partial\mathbb{D})\to 0
while
|x-y|
stays at least
\delta
. By extracting subsequences if necessary, we can moreover assume that
x
and
y
converge to distinct boundary points.
So, let
x_{0},y_{0}\in\partial\mathbb{D}
be two distinct boundary points and let
(x_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
and
(y_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be two sequences of points in
\mathbb{D}
that converge to
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
respectively.
We first notice that the loops in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
that will contribute to the probability in (
7.13
) are localised near
x_{\varepsilon}
and
y_{\varepsilon}
. More precisely, consider
A>0
large and denote by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},z}^{\theta}=\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}:\wp\subset D(z,A\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D}))\}
, for
z\in D
.
Then

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}}\right)=(1+o(1))\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},x_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},y_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}}\right)

(7.14)

where
o(1)\to 0
as
A\to\infty
, uniformly in
\gamma
and
\varepsilon>0
. This follows from the same type of considerations as in the proof of (
7.9
), and eventually boils down to the fact that the random variables
R_{z}
,
z\in\{x,y\}
, defined in (
7.10
), are of order
\mathrm{d}(z,\partial\mathbb{D})
. See in particular (
7.12
). But now, for
A>0
large but fixed, if
\varepsilon
is small enough the discs
D(x_{\varepsilon},A\mathrm{d}(x_{\varepsilon},\partial\mathbb{D}))
and
D(y_{\varepsilon},A\mathrm{d}(y_{\varepsilon},\partial\mathbb{D}))
are disjoint implying that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},x_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},y_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}
are independent. Hence the probability on the right hand side of (
7.14
) is equal to

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},x_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}}\right|\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},y_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}}\right|\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\right)\right].

As in (
7.14
) and by sending
A\to\infty
, we can replace
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},x_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D},y_{\varepsilon}}^{\theta}
by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}
(alternatively, adding more loops can only increase the probability of connection). This shows that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}},\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{x_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}}\right|\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}}{\longleftrightarrow}\Xi_{a}^{y_{\varepsilon},\mathbb{D}}\right|\mathcal{E}_{\partial\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\right)\right]=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[u(x_{\varepsilon})u(y_{\varepsilon})\right]

where
u(z),z\in\mathbb{D},
is defined in (
7.6
). In the last equality above we applied dominated convergence theorem (the domination is obtained from the estimate (
7.12
) on
R_{z}
and by Proposition
6.2
).
By Proposition
7.3
,
\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[u(x_{\varepsilon})u(y_{\varepsilon})\right]\leq c(\theta)^{2}
. This concludes the proof that (
7.8
) is at most
c(\theta)^{2}
which concludes the proof of (
1.8
).
∎

7.4
Quasi independence of excursions near two distinct points

This section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
7.3
.

Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

By conformal invariance, we may work in the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}
instead of the unit disc
\mathbb{D}
and assume that our boundary points are
0
and
1
.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4)
,
z_{1}\in\mathbb{H}\cap D(0,\varepsilon)
,
z_{2}\in\mathbb{H}\cap D(1,\varepsilon)
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{2}
) be the set of excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
with both endpoints in
[-\varepsilon^{2/3},\varepsilon^{2/3}]
(resp.
[1-\varepsilon^{2/3},1+\varepsilon^{2/3}]
).
For
j=1,2
, define

u_{j}:=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{j}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z_{j},\mathbb{H}}}{\longleftrightarrow}z_{j}\mid\mathcal{E}_{j}).

(7.15)

The difference with
u(z_{j})
(
7.6
) is that the intersection can only come from an excursion from
\mathcal{E}_{j}
, instead of the whole set
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
of excursions.
We are first going to show that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[u(z_{1})u(z_{2})\right]\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[u_{1}u_{2}\right].

(7.16)

Let
K_{\gamma,1}
and
K_{\gamma,2}
be two
independent
random compacts of
\mathbb{H}
distributed as (the topological closure of) the cluster of
z_{j}
in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{H}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{z_{j},\mathbb{H}}
,
j=1,2
.
We can bound for
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle u(z_{j})\leq u_{j}+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j}\cap K_{\gamma,j}\neq\varnothing\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}).

Multiplying these two inequalities for
j=1,2
leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[u(z_{1})u(z_{2})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[u_{1}u_{2}\right]\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1,2\\
j=3-i\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap K_{\gamma,i}\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{E}_{j}\cap K_{\gamma,j}\neq\varnothing\right|\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\forall j=1,2,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{j}\cap K_{\gamma,j}\neq\varnothing\right|\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1,2\\
j=3-i\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap K_{\gamma,i}\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap K_{\gamma,j}\neq\varnothing\right).

In the last inequality we exchanged expectation and limit with the help of Fatou’s lemma (or alternatively dominated convergence theorem).
For
j=1,2
, let
f_{j}:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{H}
be a conformal map that sends
0
to
z_{j}
and let

C_{j}(r):=f_{j}(r\partial\mathbb{D}),\quad r\in[0,1],\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\gamma,j}:=\sup\{r>0:C_{j}(r)\cap K_{\gamma,j}\neq\varnothing\}.

The definition of
C_{j}(r)
does not depend on the specific choice of
f_{j}
. Note also that, by conformal invariance,
R_{\gamma,1}
and
R_{\gamma,2}
have the same law as
R_{\gamma}
defined in (
2.19
).
Let
\eta>0
and let us first consider the event that
R_{\gamma,1}
and
R_{\gamma,2}
are at most
1-\eta
.
We have

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap K_{\gamma,1}\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap K_{\gamma,2}\neq\varnothing,R_{\gamma,1}\leq 1-\eta,R_{\gamma,2}\leq 1-\eta\right)

(7.17)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\int_{(0,1-\eta)^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma,1}\in\mathrm{d}r_{1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma,2}\in\mathrm{d}r_{2}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap C_{1}(r_{1})\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap C_{2}(r_{2})\neq\varnothing\right).

For
\eta>0
fixed, Lemma
6.22
shows that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap C_{1}(r_{1})\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap C_{2}(r_{2})\neq\varnothing\right)\leq o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)|\log r_{1}|^{-1}|\log r_{2}|^{-1}

where
o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1-\eta)
. Plugging this estimate into (
7.17
) and integrating by parts shows that the left hand side of (
7.17
) is at most

\frac{o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\int_{(0,1-\eta)^{2}}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(R_{1}\geq r_{1}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(R_{2}\geq r_{2}\right)}{r_{1}r_{2}|\log r_{1}\log r_{2}|^{2}}\mathrm{d}r_{1}\mathrm{d}r_{2}.

Now, by (
2.20
) in Lemma
2.11
(applied to
\eta=\theta/2
), there exists
C>0
such that for all
r_{j}\in(0,1-\eta)
,
\mathbb{P}\left(R_{j}\geq r_{j}\right)\leq CZ_{\gamma}|\log r_{j}|^{1-\theta/2}
. This shows that (
7.17
) vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
On the event that
R_{\gamma,1}
or
R_{\gamma,2}
is at least
1-\eta
, we have with a union bound,

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap K_{\gamma,1}\neq\varnothing,\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap K_{\gamma,2}\neq\varnothing,R_{\gamma,1}\text{~or~}R_{\gamma,2}\geq 1-\eta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1,2\\
j=3-i\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma,i}\geq 1-\eta\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap K_{\gamma,j}\neq\varnothing\right)=2c(\theta)\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}\left(R_{\gamma}\geq 1-\eta\right)

where
R_{\gamma}
is defined in (
2.19
). The last equality follows from conformal invariance and (
7.2
). In particular,
the right hand side is independent of
\varepsilon
and goes to zero as
\eta\to 0
((
2.21
) in Lemma
2.11
). This concludes the proof of (
7.16
).

The proof of Proposition
7.3
will then follow from (recall that
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are defined in (
7.15
)):

Lemma 7.4
.

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[u_{1}u_{2}\right]\leq c(\theta)^{2}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
.

Instead of considering directly a loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
which is wired everywhere, we will obtain
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{R}}
by exploring a loop soup which is partially wired, and then use the independence between different parts of the loop soup derived for this partial exploration process
[
Qia19
,
QW19
]
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{0}
be a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
wired on
[-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}]
and free elsewhere.
We will denote by
\mathcal{L}_{0}\cap\mathbb{H}
the subset of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
consisting of all the loops that are contained in
\mathbb{H}
.
By Theorems
6.1
and
6.2
, we can decompose
\mathcal{L}_{0}
into several independent parts:

1.

The excursions
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
attached to
[-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}]
.

2.

The CLE coming from the outer boundaries of the outermost clusters in
\mathcal{L}_{0}\cap\mathbb{H}
.

3.

For each loop
\ell
in the CLE in the previous item, let
O_{\ell}
be the domain encircled by
\ell
. Let
f_{\ell}
be a conformal map from
\mathbb{H}
onto
O_{\ell}
. For each
\ell
, we sample an independent loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}
with wired boundary conditions in
\mathbb{H}
, and put
f_{\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{\ell})
inside
O_{\ell}
.

We will denote by
\mathcal{F}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the items 1 and 2.

To obtain a loop soup
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
in
\mathbb{H}
wired on
\mathbb{R}
from
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, one can proceed as follows.
Let
\eta
be the outer boundary of the cluster in
\mathcal{L}_{0}
which is wired on
[-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}]
. Let
O
be the domain enclosed by
\eta
and
[-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}]
. Let
g
be the conformal map from
O
onto
\mathbb{H}
that sends
-\varepsilon^{2/3},\varepsilon^{2/3},\varepsilon^{1/2}
to
-\varepsilon^{2/3},\varepsilon^{2/3},\infty
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
be the image under
g
of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
restricted to
\overline{O}
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
is distributed as a loop soup in
\mathbb{H}
with wired boundary conditions. Let
\mathcal{E}
be the boundary excursions induced by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
and let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{2}
) be the set of excursions in
\mathcal{E}
with both endpoints in
[-\varepsilon^{2/3},\varepsilon^{2/3}]
(resp.
[1-\varepsilon^{2/3},1+\varepsilon^{2/3}]
).
Let
\widetilde{z}_{1}:=g^{-1}(z_{1}),\widetilde{z}_{2}:=g^{-1}(z_{2}).

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}:=g^{-1}(\mathcal{E})
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}:=g^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{1})
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{2}:=g^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{2})
. See Figure
7.1
for a schematic representation of some of these notations. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{j}:=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{O}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{\tilde{z}_{j},O}}{\longleftrightarrow}\widetilde{z}_{j}\mid\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{j},O),\quad\quad j=1,2.

By conformal invariance,
(u_{1},u_{2})
and
(\widetilde{u}_{1},\widetilde{u}_{2})
have the same law. Therefore, to prove Lemma
7.4
, it is equivalent to prove that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{u}_{1}\widetilde{u}_{2}]\leq c(\theta)^{2}.

Let
\xi
be the CLE loop that encircles
\widetilde{z}_{2}
. The wired loop soup
f_{\xi}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi})
(see item 3 above) inside the domain
O_{\xi}
delimited by
\xi
is composed of excursions
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\xi}
attached to
\xi
and loops
\mathcal{L}_{O_{\xi}}
contained in
O_{\xi}
. Define

v_{2}:=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\xi}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{O_{\xi}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{\tilde{z}_{2},O_{\xi}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\widetilde{z}_{2}\mid\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\xi},O_{\xi}).

(7.18)

By conformal invariance and the independence of
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}
and
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[v_{2}|\mathcal{F}\right]=c(\theta)
.
We are going to show that

\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{u}_{2}\leq v_{2})\to 1\quad\text{as}\quad\varepsilon\to 0.

(7.19)

Let us explain how we will conclude from this.
Notice that
\widetilde{u}_{1}
is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{F}
. Indeed,
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
, the domain
O
and
\widetilde{z}_{1}
are all measurable w.r.t. the excursions
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
and the CLE loops. Therefore, we have on the event
E:=\{\widetilde{u}_{2}\leq v_{2}\}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}\widetilde{u}_{2}\mathbf{1}_{E}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}v_{2}\mathbf{1}_{E}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}v_{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[v_{2}|\mathcal{F}\right]\right]=c(\theta)\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}\right]\leq c(\theta)^{2}

since, by adding more excursions,

\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}\right]\leq\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{O}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{\tilde{z}_{j},O}}{\longleftrightarrow}\widetilde{z}_{j}\mid\widetilde{\mathcal{E}},O)]=c(\theta).

On the complementary event
E^{c}
(whose probability vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
by (
7.19
)), we can bound for all
M>0
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}\widetilde{u}_{2}\mathbf{1}_{E^{c}}\right]\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}\widetilde{u}_{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{u}_{2}>M\right\}}\right]+M\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(E^{c})=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{u}_{1}\widetilde{u}_{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\tilde{u}_{1}\tilde{u}_{2}>M\right\}}\right].

We will show in Lemma
7.5
below that the quantity on the right hand side vanishes as
M\to\infty
. Altogether, this will prove Lemma
7.4
.

It remains to prove (
7.19
). We proceed in several steps.

Figure 7.1:
Proof of Lemma
7.4
. The excursions in
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
are in red. The set
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
is the union of the red and blue excursions.

\bullet~

Step 1.
The goal of this step is to show that
\mathbb{P}[g([-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}])\subseteq[-2\varepsilon^{1/2},2\varepsilon^{1/2}]]=1+o(1)
.
Let
\varepsilon^{-1/2}O
be the domain
O
rescaled by
\varepsilon^{-1/2}
.
Let
f
be the conformal map from
\varepsilon^{-1/2}O
onto
\mathbb{H}
that sends
-1/2,1/2,1
to
-1,1,\infty
.
Then
g(z)=h\circ f(\varepsilon^{-1/2}z)
, where
h
is a composition of scaling and translation that sends
f(\pm\varepsilon^{1/6})
to
\pm\varepsilon^{2/3}
.
Note that
f
is analytic, so for all
z\in D(0,\varepsilon^{7/12})\cap\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(z)=\varepsilon^{-1/2}f^{\prime}(\varepsilon^{-1/2}z)\frac{2\varepsilon^{2/3}}{f(\varepsilon^{1/6})-f(-\varepsilon^{1/6})}=(f^{\prime}(0)+O(\varepsilon^{1/12}))\frac{2\varepsilon^{1/6}}{f^{\prime}(0)2\varepsilon^{1/6}+O(\varepsilon^{1/3})}=1+O(\varepsilon^{1/12}).

Consequently, there exists
x_{0}\in[\varepsilon^{2/3},\varepsilon^{1/2}]
such that

\displaystyle g(\varepsilon^{1/2})=g(\varepsilon^{2/3})+(\varepsilon^{1/2}-\varepsilon^{2/3})g^{\prime}(x_{0})=\varepsilon^{1/2}+O(1)\varepsilon^{7/12},

where
O(1)
is bounded by some random variable
A_{0}
whose law only depends on the law of
f
, which does not depend on
\varepsilon
.
Let
F_{1}
be the event that
A_{0}\leq\varepsilon^{-1/24}
, so that
\mathbb{P}(F_{1})=1+o(1)
. On
F_{1}
, we have
g(\varepsilon^{1/2})\leq 2\varepsilon^{1/2}
. Similarly, we can define
F_{2}
with
\mathbb{P}(F_{2})=1+o(1)
so that
g(-\varepsilon^{1/2})\geq-2\varepsilon^{1/2}
.
On the event
F_{1}\cap F_{2}
, we have
g([-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}])\subseteq[-2\varepsilon^{1/2},2\varepsilon^{1/2}]
. This concludes Step 1.

\bullet~

Step 2.
Recall that
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
denotes the set of excursions in
\mathcal{E}_{0}
with both endpoints in
[-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}]
.
The goal of this step is to prove that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[g(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\cap D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})=\emptyset]=1+o(1).

(7.20)

Let
\mathcal{E}_{4}
be the set of excursions in
\mathcal{E}
that have both endpoints in
[-2\varepsilon^{1/2},2\varepsilon^{1/2}]
. Then
g(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\subset\mathcal{E}_{4}
on the event that
g([-\varepsilon^{1/2},\varepsilon^{1/2}])\subseteq[-2\varepsilon^{1/2},2\varepsilon^{1/2}]
. Thanks to Step 1, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[g(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\cap D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})\neq\emptyset]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{4}\cap D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})\neq\emptyset]+o(1)

By Lemma
6.8
, we can further bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{4}\cap D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})\neq\emptyset]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E};a(e),b(e)\in[-2\varepsilon^{1/2},2\varepsilon^{1/2}]}\frac{(b(e)-a(e))^{2}}{(1-a(e))^{2}(1-b(e))^{2}}\right]\varepsilon^{7/6}+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E};a(e),b(e)\in[-2\varepsilon^{1/2},2\varepsilon^{1/2}]}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\right]\varepsilon^{7/6}+o(1).

With the help of Lemma
6.20
, we can bound the last expectation above by
C\varepsilon^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1/2}
proving (
7.20
).

\bullet~

Step 3.
In this step we show that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}\subset D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})]=1+o(1).

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}\not\subset D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})]\leq C_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{2}}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\varepsilon^{-7/6}\right]\leq C_{2}\varepsilon^{4/3}\varepsilon^{-7/6}=C\varepsilon^{1/6},

where the term
C_{1}(b(e)-a(e))^{2}\varepsilon^{-7/6}
represents the probability that an excursion with endpoints
a(e)
and
b(e)
has diameter at least
\varepsilon^{7/12}/2
, and the last inequality follows from Lemma
6.20
.

\bullet~

Step 4. Conclusion.

On the event
F:=\{g(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\cap D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})=\emptyset\}\cap\{\mathcal{E}_{2}\subset D(1,\varepsilon^{7/12})\}
,
g(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}=\varnothing
and the loops in
\mathcal{L}_{O}
that can contribute to
\widetilde{u}_{2}
can only come from
\mathcal{L}_{O_{\xi}}
(
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
cannot contribute). Hence, on
F
,

\widetilde{u}_{2}=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{O_{\xi}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{\tilde{z}_{2},O_{\xi}}}{\longleftrightarrow}\widetilde{z}_{2}\mid\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{2},O_{\xi}).

Now, because on the event
F
, the excursions in
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
are contained in
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\xi}
, changing
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
into
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{\xi}
can only increase the above probability, i.e.
\widetilde{u}_{2}\leq v_{2}
. Since
\mathbb{P}[F]=1+o(1)
by Steps 2 and 3, this concludes the proof of (
7.19
) and the proof of Lemma
7.4
.
∎

To finish the proof of Proposition
7.3
, we need to deal with the uniform integrability of
u_{1}u_{2}
:

Lemma 7.5

(Uniform integrability of
u_{1}u_{2}
)
.

For all
p\in(0,1/(1-\theta))
,
\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[u_{1}^{p}u_{2}^{p}\right]<\infty
. In particular,

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[u_{1}u_{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{u_{1}u_{2}>M\right\}}\right]=0.

(7.21)

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

Let
p\in(0,1/(1-\theta))
and let
\eta>0
be small enough so that
p(1-\theta+\eta)<1
.
For
j=1,2
, let
f_{j}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
be a conformal map that sends
z_{j}
to 0 and let
R_{j}:=\mathrm{d}(0,f_{j}(\mathcal{E}_{j}))
. By conformal invariance and Lemma
2.11
,

u_{j}=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}(f_{j}(\mathcal{E}_{j})\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}0\mid\mathcal{E}_{j})\leq\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{1}{Z_{\gamma}}\mathbb{P}(R_{j}\partial\mathbb{D}\overset{\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{D}}^{\theta}\cup\Xi_{a}^{0,\mathbb{D}}}{\longleftrightarrow}0\mid R_{j})\leq C(1+|\log R_{j}|)^{1-\theta+\eta}.

Using that for nonnegative random variables
X
and
Y
,
\mathbb{E}\left[XY\right]=\int_{(0,\infty)^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(X\geq x,Y\geq y\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y
, we deduce that
\mathbb{E}\left[u_{1}^{p}u_{2}^{p}\right]
is at most

\displaystyle C\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{j=1,2}(1+|\log R_{j}|)^{p(1-\theta+\eta)}\Big]\leq C\int_{(0,\infty)^{2}}\mathbb{P}\left((1+|\log R_{j}|)^{p(1-\theta+\eta)}\geq t_{j},j=1,2\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}.

By (
6.2
) in Proposition
6.4
, the probability in the integral is equal to

\mathbb{P}\left(\forall j=1,2,R_{j}\leq\exp\left(1-t_{j}^{\frac{1}{p(1-\theta+\eta)}}\right)\right)\leq C((t_{1}+1)(t_{2}+1))^{-\frac{1}{p(1-\theta+\eta)}}.

Since
p(1-\theta+\eta)<1
, this implies that the integral converges which concludes the proof of the bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[u_{1}^{p}u_{2}^{p}\right]
. (
7.21
) then follows by Hölder’s inequality and the fact that the interval
(0,1/(1-\theta))
contains real numbers that are strictly larger than 1.
∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
7.3
.
∎

8
Approximation of
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+}
from the discrete

The purpose of this section is to show that the field
h_{\theta}
agrees with a multiple of the GFF when
\theta=1/2
. See Theorem
1.4
. To do so, we will show in Theorem
8.3
a stronger version of the convergence of the discrete approximation
\mathcal{M}_{a,N}
(
2.10
) to
\mathcal{M}_{a}
: we will show that this convergence even holds when one restricts the measures to individual clusters. This result of independent interest holds for any subcritical intensity
\theta\in(0,1/2]
.

Notation 8.1
.

For all
x\in D
and
k\geq 1
, we will denote by
\mathcal{C}(x,k)
the
k
-th outermost cluster surrounding
x
. (
k=1
corresponds to the outermost cluster)

Recall that we denote by
\mathfrak{C}
the collection of all clusters
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
be a random walk loop soup approximating
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
as in Section
2.2
.
We will use analogous notations
\mathfrak{C}_{N}
,
\mathcal{C}_{N}(x,k)
, etc. in the discrete. In this section we will view clusters (or rather their closures) as elements of
(\mathcal{K},\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{K}})
(
2.3
), i.e. as random compact subsets of
\overline{D}
. We recall that discrete clusters converge to continuous clusters:

Theorem 8.2

(
[
Lup18a
]
, Section 4.3 in
[
ALS20b
]
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
x_{i}\in D,k_{i}\geq 1
,
i=1,\dots,n
. The following convergence

(\{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}(\{\overline{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta})

holds in distribution relatively to the topologies induced by
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mathrm{d}_{\mathfrak{L}}
respectively.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{a,N}
be the discrete approximation (
2.10
) of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
. The main result of this section is:

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
\theta\leq 1/2
and
a\in(0,2)
.
For all
n\geq 1
,
x_{i}\in D,k_{i}\geq 1
,
i=1\dots n
, the following convergence

\left(\{\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}\right)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\left(\{\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\{\overline{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right)

holds in distribution relatively to the weak topologies of measures and the topologies induced by
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mathrm{d}_{\mathfrak{L}}
.

As in the continuum, let
\mathcal{M}_{a,N}^{+}
be the measure
\mathcal{M}_{a,N}
restricted to positive clusters, where we assign independent spins to each cluster. As a consequence of Theorem
8.3
, we will get:

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1/2]
and
a\in(0,2)
. The following convergence

(\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta},\mathcal{M}_{a,N}^{+})\to(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{+})\quad\quad\text{as}\quad N\to\infty,

holds in distribution relatively to the topology induced by
d_{\mathfrak{L}}
for
\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}
and the weak topology on
\mathbb{C}
for
\mathcal{M}_{a,N}^{+}
.

The exact same result would still hold if we were considering the loop soup on the cable graph where the connection with the Gaussian free field is the strongest
[
Lup16
]
. This allows us to prove Theorem
1.4
:

Proof of Theorem
1.4
, assuming Corollary
8.4
.

Once Corollary
8.4
is established, the proof of Theorem
1.4
follows along the same lines as the proof of
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.5]
. The main difference is that we need to use Lupu’s version
[
Lup16
]
of Le Jan’s isomorphism which recovers the signs of the discrete GFF: on the cable graph,
(\sigma_{x}\sqrt{2\ell_{x}})_{x}
has the same law as the cable graph GFF
[
Lup16
]
. In particular, this identifies
\mathcal{M}_{a,N}^{+}
with the uniform measure on the set of
a
-thick points of the cable graph GFF which is known to converge to the exponential of the continous GFF
[
BL19
]
. Together with Corollary
8.4
(or rather, the cable graph analogue), this allows us to prove Theorem
1.4
. We refer to the proof of
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.5]
for more details.
∎

The rest of Section
8
is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
8.3
and Corollary
8.4
. We will first state and prove some preliminary lemmas before returning to the proof of the main results of this section.

8.1
Preliminary lemmas

Let us denote by

\mathfrak{Q}_{p}:=\{x+[-2^{-p},2^{-p}]^{2},x\in 2^{-p}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\},\quad\quad p\geq 1,

(8.1)

a collection of dyadic squares of side length
2^{-p+1}
. For all
x\in D
and
k\geq 1
, we enlarge the cluster
\mathcal{C}(x,k)
with the help of these dyadic squares and we denote by

\mathcal{C}^{(p)}(x,k):=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathfrak{Q}_{p},Q\cap\mathcal{C}(x,p)\neq\varnothing}Q.

(8.2)

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
x\in D
,
k\geq 1
. Let
O_{i},i\geq 0
, be the connected components of
D\setminus\mathcal{C}(x,k)
with
O_{0}
being the unique component whose boundary include
\partial D
. Conditionally on
\mathcal{C}(x,k)
, we have:

•

The collections of loops
\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta},\wp\subset O_{i}\},i\geq 0
, are independent;

•

For all
i\geq 1
,
\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta},\wp\subset O_{i}\}
has the law of a Brownian loop soup in
O_{i}
with intensity
\theta
;

•

\{\wp\in\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta},\wp\subset O_{0}\}
has the law of a Brownian loop soup in
O_{0}
with intensity
\theta
conditionally to have exactly
k-1
clusters surrounding the inner boundary of
O_{0}
.

Proof.

This lemma can be proven by working on the event that the enlargement
\mathcal{C}^{(p)}(x,k)
of
\mathcal{C}(x,k)
is equal to a specific union
C_{p}
of dyadic squares. One then uses that the loops that do not intersect
C_{p}
are independent from
\mathcal{C}(x,k)
(except that
\mathcal{C}(x,k)
is exactly the
k
-th outermost cluster surrounding
x
) and then let
p\to\infty
. We omit the details.
∎

Next, we show that the
\mathcal{M}_{a}
-measure of one cluster is well approximated by the
\mathcal{M}_{a}
-measure of its
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
n,p\geq 1
, and for
i=1\dots n
, let
x_{i}\in D
,
k_{i}\geq 1
and let
C_{i,p}
be a subset of
D
which is a union of dyadic squares in
\mathfrak{Q}_{p}
. Assume that the sets
C_{i,p},i=1\dots n
, are pairwise disjoint and let
E_{p}
be the event that for all
i=1\dots n
,
\mathcal{C}^{(p)}(x_{i},k_{i})=C_{i,p}
. Then

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}_{a}(C_{i,p}\setminus\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i}))\Big|\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i}),i=1\dots n\Big]\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}\leq C2^{-pa}\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}

(8.3)

for some deterministic constant
C>0
which only depends on the domain
D
.

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.

To ease notations, we will prove this lemma for the
n
outermost clusters surrounding a given point
x\in D
. With the notations of the lemma, this corresponds to the particular case
x_{i}=x
and
k_{i}=i
for
i=1\dots n
. The general case follows from the same proof.

For
j=1\dots n
, let
O_{j}
be the connected component of
D\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}(x,i)
whose boundary intersects both
\mathcal{C}(x,j-1)
and
\mathcal{C}(x,j)
, where by convention we write
\mathcal{C}(x,0)=\partial D
. Let
O_{j},j\geq n+1
, denote the other connected components of
D\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}(x,i)
.
O_{1},\dots,O_{n}
are annular-like, whereas
O_{n+1},O_{n+2},\dots
are disc-like. Conditionally on
\mathcal{C}(x,i),i=1\dots n
, the law of the loops in each of these connected components is described in Lemma
8.5
.
For all
j\geq 1
, let
O_{j}^{p}
denote the set of points of
O_{j}
which are at distance at most
\sqrt{2}\cdot 2^{-p+1}
to the boundary of
O_{j}
. On the event
E_{p}
,

\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}C_{i,p}\setminus\mathcal{C}(x,i)\subset\bigcup_{j\geq 1}O_{j}^{p},

which implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}_{a}(C_{i,p}\setminus\mathcal{C}(x,i))\Big|\mathcal{C}(x,i),i=1\dots n\Big]\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}\leq\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}(O_{j}^{p})\Big|\mathcal{C}(x,i),i=1\dots n\right]\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}.

For all
j\geq n+1
, conditionally on
\mathcal{C}(x,i),i=1\dots n
, the law of the loops inside
O_{j}
is that of a Brownian loop soup in
O_{j}
. Therefore, by
[
ABJL23
, Theorem 1.1]
, we have for all
j\geq n+1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}(O_{j}^{p})\Big|\mathcal{C}(x,i),i=1\dots n\right]\leq C\int_{O_{j}^{p}}\CR(z,O_{j})^{a}\mathrm{d}z\leq C2^{-pa}|O_{j}|

because if a point
z
is a distant at most
\sqrt{2}\cdot 2^{-p+1}
to the boundary of
O_{j}
, then
\CR(z,O_{j})
is at most
4\sqrt{2}\cdot 2^{-p+1}
(Koebe quarter theorem).
For
j=1\dots n
, the conditional law of the loops in
O_{j}
is that of a loop soup in
O_{j}
conditioned to not have any cluster surrounding the inner boundary of
O_{j}
. By FKG inequality, such a loop soup is stochastically dominated by an unconditioned loop soup in the same domain. So the same reasoning applies, except that the domain
O_{j}
is not simply connected so one has to replace
\log\CR(z,O_{j})
by the harmonic extension of
\log|z-\cdot|
from
\partial O_{j}
to
O_{j}
(see Remark
2.2
). Nevertheless, the same conclusion holds: for all
j=1\dots n
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}(O_{j}^{p})\Big|\mathcal{C}(x,i),i=1\dots n\right]\leq C2^{-pa}|O_{j}|.

Summing over
j
leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}_{a}(C_{i,p}\setminus\mathcal{C}(x,i))\Big|\mathcal{C}(x,i),i=1\dots n\Big]\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}\leq C2^{-p\gamma^{2}/2}\sum_{j\geq 1}|O_{j}|\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}\leq C|D|2^{-pa}\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
8.6
.
∎

We will also need to ensure that most of the
\mathcal{M}_{a}
-mass comes from the “large” clusters:

\mathfrak{C}_{q}:=\{\mathcal{C}(x,k),x\in 2^{-q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cap D,k\leq 2^{q}\},\quad\mathfrak{C}_{N,q}:=\{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x,k),x\in 2^{-q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cap D,k\leq 2^{q}\},\quad q\geq 1.

Lemma 8.7
.

As
q\to\infty
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{a}(D\setminus\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{q}}\mathcal{C})\Big]\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D\setminus\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N,q}}\mathcal{C})\Big]\to 0.

Proof of Lemma
8.7
.

We prove the claim in the discrete setting. The continuous case is similar.
The proof of this lemma is very close in spirit to the the proof of Lemma
8.6
. Let
q\geq 1
and let
p\leq q
be much small than
q
. For a cluster
\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N}
, we will denote by
\mathcal{C}^{(p)}
its
2^{-p}
-enlargement with dyadic squares as in (
8.2
).
Let
E_{N,q,p}
be the event that
\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N,q}}\mathcal{C}^{(p)}
covers the whole domain
D
. Since the clusters
\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N}
are dense in
D
, for any fixed
p
,

\lim_{q\to\infty}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(E_{N,q,p}\right)\to 1.

(8.4)

On this event, we can write

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\Big(D\setminus\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N,q}}\mathcal{C})\mathbf{1}_{E_{N,q,p}}\Big]\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\Big(\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N,q}}\Big(\mathcal{C}^{(p)}\setminus\mathcal{C}\Big)\Big)\Big]

and we can use the exact same strategy as in the proof of Lemma
8.6
to show that the right hand side term is at most
C2^{-pa}
for some constant
C>0
depending only on the domain
D
. More precisely, we use a discrete version of Lemma
8.5
together with the expression given in
[
ABJL23
, Propostion 10.1]
for the first moment of
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}
that converges to the continuum expression.

On the complementary event, we simply bound

\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D\setminus\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N,q}}\mathcal{C})\mathbf{1}_{E_{N,q,p}^{c}}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D)\mathbf{1}_{E_{N,q,p}^{c}}\right].

The uniform integrability of
(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D))_{N\geq 1}
(see
[
ABJL23
]
), together with (
8.4
), ensures that the right hand side term goes to zero as
N\to\infty
and then
q\to\infty
. Let us give some details. Let
M>0
be large. We can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D)\mathbf{1}_{E_{N,q,p}^{c}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D)\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D)>M}\right]+M\mathbb{P}\left(E_{N,q,p}^{c}\right).

By (
8.4
), we deduce that

\limsup_{q\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D)\mathbf{1}_{E_{N,q,p}^{c}}\right]\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D)\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D)>M}\right].

By the uniform integrability of
(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D))_{N\geq 1}
, the right hand side term goes to zero as
M\to\infty
. The left hand side term being independent of
M
, it has to vanish as well.
Wrapping things up, we have

\limsup_{q\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}(D\setminus\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N,q}}\mathcal{C})\Big]\leq C2^{-pa}.

We obtain the desired result by letting
p\to\infty
.
∎

8.2
Proof of Theorem
8.3

Proof of Theorem
8.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in D
and
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\geq 1
.
First of all, we claim that the convergence in distribution

\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N},\{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}\right)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\left(\mathcal{M}_{a},\{\overline{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right)

(8.5)

holds. Indeed it was proven in
[
ABJL23
]
that

\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N},\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}\right)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\left(\mathcal{M}_{a},\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right)

and in
[
Lup18b
]
(see Theorem
8.2
) that

\left(\{\overline{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}\right)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\left(\{\overline{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right).

The convergence (
8.5
) then follows from the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{a}
and
\{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
are both measurable with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}
.
Now, we want to establish the joint convergence of

\left(\{\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})}\}_{i=1}^{n},\{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}\right),\quad N\geq 1.

(8.6)

The convergence (
8.5
) together with the trivial bound
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})}\leq 1
shows that this sequence is tight. To conclude the proof, it is enough to identify the law of the subsequential limits. We will do this in three steps.

Step 1: stochastic domination of the subsequential limits.

Let
\left(\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n},\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{L}\right)
be any subsequential limit of (
8.6
). In the following and to ease the notations, we will omit the subsequence and we will assume that the convergence holds as
N\to\infty
.
For
i=1\dots n
, let
f_{i}:D\to[0,\infty)
be bounded nonnegative continuous functions, let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
be a nondecreasing, bounded, 1-Lipschitz function and let
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
be two nonnegative continuous bounded functions. The goal of this first step is to establish the following stochastic domination:

\varphi\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{i},f_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\preceq\varphi\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})},f_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right).

(8.7)

Let
p\geq 1
be large and recall the definition (
8.1
) of
\mathfrak{Q}_{p}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{N}^{(p)}(x_{i},k_{i})
be the discrete analogue of the enlarged version
\mathcal{C}^{(p)}(x_{i},k_{i})
of
\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})
(
8.2
).
For
i=1,\dots,n
, let
C_{i,p}
be any subset of
D
which can be written as a union of dyadic squares
Q\in\mathfrak{Q}_{p}
and let
E_{N,p}
(resp.
E_{p}
) be the event that for all
i=1\dots n
,
\mathcal{C}_{N}^{(p)}(x_{i},k_{i})=C_{i,p}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}^{(p)}(x_{i},k_{i})=C_{i,p}
). Because we are looking at “large” clusters, we will be able to restrict ourselves to the event that these clusters are well separated. For now, this means that we can assume that the sets
C_{i,p},i=1\dots n
are pairwise disjoint. As a consequence of this assumption, we will later have to deal with the probability that the clusters
\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})
,
i=1\dots n
, are at distance at least
10.2^{-p}
to each other, but this probability tends to 1 as
p\to\infty
.
Because the
f_{i}
’s are nonnegative, on the event
E_{N,p}
, we can bound

\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})},f_{i}\right)\leq\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{C_{i,p}},f_{i}\right),\quad i=1\dots n.

By the joint convergence (
8.5
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{E_{N,p}}\varphi\left((\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})},f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}_{N}(x_{i},k_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{D_{N}}^{\theta}\right)\right]

(8.8)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}\varphi\left((\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{C_{i,p}},f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right)\right].

On the event
E_{p}
, we can bound

(\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{C_{i,p}},f_{i})\leq(\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})},f_{i})+\left\|f_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\mathcal{M}_{a}(C_{i,p}\setminus\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})).

Recalling that
\varphi
is 1-Lipschitz, this leads to the following upper bound for the right hand side of (
8.8
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}\varphi\left((\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})},f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\left\|\psi_{1}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\psi_{2}\right\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|f_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{E_{p}}\mathcal{M}_{a}(C_{i,p}\setminus\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i}))\right].

By Lemma
8.6
, the second term is at most
C2^{-pa}\mathbb{P}\left(E_{p}\right)
.
Summing over all pairwise disjoint
C_{i,p},i=1\dots n
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi\left((\mathcal{M}_{i},f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi\left((\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})},f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~+C2^{-pa}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\psi_{1}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\psi_{2}\right\|_{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{i\neq j}\mathrm{d}(\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i}),\mathcal{C}(x_{j},k_{j}))\leq 10.2^{-p}\right).

After letting
p\to\infty
, this gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi\left((\mathcal{M}_{i},f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}\right)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi\left((\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})},f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{1}\left(\left(\mathcal{C}(x_{i},k_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}\right)\psi_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}_{D}^{\theta}\right)\right].

Since this is true for any suitable
\varphi,\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
, this shows the desired stochastic domination (
8.7
).

Step 2: the expectations agree.

We specify the result of the first step to a single test function
f:D\to[0,\infty)
.
Recall the notation of Lemma
8.7
. As a consequence of Step 1, any subsequential limit

\left((\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}},f)_{\mathcal{C}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{q_{2}}\setminus\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{q_{1}}},0\leq q_{1}<q_{2}\right)\quad\text{of}\quad\left((\mathcal{M}_{a}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{N}},f)_{\mathcal{C}_{N}\in\mathfrak{C}_{N,q_{2}}\setminus\mathfrak{C}_{N,q_{1}}},0\leq q_{1}<q_{2}\right)_{N\geq 1}

is stochastically dominated by its continuum counterpart (in the sense that each finite dimensional marginals are stochastically dominated). In particular, for all
q_{2}>q_{1}\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{q_{2}}\setminus\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{q_{1}}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}},f\right)\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}_{q_{2}}\setminus\mathfrak{C}_{q_{1}}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}},f\right)\Big].

(8.9)

Assume that this inequality is strict for some
q_{2}>q_{1}\geq 0
and let
\varepsilon>0
be the difference of the right hand side and the left hand side. Applying (
8.9
) between 0 and
